明天她或许再去找他们一趟。就算不顺利，如今他们也翻不起大浪了。
萨米差不多认输了。眼看马修就要奏着凯歌拿到提名。实际上，他刚得知马修走了一步出人意料的棋，是蠢是高，萨米还拿不定。卡拉瑟斯告诉他，就在今天下午，左翼劳工的人找过马修，抱怨说自萨拉初次登门之后，谈判就再没带过他们，今晚的会谈也没请他们的代表。马修同他们关起门谈了两个钟头，谈了什么、做了什么，萨米无从打听。他那年轻黑人喽啰显然不在场，印度人也寻不见。这样一来，他指望激进派在最后关头破门而入、大闹会场的一线希望，看来落了空。也许马修变了个什么戏法，换得他们默许。劳工代表团八成根本不会来。
眼下，会谈总得办下去。既然成功在望，他不能不上车。他慢慢把他的有色政客们聚到餐厅去，那里有好酒。他把白人太太们同有色太太们请到围廊上，热络地谈南区的社会服务工作。年轻些的男男女女——连共和党巨头同他的朋友也在内——他拢到大客厅里，起了一场活泼的谈锋。这一切，都赶在萨拉细心而不露痕迹地安排马修个别会谈的当口办妥。唉，大势已去。
忽然他瞧见卡拉瑟斯从通往门厅那半挑起的门帘后头，鬼鬼祟祟朝他打手势。他四下一望：有色客人正三三两两同白人交谈，马修也从小书房里出来了，正同牧师谈得投机。萨米溜了出去。
“喂，”卡拉瑟斯说，“劳工代表团到了，要进来。”
萨米竖起了耳朵。
啊哈！看来到底有戏可看了。他走到萨拉跟前，透了个信。
“反正不能进这儿。”萨拉说。
“可是，”萨米争辩，“人家分明是受了邀请的。”
“反正不是我请的。”萨拉厉声说。
“可我疑心是马修请的。他今天下午还同他们在一块儿。

===Original===

He was with them this afternoon.”
Sara started and tapped her foot impatiently. But Sammy went on:
“Don’t you think it would be good politics to let them have their say? We don’t need to yield to them in any way.”
Sara was unwilling, but she saw the point. It was a shame to have this love feast broken into. Then a plan occurred to her. They need not come in here; they could meet Matthew in the little library. The door to the reception room could be closed, and they could enter from the hall. Meantime, Sammy saw Corruthers again beckoning excitedly from the door. He walked over quickly, and Corruthers whispered to him.
“My God!” said Sammy. “Hush, Corruthers, and don’t say another word. Here, come and have a drink!”
Then he hurried back to Sara. Sara interrupted him before he could speak.
“Take them into the library. I will have Matthew receive them.” She sauntered over to Matthew. “Matthew, dear, some of the Bolsheviks are here and want to talk to you. I have had them taken from the hall directly to the library. You can close the door. They will probably feel more at ease then.”
Matthew rose and said a little impatiently: “Why not have them in here?”
“They preferred the smaller room,” said Sara. “They are not exactly﻿—dressed for an evening function.”
And then, turning, she ordered the portières which concealed the dining-room to be thrown open, and as Matthew stepped into the small library, the blaze of Sara’s supper fell upon the company in the reception room.
The table was a goodly sight. The waiters were deft and silent. The music rose sweetly. The company was hungry, for it was nearly nine. Even Mrs. Beech, who had meant to dine in Hubbard Woods, changed her mind. Little tables with lace, linen, China, and silver were set about, and soon a regular dinner of excellent quality was being served. Tongues loosened, laughter rose, and a feeling of good fellowship began to radiate. Mr. Cadwalader and Mrs. Beech agreed sotto voce that really this was quite average in breeding and as a spectacle; they glowed at the rainbow of skins﻿—it was positively exciting.
Sammy was almost hilarious. He could not restrain a wink at Corruthers, and both of them simultaneously bolted for the hall in order to laugh freely and get some more of that other punch. Meantime, Sara’s unease increased.

===Chinese===

萨拉一惊，不耐烦地跺脚。萨米只管往下说：
“放他们进来说道说道，岂不是步好棋？咱们什么也不用让。”
萨拉不情愿，但听出了道理。好好一场爱的宴会，叫人破门而入，实在可惜。随即她心生一计：不必进这儿，可以让他们到小书房去见马修。客厅的门一关，他们从门厅进出就是。正想着，萨米看见卡拉瑟斯又在门口急切地招手。他快步过去，卡拉瑟斯对他耳语。
“我的老天！”萨米说，“嘘，卡拉瑟斯，一个字也别说。来，喝一杯去！”
他急忙回到萨拉身边。萨拉没等他开口就打断了：
“领他们进书房。我叫马修见他们。”她踱到马修跟前。“马修，亲爱的，几位布尔什维克来了，想同你谈谈。我已让人把他们从门厅直接领进书房了。你可以把门关上，那样他们大概更自在些。”
马修起身，略带不耐烦地说：“干吗不请他们到这儿来？”
“他们自己愿意在小间里，”萨拉说，“他们的穿着，到底——不便赴晚间盛会。”
说完她转身吩咐，把遮着餐厅的门帘全部拉开。马修跨进小书房的那一刻，萨拉晚宴的辉煌，向客厅里的宾客轰然展开。
餐桌好一派气象。侍者们轻捷无声。乐声悠然扬起。宾客们全饿了，已近九点。连原本打算回哈伯德林地去吃饭的比奇太太，也改变了主意。一张张小桌，铺着花边、亚麻布，摆着瓷器和银器，不多时，一顿像样的、质量上乘的正餐就开了上来。舌头松了，笑声起了，一团融融的好意开始荡漾。卡德瓦拉德先生同比奇太太压低嗓音连连称道：论教养、论场面，这实在够得上寻常水准；他们望着那一道彩虹般的肤色，眼里放光——真叫人兴奋。
萨米几乎乐不可支。他忍不住朝卡拉瑟斯挤挤眼，两人不约而同冲进门厅，好痛痛快快笑一场，顺便再灌一点“另一种”潘趣酒。与此同时，萨拉的不安在上涨。

Her place and Matthew’s had been arranged at the edge of the dining-room at a table with Mrs. Beech and Mr. Graham. The banker, the state official, and the two pretty young politicians’ wives were at a table next, and the other tables were arranged as far as possible with at least one bit of color.
But where was Matthew? thought Sara impatiently. It was time for the toast and the great announcement﻿—the culmination of the feast and conference. Mrs. Beech asked for Mr. Towns.
“He’s having a last word with the Communists,” laughed Sara.
“Oh, are they here?” asked Mr. Cadwalader uneasily﻿—“at the last moment?”
“They wouldn’t come in﻿—they are asking about some minor matters of adjustment, I presume.”
But Sara knew she must interfere. She distrusted Matthew’s mushy indecision. To reopen the argument now might spoil all. She could stand it no longer. She arose easily, a delicate coffee cup in hand, and said a laughing word. She moved to the library door. Sammy watched her. The others sensed in different ways some slight uneasiness in the air.
“Well, Mr. Towns,” said Sara, pushing the door wide, “we﻿—”
The light of the greater room poured into the lesser﻿—searching out its shadows. The ugly Chinese god grinned in the corner, and a blue rug glowed on the floor. In the center two figures, twined as one, in close and quivering embrace, leapt, etched in startling outline, on the light.
XXIII
Matthew had turned and started for the library. He had glanced at the reception room. He would not have been human not to be impressed. He was going to be a member of the Congress of the United States. He was going to be the first Negro congressman since the war. No﻿—really the first; all those earlier ones had been exceptions. He was real power. Power and money. Sara should not fool him this time. He understood her. He would have his own funds. He would, of course, follow the machine. He must keep power and get money. But he would have some independence﻿—more and more as time flew. Until﻿—He squared his shoulders, opened the door, and closed it behind him. The room was dimly lighted save the circle under the reading-light on the table. He looked about. No one was there. But there were voices in the hall. He waited.
Then slowly shame overwhelmed him. He was paying a price for power and money. A great, a terrible price. He was lying, cheating, stealing.

===Chinese===

她同马修的座位安排在餐厅边上，同比奇太太、格雷厄姆先生一桌。下一桌是银行家、州高官同两位年轻貌美的政客太太；其余各桌，尽可能每桌都配上一抹“颜色”。
可马修呢？萨拉不耐烦地想。该敬酒了，该发布那个重大宣布了——这场宴与会谈的顶点。比奇太太问起唐斯先生。
“他正同共产党们说最后几句呢。”萨拉笑着说。
“哦？他们来了？”卡德瓦拉德先生不安地问，“赶在这最后关头？”
“他们不肯进来——多半在交涉几桩善后的小事。”
可萨拉知道，她必须出面干预了。她信不过马修那副软塌塌的犹豫。这时候再翻起争论，可能前功尽弃。她再忍不下去了。她轻轻盈盈地站起身，手里托一只精巧的咖啡杯，笑着说了句闲话，移步向书房门去。萨米盯着她。其余的人也各以各的方式，觉出空气里有一丝不安。
“好了，唐斯先生，”萨拉说着，把门整个推开，“我们——”
大房间的光涌进小房间——照遍它的每个暗角。角落里那尊丑陋的中国神像咧着嘴笑，地板上一张蓝地毯幽幽发亮。正中央，两个人影绞作一团，紧紧相拥、微微颤抖，被光刻成惊人的剪影，跃在光里。
二十三
马修方才转身向书房走去。他朝客厅里扫了一眼。不是血肉之躯，就挡不住那一眼的冲击。他就要成为合众国国会议员了。他就要成为战后第一位黑人国会议员。不——是真正的第一位；从前那几位都只是例外。他是真实的权力。权力和金钱。这一回，可不能再让萨拉耍他。他识得她了。他要握有自己的钱袋。他自然要跟着机器走——必须保住权力、弄到钱。但他也要有一点独立——随着岁月飞逝，越来越多。直到——他挺起肩膀，推门进去，随手把门带上。屋里只有桌上那圈阅读灯的光，其余昏昏然。他环顾四周。没有人。门厅里却有语声。他等着。
随即，羞耻慢慢把他淹没。他在为权力和金钱付价。一笔巨大的、可怕的价。他在撒谎，在行骗，在偷窃。

===Original===

He was fooling these poor, driven slaves of industry. He had listened to their arguments all this afternoon. He had meant now to meet the delegation brusquely and tell them railingly that they were idiots, that he could do little﻿—something he’d try, but first he must get into Congress.
But he couldn’t find the words. He walked slowly over to the table and stood facing the door. It was all done. It was all over. He had sold his soul to the Devil, but this time he had sold it for something. Power? Money? Nonsense! He had sold it for beauty; for ideal beauty, fitness and curve and line; harmony and the words of the wise spoken long ago. He stood in his dinner jacket, sleek but careless, his shirt front rumpled, the satin of his lapel flicked with ash, his eyes tired and red, his hair untidy. He stood and looked at the door. The door opened; he dropped his eyes. He could not look up. He heard not the clumping tramp of a delegation, but the light step of a single person. He almost knew that it was the national president of the Box-Makers, come to make their last appeal. Somehow he had a desperate desire to defend himself before the merciless logic and wide knowledge of this official whom he had never met. She had never even written or answered his letters directly, but only through that dumpy stupid state president. She was to have been present this afternoon. She was not; only her pitiless written arraignment of his platform had been read. He had expected her tonight when he heard the delegation had arrived. But he could not look up. He simply took the paper which was handed to him, sensing the dark veil-like garments and the small hand in its cheap cotton glove. He took the paper which the woman handed him. On it was written:

> “Our labor union, in return for its support, asks if you will publicly promise them that on every occasion you will cast your vote in Congress for the interests of the poor man, the employee, and the worker, whenever and wherever these interests are opposed to the interests of the rich, the employer, and the capitalist. For instance﻿—”

===Chinese===

他在愚弄这些可怜的、被工业驱赶的奴隶。他们那一套道理，他听了一下午。他本打算硬邦邦地接待代表团，骂他们是一群蠢货，说自己能做的不多——总要试一试，可首先得进国会。
可他找不着那些字眼。他慢慢走到桌前，面门而立。全都定了。全都完了。他把灵魂卖给了魔鬼，可这一回，卖出了价钱。权力？金钱？胡扯！他卖它为了美；为了理想的美，为了合宜、曲线与线条；为了和谐，为了古昔智者的话语。他穿着晚礼服立在那里，光洁却潦草，衬衫前襟皱着，缎面领襟弹着烟灰，眼睛又倦又红，头发凌乱。他立着，望着门。门开了；他垂下眼。他抬不起头来。他听见的不是代表团的咚咚脚步，而是一个人的轻步。他几乎认定，来的是制箱工人工会的国际会长，来做最后的恳请。不知怎么，他有一种拼死的欲望，要在这位从未谋面、却逻辑无情、见识渊博的会长面前为自己辩白。她从未直接给他写过信、回过信，回信都经那个矮胖愚蠢的州会长转。今天下午她本该到场，却没有；只有她那封不留情面、痛陈他政纲的檄文被宣读。听说代表团到了，他料她今晚必来。可他抬不起头。他只是接过递来的那张纸，感觉到那暗色的纱似的衣衫，感觉到那只戴着廉价棉手套的小手。他接过那女人递来的纸。纸上写着：

> “本工会在以其支持为交换之际，请问您可愿公开承诺：每逢穷人的利益、雇工的利益、工人的利益，同富人、雇主、资本家的利益相违之时，无论何时何地，您在国会里的每一票都投给前者。例如——”

===Original===

Thus the paper began, and Matthew began slowly to read it. It was an absurd request. Matthew almost laughed aloud. He had thought to carry it off with a high hand, to laugh at these oafs and jolly them, insisting that first he must get to Congress, and then, of course, he would do what he could. Naturally, he was with them. Was he not a son of generations of workers? Well, then, trust him. But they had not come to argue. They were asking him to sign another paper, and to sign on the line. They could never be trusted to keep such a pledge silent. No, they would publish it to the world. Ha, ha, ha! What ghastly nonsense all this lying was! He stopped and went back to the paper and began reading it again. Something was gripping at him. Some tremendous reminder, and then suddenly the letters started out from the page and burned his gaze, they flamed and spread before him. He saw the strong beauty of the great curves, the breadth and yet delicate uplifting of the capitals, the long, sure sweep of the slurred links. Great God! That writing! He knew it as he knew his own face. His hand had started to his inner pocket﻿—then he tried to whisper, hoarsely﻿—
“Where﻿—who wrote this? Who﻿—” He looked up.
A dark figure stood by the table. An old dun-colored cloak flowed down upon her, and a veil lay across her head. Her thin dark hands, now bare and almost clawlike, gripped each other. They were colored hands. Quickly he stepped forward. And she came like a soft mist, unveiled and uncloaked before him. Always she seemed to come thus suddenly into his life. And yet perhaps it was he himself that supplied the surprise and sudden wonder. Perhaps in reality she had always come quite naturally to him, as she came now.
She was different, yet every difference emphasized something eternally marvelous. Her hair was cut short. All that long, cloudlike hair, the length and breadth of it, was gone; but still it nestled about her head like some halo. Her gown was loose, ill-fitting, straight; her hands, hard, wore no jewels, but were calloused, with broken nails. The small soft beauty of her face had become stronger and set in still lines. Only in the steadfast glory of her eyes showed unchanged the Princess. She watched him gravely as he searched her with his eyes; and then suddenly Matthew awoke.
Then suddenly the intolerable truth gripped him.

===Chinese===

纸就这样开了头，马修慢慢地读了下去。这是个荒唐的要求。马修几乎笑出声来。他本想拿出高高在上的派头应付过去，笑话这帮乡佬，哄他们一哄，说先得进了国会，然后自然尽力，他不消说同他们是一头的——他难道不是工人世代的后人？那么，信他好了。可他们不是来辩论的。他们是来请他再签一份文件的，签在指定的一线上。这样的誓约，指望他们保密是万万不能的。不，他们会把它公之于天下。哈，哈，哈！这一切谎话，何其荒唐透顶！他停住，又回到纸上，重新读。有什么东西攥住了他。一声巨大的提醒，随后，那些字忽然从纸面上跳了出来，灼着他的目光，它们燃烧着、漫延着。他看见那大幅曲线的雄劲之美，大写字母开阔而挺拔的抬举，连笔处悠长笃定的荡漾。伟大的上帝啊！那笔迹！他认得它，像认得自己的脸。他的手已经伸向内袋——随即他哑着嗓子想问——
“哪儿——这是谁写的？谁——”他抬起头。
一个暗色的身影立在桌旁。一件暗驼色的斗篷从她身上流泻而下，一块面纱覆在她头上。她那瘦削的深色双手，此刻赤裸着，几乎像爪，彼此紧攥。那是有色人的手。他抢步上前。她便像一缕软雾，在他面前揭开面纱、褪下斗篷。她似乎总是这样蓦地闯进他的生命。可也许，制造这份意外与惊奇的，本是他自己。也许事实上，她从来都是自然而然地来到他跟前，一如此刻。
她变了，而每一处不同，都衬出某种永恒的奇迹。她的头发剪短了。那一头长发，那绵长的云雾，没有了；却仍像一圈光环，偎在她头顶。她的裙衫宽大、不合身、直筒筒；她的双手粗硬，不戴首饰，长着茧，指甲劈裂。她脸上那小巧柔嫩的美，变得坚毅，凝成安静的线条。唯有她眼中那份坚定不移的光华，还是当年的公主。她静静望着他，任他用目光搜寻；随后，马修猛然醒了。
骤然，那不可忍受的真实攫住了他。

===Original===

He lifted his hands to heaven, stretched them to touch the width of the world, and swept her into his tight embrace. He caught her to him so fiercely that her little feet almost left the ground, and her arms curled around his neck as their lips met.
“Kautilya,” he sobbed. “Princess of India.”
“Matthew,” she answered, in a small frightened whisper.
There was a silence as of a thousand years, a silence while again he found her lips and kept them, and his arms crept along the frail, long length of her body, and he cried as he whispered in her ears. Perhaps some murmur from the further rooms came to them, for suddenly they started apart. She would have said the things she had planned to say, but she did not. All the greater things were forgotten. She only said as he stared upon her with wild light in his eyes:
“I am changed.”
And he answered:
“The Princess that I worshiped is become the working-woman whom I love. Life has beaten out the gold to this fine stuff.” And then with hanging head he said: “But I, ah, I am unchanged. I am the same flying dust.”
She walked toward him and put both hands upon his shoulders and said, “Flying dust, that is it. Flying dust that fills the heaven and turns the sunlight into jewels.” And then suddenly she stood straight before him. “Matthew, Matthew!” she cried. “See, I came to save you! I came to save your soul from hell.”
“Too late,” he murmured. “I have sold it to the Devil.”
“Then at any price,” she cried in passion, “at any price, I will buy it back.”
“What shall we do﻿—what can we do?” he whispered, troubled, in her hair.
“We must give up. We must tell all men the truth; we must go out of this Place of Death and this city of the Face of Fear, untrammeled and unbound, walking together hand in hand.”
And he cried, “Kautilya, darling!”
And she said, “Matthew, my Man!”
“Your body is Beauty, and Beauty is your Soul, and Soul and Body spell Freedom to my tortured groping life!” he whispered.
“Benediction﻿—I have sought you, man of God, in the depths of hell, to bring your dead faith back to the stars; and now you are mine.”
And suddenly there was light.
And suddenly from Matthew dropped all the little hesitancies and cynicisms. The years of disbelief were not. The world was one woman and one cause. And with one arm almost lifting her as she strained toward him, they walked shoulder to shoulder out into that blinding light.

===Chinese===

他双手举向天空，张开去够世界的宽度，把她紧紧拥进怀抱。他把她搂得那样紧，她的双脚几乎离了地；她的手臂蜷上他的颈项，唇贴上了唇。
“考蒂利亚，”他呜咽道，“印度的公主。”
“马修，”她应道，声音细小，是一声带着惊惶的耳语。
有一阵千年的静默。静默中他再次寻到她的唇，久久不放；他的手臂沿着她纤长的身子一寸寸挪动；他伏在她耳边低语，一边流着泪。也许远处的房间里传来了一丝响动，两人蓦地分开。她原想说那些准备好的话，却没有说。那些更要紧的话全被忘掉了。他目光灼灼地盯住她，她只说：
“我变了。”
他答道：
“我从前膜拜的公主，成了我如今深爱的女工。生活把金子打成了这般细亮的箔。”随后他垂首道：“可我，唉，我没有变。我还是那飞尘。”
她走向他，双手按上他的肩，说：“飞尘，正是。飞尘，漫天漫地，把日光变作珠宝。”随即她陡然在他面前站直。“马修，马修！”她喊道，“看，我是来救你的！我来把你的灵魂从地狱里救出来。”
“太迟了，”他喃喃道，“我已把它卖给了魔鬼。”
“那么无论什么代价，”她激昂地喊道，“无论什么代价，我也要把它赎回来。”
“我们怎么办——我们还能怎么办？”他困惑地在她发间低语。
“我们必须放手。我们必须向所有人说出真话；我们必须走出这死亡之殿、这恐惧之面的城，无拘无束，手挽着手一路同行。”
他喊：“考蒂利亚，亲爱的！”
她说：“马修，我的男人！”
“你的身是美，美就是你的魂，而魂与身，于我受尽折磨、摸索前行的生命，拼出的正是自由！”他低语。
“祝福——我在地狱深处寻到了你，上帝的人，要把你死去的信仰带回星辰；如今你是我的了。”
蓦然，光亮了起来。
蓦然，马修身上一切小小的迟疑同讥诮纷纷剥落。那些不信的岁月不复存在。世界就是一个女人、一桩事业。他一只臂膀几乎把她凌空托起，她向他偎紧；两人肩并着肩，走进那片眩目的光里。

===Original===

And as they walked there seemed to rise above the startled, puzzled guests some high and monstrous litany, staccato, with moaning monotones, bearing down upon their whisperings, exclamations, movements, words and cries, across the silver and crystal of the service:
“I will not have your nomination.”
(What does he mean﻿—who is this woman?)
“I’d rather go to hell than to Congress.”
(Is the man mad?)
“I’m through with liars, thieves, and hypocrites.”
(This is insulting, shameless, scandalous!)
“The cause that was dead is alive again; the love that I lost is found!”
(A married man and a slut from the streets!)
“Have mercy, have mercy upon us!” whispered the woman.
The company surged to its feet with hiss and oath.
Sara, white to the lips, her hard-clenched hand crushing the fragile China to bits, walked slowly backward before them with blazing eyes.
“I am free!” said Matthew.
The low voice of the Princess floated back again from the crimson curtains of the hall:
“Kyrie Eleison.”
The high voice of Sara, like the final fierce upthrusting of the Host, shrilled to a scream:
“You fool﻿—you Goddamned fool!”
XXIV
The hall door crashed. The stunned company stared, moved, and rushed hurriedly to get away, with scant formality of leave-taking. It was raining without, a cold wet sleet, but the beautiful apartment vomited its guests upon the sidewalk while taxis rushed to aid.
The president of the Woman’s City Club rushed out the door with flushed face.
“These Negroes!” she said to the settlement worker. “They are simply impossible! I have known it all along, but I had begun to hope; such persistent, ineradicable immorality! and flaunted purposely in our very faces! It is intolerable!”
The settlement worker murmured somewhat indistinctly about the world being “well lost” for something, as they climbed into a cab and flew north.
The Republican boss, the state official, and the banker loomed in the doorway, pulling on their gloves, adjusting their coats and cravats, and hailing hurrying taxis.
“Well, of all the damned fiascos,” said the banker.
“Niggers in Congress! Well!” said the official.
“It is just as well,” said the boss. “In fact it is almost providential. It looked as though we had to send a Negro to Congress. That unpleasant possibility is now indefinitely postponed. Of course, now we’ll have to send you.”
“Oh!” said the banker softly and deprecatingly.

===Chinese===

两人走过时，惊愕茫然的宾客头顶上，仿佛升起一支高亢而可怖的连祷，顿挫分明，夹着呜咽般的单调，压过他们的窃语、惊叹、骚动、言语和呼喊，掠过席面的银器与水晶：
“我不要你们的提名。”
（他说什么——这女人是谁？）
“我宁可下地狱，也不进国会。”
（这人疯了吗？）
“我同骗子、窃贼、伪君子一刀两断。”
（无礼，无耻，下流！）
“死了的事业又活了；失去的爱又寻回了！”
（一个有妇之夫，一个街头来的荡妇！）
“怜悯我们，怜悯我们吧。”那女子低声道。
满座宾客嘶嘶作响、咒骂着霍然起立。
萨拉面白如纸，死攥的手把精瓷捏得粉碎，目光如炬，在他们面前缓缓退着走。
“我自由了！”马修说。
公主低低的声音又从门厅猩红的帘幕后飘了回来：
“主啊，怜悯。”
萨拉尖利的嗓音，像圣体最后猛然的高举，直拔成一声长啸：
“你这傻瓜——你这遭天谴的傻瓜！”
二十四
客厅的门轰然撞上。惊呆的宾客们瞪着眼、骚动着，手忙脚乱地夺路而走，连告辞的虚礼都顾不得。外面下着雨，冰冷的湿霰；那套漂亮的公寓把宾客们尽数吐到人行道上，出租车纷纷赶来救场。
妇女市政俱乐部主席涨红着脸冲出门。
“这些黑人！”她对那位社会服务工作者说，“简直无从理喻！我早就知道，可我一度竟起了指望；这样冥顽、这样根深蒂固的不道德！还故意炫示到我们脸上！忍无可忍！”
社会服务工作者含含糊糊咕哝了一句“为了……世界失去也值”之类的话，两人爬进一辆车，向北飞驰而去。
共和党巨头、州高官同银行家堵在门口，戴手套、整衣扣、招手叫车。
“嘿，多大的一个烂摊子。”银行家说。
“黑鬼进国会！哼！”那位官员说。
“这样倒好，”巨头说，“简直是天意。眼看我们非送一个黑人进国会不可了，如今这个不愉快的可能，无限期推迟了。当然，回头只好送你上了。”
“哦！”银行家轻轻地说，带着假意的推辞。

===Original===

“It is going to cost something,” said the boss shrewdly. “You will have to buy up all these darky newspapers and grease Sammy’s paw extraordinarily well. The point is, buying is possible now. They have no comeback. Sammy may have aspirations, but I think we can make even him see that it will be unwise to put up another colored candidate now. No, the thing has turned out extraordinarily well; but I wonder what the devil got hold of Towns, acting as though he was crazy?”
The physician’s wife and the lawyer’s lingered a little, clustering to one side so as to avoid meeting the white folks; they stared and whispered.
“It is the most indecent thing I have heard of,” said the physician’s wife. The lawyer’s wife moaned in her distress:
“To think of a Negro acting that way, and before these people! And after all this work. Won’t we ever amount to anything? Won’t we ever get any leaders? I am simply disgusted and discouraged. I’ll never work for another Negro leader as long as I live.”
And they followed their husbands to the two large sedans that stood darkly groaning, waiting.
The physician snarled to the minister, “And with the streets full of women cheaper and prettier.”
The Labor delegation had pushed into the library as Matthew and Kautilya left, and entered the reception room. They stood now staring at the disheveled room and the guests rushing away.
“What’s happened?”
“Has he told them what’s what?”
“Are they deserting us? Are they running away?”
But the colored club women walked away in silence in the rain. They parted at the corner and one said:
“I’m proud of him, at last.”
But the other spit:
“The beast!”
XXV
Sammy’s world was tottering, and looking upon its astonishing ruins he could only gasp blankly:
“What t’ hell!”
Never before in his long career and wide acquaintanceship with human nature had it behaved in so fantastic and unpredictable a manner. Never had it acted with such incalculable and utter disregard of all rules and wise saws. That a man should cheat, lie, steal, and seduce women, was to Sammy’s mind almost normal; that he should tell the truth, give away his money, and stick by his wife was also at times probable. These things happened. He’d seen them done.

===Chinese===

“这可得破费一点。”巨头精明地说，“你得把这些黑鬼报纸统统买下来，还得格外重重地给萨米上油。好在如今买得动了——他们没了还手之力。萨米也许还存着念想，可我想，咱们连他也能说得通：这时候再推出一个有色候选人，是不明智的。不，事情的结果好得不能再好；我就是纳闷，到底什么邪魔附了唐斯的身，弄得他跟疯了似的？”
医生的太太同律师的太太流连了片刻，聚在一边，避着白人；她们瞪着眼，窃窃私语。
“这是我听说过的最不成体统的事。”医生的太太说。律师的太太悲叹道：
“想想看，一个黑人当众闹出这种事，还当着这些人！这许多心血全完了。我们就永远成不了气候？就永远出不了领袖？我真是又厌恶又灰心。这辈子我再不给第二个黑人领袖卖力气了。”
她们跟着各自的丈夫，走向两辆黑洞洞地哼着、等在那儿的大轿车。
医生朝牧师咆哮：“何况满大街多的是更便宜也更标致的女人。”
劳工代表团在马修同考蒂利亚离开时已挤进书房，又走进客厅。此刻他们瞪着这狼藉的房间和四散奔逃的宾客。
“出什么事了？”
“他把实情都抖给他们了？”
“他们抛弃我们了？他们逃了？”
可有色俱乐部的妇女们默默走进雨里。她们在街角分手，一个说：
“我终于为他骄傲了。”
另一个却啐了一口：
“畜生！”
二十五
萨米的天地在崩塌。望着这惊人的废墟，他只能干瞪眼，喃喃一句：
“搞什么名堂！”
在他漫长的生涯里，在他对人性广博的阅历中，人性从未表现得如此离奇、如此不可测度，从未这样全然无视一切规矩与格言。一个人撒谎、偷窃、骗人、勾引女人，在萨米看来近乎常态；一个人讲真话、散尽钱财、守着妻子，有时也在情理之中。这些事都发生过，他都亲眼见过。

===Original===

But that a man with everything should choose nothing: that a man with high office in his grasp, money ready to pour into his pocket, a home like this, and both a wife and a sweetheart, should toss them all away and walk out into the rain without his hat, just for an extra excursion with a skirt﻿—
“What t’ hell!” gasped Sammy, groping back into the empty house. Then suddenly he heard the voice of Sara.
He found her standing stark alone, a pitiful, tragic figure amid the empty glitter of her triumph, with her flesh-colored chiffon and her jewels, her smooth stockings and silver slippers. She had stripped the beads from her throat, and they were dripping through her clenched fingers. She had half torn the lace from her breast, and she stood there flushed, trembling, furious with anger, and almost screaming to ears that did not hear and to guests already gone.
“Haven’t I been decent? Haven’t I fought off you beasts and made me a living and a home with my own hands? Wasn’t I married like a respectable woman, and didn’t I drag this fool out of jail and make him a man? And what do I get? *What do I get?* Here I am, disgraced and ruined, mocked and robbed, a laughingstock to all Chicago. What did he want? What did the jackass want, my God? A cabaret instead of a home? A whore instead of a wife? Wasn’t I true to him? Did I ever let a man touch me? I made money﻿—sure, I made money. I *had* to make money. *He* couldn’t. I made money out of politics. What in hell is politics for, if it isn’t for somebody to make money? Must we hand all the graft over to the holy white folks? And now he disgraces me! Just when I win, he throws me over for a common bawd from the streets, and a mess of dirty white laborers; a common slut stealing decent women’s husbands. Oh﻿—”
Sammy touched her hesitatingly on the shoulder and pleaded:
“Don’t crack, kid. Stand the gaff. I’ll see you through.”
But she shrank away from him and screamed:
“Get out, don’t touch me. Oh, damn him, damn him! I wish I could horsewhip them; I wish I could kill them both.”
And suddenly Sara crumpled to the floor, crushing and tearing her silks and scattering her jewels, drawing her knees up tight and gripping them with twitching hands, burying her hair, her head and streaming eyes, in the crimson carpet, and rolling and shaking and struggling with strangling sobs.

===Chinese===

可一个应有尽有的人，偏要选择一无所有：一个高官在握、金钱正待滚滚入袋、家业如此、既有一妻又有一爱的人，竟把这一切统统抛掉，帽子也不戴，走进雨里——只为同一个“娘们”多逛一遭——
“搞什么名堂！”萨米倒抽着气，摸索着退回空屋。忽然，他听见了萨拉的声音。
他发现她孤零零直挺挺立着，一身肉色雪纺绸，一身珠宝，丝袜锃亮，银鞋熠熠，在她那空荡辉煌的凯旋中央，一个可怜的、悲剧的身影。她已把珠串从颈上扯下，珠子正从她紧攥的指缝间往下滴。她已把胸前的花边撕开了一半。她立在那里，红着脸，发着抖，怒不可遏，对着一双听不见的耳朵、一群早已散尽的宾客，几乎是尖声嘶喊：
“我不规矩吗？我没有斗开你们这些畜生，凭自己这双手挣出一份营生、一个家吗？我不是像个体面女人一样嫁的人？不是我把这个蠢货从牢里拖出来，把他造就成个人？我得了什么？*我得了什么？*瞧瞧我，丢尽了脸，毁了，遭人讥笑，遭人抢夺，成了全芝加哥的笑柄。他要什么？这头蠢驴要什么，我的天？要一家卡巴莱不要一个家？要一个婊子不要一个太太？我对他不忠吗？我让哪个男人碰过一根指头吗？我挣了钱——不错，我挣了钱。我*不得不*挣钱。*他*挣不来。我从政治里挣钱。政治不为挣钱，还要它做什么？难道油水全得孝敬神圣的白人老爷？如今他倒羞辱我！偏偏在我赢的时候，他为了一个街边拉客的贱货、一帮肮脏的白人苦力把我甩了；一个专偷人家丈夫的下贱货。哦——”
萨米迟疑着碰碰她的肩，劝道：
“别垮，丫头。咬牙顶住。我保你过关。”
可她缩开身，尖叫：
“滚开，别碰我。哦，该死的，该死的！真想拿马鞭抽他们；真想宰了他们两个。”
萨拉蓦地瘫倒在地板上，绸衣压皱撕裂，珠宝四散，双膝死死收起，痉挛的双手紧紧箍住，把头发、头颅同泪流满面埋进猩红的地毯，翻滚着、抖动着，同噎住咽喉的呜咽挣扎。

===Original===

---
While without gray mists lay thin upon a pale and purple city. Through them, like cold, wet tears dripped the slow brown rain. The muffled roar of moving millions thundered low upon the wind, and the blue wind sighed and sank into the black night; and through the chill dripping of the waters, hatless and coatless, moved two shapes, hand in hand, with uplifted heads, singing to the storm.

===Chinese===

---
而外面，灰雾薄薄地覆着一座苍白而紫暗的城。雾中，冷而湿的泪——褐色的雨——缓缓滴落。千百万人行进那沉闷的轰鸣，低低地滚在风上；蓝色的风叹息着，沉入黑夜；透过水的寒凉滴沥，两个身影不戴帽、不穿外套，手牵着手，昂着头，向风暴歌唱。

## Part / 部
===Original===

I The Exile
August, 1923
Summer is come with bursting flower and promise of perfect fruit. Rain is rolling down Nile and Niger. Summer sings on the sea where giant ships carry busy worlds, while mermaids swarm the shores. Earth is pregnant. Life is big with pain and evil and hope. Summer in blue New York; summer in gray Berlin; summer in the red heart of the world!
I
Matthew Towns was in a cold white fury. He stood on the deck of the *Orizaba* looking down on the flying sea. In the night America had disappeared and now there was nothing but waters heaving in the bright morning. There were many passengers walking, talking, laughing; but none of them spoke to Matthew. They spoke about him, noting his tall, lean form and dark brown face, the stiff, curled mass of his sinewy hair, and the midnight of his angry eyes.
They spoke about him, and he was acutely conscious of every word. Each word heard and unheard pierced him and quivered in the quick. Yet he leaned stiff and grim, gazing into the sea, his back toward all. He saw the curled grace of the billows, the changing blues and greens; and he saw, there at the edge of the world, certain shining shapes that leapt and played.
Then they changed﻿—always they changed; and there arose the great cool height of the room at the University of Manhattan. Again he stood before the walnut rail that separated student and Dean. Again he felt the bewilderment, the surge of hot surprise.
“I cannot register at all for obstetrics?”
“No,” said the Dean quietly, his face growing red. “I’m sorry, but the committee﻿—”
“But﻿—but﻿—why, I’m Towns. I’ve already finished two years﻿—I’ve ranked my class. I took honors﻿—why﻿—I﻿—This is my Junior year﻿—I must﻿—.”
He was sputtering with amazement.
“I’m sorry.”
“Hell! I’m not asking your pity, I’m demanding﻿—”
The Dean’s lips grew thin and hard, and he sent the shaft home as if to rid himself quickly of a hateful task.
“Well﻿—what did you expect? Juniors must have obstetrical work. Do you think white women patients are going to have a nigger doctor delivering their babies?”
Then Matthew’s fury had burst its bounds; he had thrown his certificates, his marks and commendations straight into the drawn white face of the Dean and stumbled out. He came out on Broadway with its wide expanse, and opposite a little park.

===Chinese===

一 流亡
一九二三年八月
夏天来了，繁花迸放，饱孕着完美果实的允诺。雨水顺着尼罗河和尼日尔河滚落。夏天在大海上歌唱，巨轮载着忙碌的世界，人鱼成群簇拥着海岸。大地怀了身孕。生命硕大，怀着痛苦、罪恶和希望。夏天在蓝色的纽约；夏天在灰色的柏林；夏天在世界的赤色心脏！

一
马修·唐斯（Matthew Towns）正处在一阵冰冷的白色狂怒里。他站在「奥里萨巴」号的甲板上，俯视着飞掠的海面。夜里，美洲消失了，如今只剩明亮的早晨里翻涌的海水。甲板上许多乘客在散步、交谈、说笑；没有一个人同马修说话。他们谈论他，注意他瘦长笔挺的身量和深棕色的脸，注意他多筋的头发那硬硬的一团鬈曲，和他怒目里的一片午夜。
他们谈论他，他对每一个字都敏锐地知觉。听见和没听见的字字句句都刺进他，在痛处颤抖。可他僵硬而阴沉地倚着，凝望海心，背对众人。他看见巨浪蜷曲的优雅，变幻的蓝与绿；他看见，在世界的边上，有些闪闪发亮的形体在跳跃嬉戏。
随后它们变了——它们总在变；升起来的是曼哈顿大学那间屋子高而清凉的空阔。他又一次立在隔开学生与院长的胡桃木栏杆前。又一次感到那份惶惑，那阵火热的惊愕的翻涌。
“产科我一点都注册不了？”
“不行，”院长平静地说，脸涨红了。“对不起，可是委员会——”
“可是——可是——为什么，我是唐斯（Towns）。我已经读完了两年——我在班里名列前茅。我得过荣誉——为什么——我——这是我的三年级——我必须——。”
他惊讶得语无伦次。
“对不起。”
“见鬼！我不是来讨你的怜悯，我是要求——”
院长的嘴唇抿得又薄又硬，他把那支镖射了出去，仿佛要赶紧甩掉一桩可憎的差事。
“哼——你还指望什么？三年级必须做产科实习。你以为白人女病人会肯让一个黑鬼医生来接生吗？”
随后马修的怒火冲破了堤；他把他的证书、成绩单和嘉奖状，直直摔进院长那张绷紧的白脸，踉跄着冲了出去。他出来到了宽阔的百老汇大街上，对面是一座小公园。

===Original===

He turned and glanced up at the gray piles of tan buildings, threatening the sky, which were the University’s great medical center. He stared at them. Then with bowed head he plunged down 165th Street. The gray-blue Hudson lay beneath his feet, and above it piled the Palisades upward in gray and green. He walked and walked: down the curving drive between high homes and the Hudson; by graveyard and palace; tomb and restaurant; beauty and smoke. All the afternoon he walked, all night, and into the gray dawn of another morning.
II
In after years when Matthew looked back upon this first sea voyage, he remembered it chiefly as the time of sleep; of days of long, long rest and thought, after work and hurry and rage. He was indeed very tired. A year of the hardest kind of study had been followed by a summer as clerical assistant in a colored industrial insurance office, in the heat of Washington. Thence he had hurried straight to the university with five hundred dollars of tuition money in his pocket; and now he was sailing to Europe.
He had written his mother﻿—that tall, gaunt, brown mother, hard-sinewed and somber-eyed, carrying her years unbroken, who still toiled on the farm in Prince James County, Virginia﻿—he had written almost curtly: “I’m through. I cannot and will not stand America longer. I’m off. I’ll write again soon. Don’t worry. I’m well. I love you.” Then he had packed his clothes, given away his books and instruments, and sailed in mid-August on the first ship that offered after that long tramp of tears and rage, after days of despair. And so here he was.
Where was he going? He glanced at the pale-faced man who asked him. “I don’t know,” he answered shortly. The good-natured gentleman stared, nonplussed, Matthew turned away. Where *was* he going? The ship was going to Antwerp. But that, to Matthew, was sheer accident. He was going away first of all. After that? Well, he had thought of France. There they were at least civilized in their prejudices. But his French was poor. He had studied German because his teachers regarded German medicine as superior to all other. He would then go to Germany. From there? Well, there was Moscow. Perhaps they could use a man in Russia whose heart was hate. Perhaps he would move on to the Near or Far East and find hard work and peace.

===Chinese===

他转过身，仰望那一堆堆灰色的褐石大楼，威胁着天空——那是大学的宏大医学中心。他瞪着它们。随后垂着头，一头扎进第一百六十五街。灰蓝色的哈得逊河躺在脚下，河对岸帕利塞德山崖灰一层绿一层地向上堆。他走啊走：沿着高宅与哈得逊河之间那条弯曲的车道往下；经过墓园与宫殿；陵墓与饭店；美与烟。走了一个下午，一个整夜，走进另一个早晨灰色的黎明。

二
多年以后，马修回顾这头一次海上航行时，记得最清的是它是一段睡眠的时光；是在劳碌、奔忙和狂怒之后，长长长长的休息与思索的日子。他确实累极了。一年最苦的学业之后，是一个夏天在华盛顿的酷热里，一家有色人产业保险行当文书助手。随后他揣着五百块学费，径直赶回大学；如今他正驶往欧洲。
他给母亲写过信——那位高瘦的棕色母亲，筋骨结实，眼神沉郁，岁月压不弯她，如今仍在弗吉尼亚州詹姆斯王子县的农场上操劳——信写得近乎简慢：“我不读了。我不能、也不会再忍受美国。我走了。不久再写信。别担心。我很好。我爱你。”随后他收拾了衣裳，把书和器械送了人，八月中旬，在泪水与狂怒的那场长奔、在那绝望的许多天之后，搭上头一班肯载他的船。于是他就到了这儿。
他去哪儿？他瞟了一眼发问的那位白脸先生。“不知道。”他简短地答。那位好心的先生愣住了；马修转过脸去。他*究竟*去哪儿？船去安特卫普。可那对马修纯属偶然。他首先是要走开。然后呢？这个，他想过法国。那里的偏见至少是文明的。可他法语不行。他学的是德语，因为他的老师们把德国医学看得高于一切。那他就去德国。再然后呢？还有莫斯科。俄国兴许用得上一个心里装满仇恨的人。兴许他再往下走，到近东或远东，找一份苦工和安宁。

===Original===

At any rate, he was going somewhere; and suddenly letting his strained nerves go, he dragged his chair to a sheltered nook apart and slept.
To the few who approached him at all, Matthew was boorish and gruff. He knew that he was unfair, but he could not help it. All the little annoyances, which in healthier days he would have laughed away, avoided, or shortly forgotten, now piled themselves on his sore soul. The roommate assigned him discovered that his companion was colored and quickly decamped with his baggage. A Romanian who spoke little English, and had not learned American customs, replaced him.
Matthew entered the dining-room with nerves a-quiver. Every eye caught him during the meal﻿—some, curiously; some, derisively; some, in half-contemptuous surprise. He felt and measured all, looking steadily into his plate. On one side sat an old and silent man. To the empty seat on the other side he heard acutely a swish of silk approach﻿—a pause and a consultation, The seat remained empty. At the next meal he was placed in a far corner with people too simple or poor or unimportant to protest. He heaved a sigh to have it over and ate thereafter in silence and quickly.
So at last life settled down, soothed by the sea﻿—the rhythm and song of the old, old sea. He slept and read and slept; stared at the water; lived his life again to its wild climax; put down repeatedly the cold, hard memory; and drifting, slept again.
Yet always, as he rose from the deep seas of sleep and reverie, the silent battle with his fellows went on. Now he yearned fiercely for someone to talk to; to talk and talk and explain and prove and disprove. He glimpsed faces at times, intelligent, masterful. They had brains; if they knew him they would choose him as companion, friend; but they did not know him. They did not want to know him. They glanced at him momentarily and then looked away. They were afraid to be noticed noticing him.
And he? He would have killed himself rather than have them dream he would accept a greeting, much less a confidence. He looked past and through and over them with blank unconcern. So much so that a few﻿—simpler souls, themselves wandering alone hither and thither in this aimless haphazard group of a fugitive week﻿—ventured now and then to understand: “I never saw none of you fellers like you﻿—” began one amiable Italian. “No?”

===Chinese===

不管怎样，他总要去个地方；他猛然松开绷紧的神经，把椅子拖到一个避风的角落，睡着了。
对少数几个真来搭话的人，马修粗鲁而呛人。他知道自己不讲理，可忍不住。一切小小的烦恼——换在健康的日子里，他一笑就散、绕开就忘——如今全堆上他溃痛的灵魂。分给他的同舱客一发现伙伴是有色人，立刻卷起行李搬走了。顶替的是个罗马尼亚人，英文说得磕巴，没学过美国规矩。
马修走进餐厅，神经突突地跳。饭桌上每一双眼睛都逮住他——有的好奇，有的讥诮，有的半带轻蔑地诧异。他把这一切都感到、都量过，眼睛稳稳盯着盘子。一边坐着一位沉默的老人。另一边那个空位，他尖利地听见一阵绸衣窸窣着临近——一停，一商量。位子空着没坐人。下一顿，他被安排到遥远的角落，同一些太普通、太穷或太无足轻重、无从抗议的人坐在一起。他叹了口气，巴不得这顿吃完，从此默默而迅速地吃。
就这样，生活终于安定下来，被海抚平——那古老的、古老的海的节拍与歌。他睡了读、读了睡；盯着水；把一生重演到它狂乱的高潮；一遍遍按下那段又冷又硬的记忆；飘飘荡荡，又睡着了。
然而，每当他从睡眠与冥想的深海里浮上来，同旅伴们那场无声的战斗仍在继续。这时他强烈渴望有个人说话；说啊说啊，解释、论证、驳斥。他时时瞥见几张脸，聪明，有主宰力。他们有头脑；倘若认识他，他们会选他做伴、做友；可他们不认识他。他们不想认识他。他们朝他瞟一眼，随即移开。他们怕被人看见自己在看他。
而他呢？他宁可杀了自己，也不肯让他们梦想他会接受一声问候，更别说一句知心话。他用一片空白的不在乎，从他们身上、中间、上面望过去。以至于有那么几个——心思简单些的、自己也在这逃亡一周的散漫人堆里东荡西逛的灵魂——时不时斗胆来求理解：“我从来没见过你们那路人像你这样的——”一个和气的意大利人开了头。“是么？”

===Original===

answered Matthew briefly and walked away.
“You’re not lonesome?” asked a New England merchant, adding hastily, “I’ve always been interested in your people.”
“Yes,” said Matthew with an intonation that stopped further conversation along that line.
“No,” he growled at an insulted missionary, “I don’t believe in God﻿—never did﻿—do you?”
And yet all the time he was sick at heart and yearning. If but one soul with sense, knowledge, and decency had firmly pierced his awkwardly hung armor, he could have helped make these long hard days human. And one did, a moment, now and then﻿—a little tow-haired girl of five or six with great eyes. She came suddenly on him one day: “Won’t you play ball with me?” He started, smiled, and looked down. He loved children. Then he saw the mother approaching﻿—typically Middle-West American, smartly dressed and conscious of her social inferiority. Slowly his smile faded; quickly he walked away. Yet nearly every day thereafter the child smiled shyly at him as though they shared a secret, and he smiled slowly back; but he was careful never to see the elaborate and most exclusive mother.
Thus they came to green Plymouth and passed the fortress walls of Cherbourg and, sailing by merry vessels and white cliffs, rode on to the Scheldt. All day they crept past fields and villages, ships and windmills, up to the slender cathedral tower of Antwerp.
III
Sitting in the Viktoria Café, on the Unter den Linden, Berlin, Matthew looked again at the white leviathan﻿—at that mighty organization of white folk against which he felt himself so bitterly in revolt. It was the same vast, remorseless machine in Berlin as in New York. Of course, there were differences﻿—differences which he felt like a tingling pain. He had on the street here no sense of public insult; he was treated as he was dressed, and today he had dressed carefully, wearing the new suit made for the opening school term; he had on his newest dark crimson tie that burned with the red in his smooth brown face; he carried cane and gloves, and he had walked into this fashionable café with an air. He knew that he would be served, politely and without question.
Yes, in Europe he could at least eat where he wished so long as he paid. Yet the very thought made him angry; conceive a man outcast in his own native land!

===Chinese===

马修简短地应了一声，走开了。
“你不孤单吗？”一位新英格兰商人问，又赶忙补一句，“我一向对你们的民族很有兴趣。”
“孤单。”马修说，一个语调截断了这条线上的谈话。
“不，”他对一位受辱的传教士咆哮，“我不信上帝——从来没信过——你信吗？”
然而自始至终，他心里发着病，渴望着。哪怕有一个有见识、有学问、有体面的人，坚定地刺穿他那身挂得笨拙的铠甲，这漫长艰苦的日子就能被帮着过得像人。而确实有一个，一时片刻，隔三差五——一个五六岁的、淡黄头发、大眼睛的小姑娘。有一天她突然撞见他：“你陪我打球好不好？”他一惊，笑了，低头看她。他爱孩子。随后他看见母亲走近——典型的中西部美国人，穿得漂亮，一心自觉门第不够。他的笑慢慢淡了；他快步走开。可打那以后，几乎每天，那孩子都羞怯地朝他微笑，仿佛两人共享一个秘密；他也缓缓回她一笑；只是他小心地，永远不去看见那位精致而极度排他的母亲。
就这样他们到了绿色的普利茅斯，驶过瑟堡的要塞高墙，傍着欢快的船帆和白垩峭壁，驶入斯海尔德河。一整天，他们在田野与村庄、船舶与风车之间缓缓爬行，直到安特卫普那细瘦的大教堂尖塔。

三
坐在柏林菩提树下大街的维多利亚咖啡馆里，马修再次打量这头白色的利维坦——那座他自觉正对它殊死反叛的、白人的庞大组织。柏林的机器同纽约的一样浩大、一样无情。当然，有差别——那些差别他感到起来像刺痛。在这儿的街上，他没有当众受辱之感；人家按他的衣着待他，而今天他穿得仔细：开学新做的那套新衣；系一条最新的暗紫红领带，同他光滑棕脸里的红互相烧着；拿着手杖和手套，派头十足地走进这家时髦咖啡馆。他知道人家会招待他，客气而不多问。
是的，在欧洲，只要付钱，他至少能在想吃的地方吃饭。可这念头本身就叫他冒火：想想吧，一个人在自己的祖国被放逐！

===Original===

And even here, how long could he pay, he who sat with but two hundred dollars in the world, with no profession, no work, no friends, no country? Yes, these folks treated him as a man﻿—or rather, they did not, on looking at him, treat him as less than a man. But what of it? They were white. What would they say if he asked for work? Or a chance for his brains? Or a daughter in marriage? There was a blonde and blue-eyed girl at the next table catching his eye. Faugh! She was for public sale and thought him a South American, an Egyptian, or a rajah with money. He turned quickly away.
Oh, he was lonesome; lonesome and homesick with a dreadful homesickness. After all, in leaving white, he had also left black America﻿—all that he loved and knew. God! he never dreamed how much he loved that soft, brown world which he had so carelessly, so unregretfully cast away. What would he not give to clasp a dark hand now, to hear a soft Southern roll of speech, to kiss a brown cheek? To see warm, brown, crinkly hair and laughing eyes. God﻿—he was lonesome. So utterly, terribly lonesome. And then﻿—he saw the Princess!
Many, many times in after years he tried to catch and rebuild that first wildly beautiful fantasy which the girl’s face stirred in him. He knew well that no human being could be quite as beautiful as she looked to him then. He could never quite recapture the first ecstasy of the picture, and yet always even the memory thrilled and revived him. Never after that first glance was he or the world quite the same.
First and above all came that sense of color: into this world of pale yellowish and pinkish parchment, that absence or negation of color, came, suddenly, a glow of golden brown skin. It was darker than sunlight and gold; it was lighter and livelier than brown. It was a living, glowing crimson, veiled beneath brown flesh. It called for no light and suffered no shadow, but glowed softly of its own inner radiance.
Then came the sense of the woman herself: she was young and tall even when seated, and she bore herself above all with a singularly regal air. She was slim and lithe, gracefully curved. Unseeing, past him and into the struggling, noisy street, she was looking with eyes that were pools of night﻿—liquid, translucent, haunting depths﻿—whose brilliance made her face a glory and a dream.
Matthew pulled himself together and tried to act sensibly.

===Chinese===

可就在这儿，他又能付多久？他这个坐拥全世界只有两百块钱、没有职业、没有工作、没有朋友、没有祖国的人？是的，这些人把他当人待——或者说，看见他时，没有把他当得低于人。可那又怎样？他们是白人。他要是讨一份工作呢？讨一个用脑子的机会呢？讨一位小姐做新娘呢？邻桌有个金发蓝眼的姑娘正吊他的眼风。呸！她是公开出售的货色，还当他是南美人、埃及人，或有钱的土王。他赶紧转开脸。
哦，他孤单；孤单，害着一场可怕的思乡病。说到底，离开白人的同时，他也离开了黑人的美国——离开了他爱过、识得的一切。天哪！他做梦也没想到，自己竟那样爱着那个温柔的棕色世界，那个被他那样漫不经心、毫无留恋地抛掉的世界。此刻若能握住一只黑手，听一句软软的南方腔，亲一亲一张棕色的脸颊，他什么都肯给。看一眼那温热的、棕色的小卷发和笑眼。天哪——他孤单。孤单得这样彻底、这样可怕。就在这时——他看见了那位公主！
此后许多许多年，他一次次想追回并重建那张脸在他心里激起的头一个狂乱的美妙幻象。他清楚，没有一个人能美得像她当时在他眼里的模样。他永远无法完全追回那幅图画最初的心醉，然而哪怕记忆本身，也回回叫他战栗、叫他复活。自那一眼之后，他和世界都不再是从前的他和世界了。
最先、最主要来的是那种颜色的感觉：在这个淡黄淡粉的羊皮纸的世界里，在这个颜色的缺席与否定的世界里，骤然来到一脉金棕色皮肤的辉光。它比阳光和黄金深；它比棕色浅、比棕色活。那是一种活着的、发亮的绯红，蒙在棕色肌肤底下。它不需要光，也吃不得影，只凭自己内里的光泽柔和地亮着。
随后来的是那女子本身的感觉：她年轻，坐着也看得出高，通身带着一种异样尊贵的气派。她纤细裊娜，曲线优雅。她没有看他，目光越过他，投向那条喧腾挣扎的街；那双眼睛是两泓夜——流动、透明、摄人的深——它们的亮使她的脸成了一片辉耀、一场梦。
马修振作起来，想理智地行事。

===Original===

Here﻿—here in Berlin and but a few tables away, actually sat a radiantly beautiful woman, and she was colored. He could see the faultlessness of her dress. There was a hint of something foreign and exotic in her simply draped gown of rich, creamlike silken stuff and in the graceful coil of her handfashioned turban. Her gloves were hung carelessly over her arm, and he caught a glimpse of slender-heeled slippers and sheer clinging hosiery. There was a flash of jewels on her hands and a murmur of beads in half-hidden necklaces. His young enthusiasm might overpaint and idealize her, but to the dullest and the oldest she was beautiful, beautiful. Who was she? What was she? How came this princess (for in some sense she must be royal) here in Berlin? Was she American? And how was he﻿—
Then he became conscious that he had been listening to words spoken behind him. He caught a slap of American English from the terrace just back and beyond.
“Look, there’s that darky again. See her? Sitting over yonder by the post. Ain’t she some pippin? What? Get out! Listen! Bet you a ten-spot I get her number before she leaves this café. You’re on! I know niggers, and I don’t mean perhaps. Ain’t I white. Watch my smoke!”
Matthew gripped the table. All that cold rage which still lay like lead beneath his heart began again to glow and burn. Action, action, it screamed﻿—no running and sulking now﻿—action! There was murder in his mind﻿—murder, riot, and arson. He wanted just once to hit this white American in the jaw﻿—to see him spinning over the tables, and then to walk out with his arm about the princess, through the midst of a gaping, scurrying white throng. He started to rise, and nearly upset his coffee cup.
Then he came to himself. No﻿—no. That would not do. Surely the fellow would not insult the girl. He could count on no public opinion in Berlin as in New York to shield him in such an adventure. He would simply seek to force his company on her in quite a natural way. After all, the café was filling. There were no empty tables, at least in the forward part of the room, and no one person had a right to a whole table; yet to approach any woman thus, when several tables with men offered seats, was to make a subtle advance; and to approach this woman?﻿—puzzled and apprehensive, Matthew sat quietly and watched while he paid his waiter and slowly pulled on his gloves.

===Chinese===

这儿——这儿在柏林，隔着不过几张桌子，实实在在坐着一位光艳照人的女人，而且她是有色人。他看得出她衣着的无瑕。她那身简单地披垂的、乳色丝料的袍子，和那手工缠出的头巾优雅的盘绕，透着一点异域的、奇珍的意味。她的手套随手搭在臂上；他瞥见细跟的缎鞋和薄薄贴肤的丝袜。她手上珠宝一闪，半掩的项链里珠子低语。他年轻的热忱也许把她涂得太美、太理想；可即便对最迟钝、最老成的人，她也美，美。
她是谁？她是什么人？这位公主（从某种意义上说，她必定是王室）怎么会来到柏林？她是美国人吗？而他怎么——
随后他察觉，自己一直在听背后说的话。露台那头，紧贴着再过去一点，甩来一串美国英语。
“瞧，那个黑丫头又来了。看见没有？柱子那边坐着的。够不够俏？什么？去你的！听着！赌十块钱，她出这咖啡馆之前我准拿到她的号码。一言为定！我懂黑鬼，不带含糊的。我不白吗？瞧我的手段！”
马修攋住桌沿。那仍像铅一样沉在心口的冰冷的怒火，重新烧了起来。行动，行动，它尖喊着——这回不许跑、不许闷——行动！他心里有谋杀——谋杀、暴乱和纵火。他只想捾这个美国白人一拳，正中下巴——看他打着旋儿飞过一张张桌子——然后搂着公主走出去，穿过一群张着嘴、抱头鼠窜的白人。他起身要站，几乎带翻了咖啡杯。
随后他清醒过来。不——不。那不行。那家伙多半不至于当面侮辱姑娘。在柏林，他指望不上纽约那种在这样一场历险里护着他的舆论。那家伙只会用一种十分“自然”的方式硬缠上她。再说，咖啡馆正坐满。没有空桌了，至少前面没有；谁也无权独占一桌；可是这样去凑一个女人的桌子——旁边明明有几张男人同桌的空位——是一种微妙的进犯；而去凑*这个*女人？——马修又困惑又提防，静静坐着，看着，一面付了侍者的账，慢慢戴好手套。

===Original===

He saw a young, smooth-faced American circle carelessly from behind him and saunter toward the door. Then he stopped, and turning, slowly came back toward the girl’s table. A cold sweat broke out over Matthew. A sickening fear fought with the fury in his heart. Suppose this girl, this beautiful girl, let the fresh American sit down and talk to her? Suppose? After all, who﻿—what was she? To sit alone at a table in a European café﻿—well, Matthew watched. The American approached, paused, looked about the café, and halted beside her table. He looked down and bowed, with his hand on the back of the empty chair.
The lady did not start nor speak. She glanced at him indifferently, unclasped her hands slowly, and then with no haste gathered up her things; she nodded to the waiter, fumbled in her purse, and without another glance at the American, arose and passed slowly out. Matthew could have shouted.
But the American was not easily rebuffed by this show of indifference. Apparently he interpreted the movement quite another way. Waving covertly to his fellows, he arose leisurely, without ordering, tossed a bill to the waiter, and sauntered out after the lady. Matthew rose impetuously, and he felt that whole terrace table of men arise behind him.
The dark lady had left by the Friedrichstrasse door, and paused for the taxi which the gold-laced porter had summoned. She gave an address and already had her foot on the step. In a moment the American was by her side. Deftly he displaced the porter and bent with lifted hat. She turned on him in surprise and raised her little head. Still the American persisted with a smile, but his hand had hardly touched her elbow when Matthew’s fist caught him right between the smile and the ear. The American sat down on the sidewalk very suddenly.
Pedestrians paused. There was a commotion at the restaurant door as several men rushed out, but the imposing porter was too quick; he had caught the eye and pocketed the bill of the lady. In a moment, evidently thinking the couple together, he had handed both her and Matthew into the taxi, slammed the door, and they had whirled away. In a trice they fled down the Friedrichstrasse, left across the Französische, again left to Charlotten, and down the Linden. Matthew glanced anxiously back. They had been too quick, and there was apparently no pursuit.

===Chinese===

他看见一个脸庞光洁的年轻美国人，从自己背后满不经心地兒过来，朝门口闲逛过去。随后他停住，一转身，慢慢朝姑娘的桌子走回来。马修出了一身冷汗。一股作呕的恐惧，同他心里的狂怒打了起来。万一这位姑娘，这位美丽的姑娘，让这个新潮的美国人坐下攀谈呢？万一？说到底，她是谁——是什么人？独自坐在欧洲一家咖啡馆的桌旁——唉，马修盯着看。那美国人走近了，一停，朝咖啡馆四下望了望，在她的桌边站定。他低头一躬，手搭在空椅背上。
那位女士既没有一惊，也没有说话。她淡漠地瞥了他一眼，慢慢松开交握的双手，随后不慌不忙收拾起自己的东西；朝侍者点了点头，在钱包里摸出钱来，对那美国人再看都不看一眼，起身，缓缓走了出去。马修简直要欢呼。
可这一副冷淡没有轻易打退那美国人。显然他把这一动解读成了另一层意思。他朝同伙偷偷一挥手，慢条斯理起身，什么也不点，扔给侍者一张票子，跟着那位女士闲逛出去。马修腾地站起，觉得背后露台上那一整桌男人也跟着站了起来。
黑衣女士是从腓特烈大街那边的门走的，在镶金绦的门房唤来的出租汽车前站了站。她报了个地址，一只脚已经踏上车阶。转眼间那美国人已到她身边。他麻利地把门房挤开，举着帽子弯下腰去。她诧异地朝他一转，昂起她小小的头。那美国人还挂着笑纠缠，可他的手刚碰着她胳膊肘，马修的拳头正打在他那笑和耳朵之间。那美国人非常突然地坐到了人行道上。
行人驻足。餐馆门口一阵骚动，冲出来几个男人，可那位气派的门房手更快；他接住了女士的眼色，把票子收进了口袋。转瞬间——显然把这一对当成了同路——他把她和马修都塞进了汽车，砰地关上门，车已经飞出去了。一眨眼他们顺腓特烈大街逃下去，在法国人街左拐，再左拐上夏洛滕堡街，又拐下林登大道。马修焦急地回头望。他们动作太快，显然没有人追。

===Original===

He leaned forward and spoke to the chauffeur, and they drove up to the curb near the Brandenburg Gate and stopped.
“Mille remerciements, Monsieur!” said the lady.
Matthew searched his head for the right French answer as he started to step out, but could not remember: “Oh﻿—oh﻿—don’t mention it,” he stammered.
“Ah﻿—you are English? I thought you were French or Spanish!”
She spoke clear clipped English with perfect accent, to Matthew’s intense relief. Suppose she had spoken only French! He hastened to explain: “I am an American Negro.”
“An American Negro?” The lady bent forward in sudden interest and stared at him. “An American Negro!” she repeated. “How singular﻿—how very singular! I have been thinking of American Negroes all day! Please do not leave me yet. Can you spare a moment? Chauffeur, drive on!”
IV
As they sat at tea in the Tiergarten, under the tall black trees, Matthew’s story came pouring out:
“I was born in Virginia, Prince James County, where we black folk own most of the land. My mother, now many years a widow, farmed her little forty acres to educate me, her only child. There was a good school there with teachers from Hampton, the great boarding-school not far away. I was young when I finished the course and was sent to Hampton. There I was unhappy. I wanted to study for a profession, and they insisted on making me a farmer. I hated the farm. My mother finally sent me North. I boarded first with a cousin and then with friends in New York and went through high school and through the City College. I specialized on the premedical course, and by working nights and summers and playing football (amateur, of course, but paid excellent ‘expenses’ in fact), I was able to enter the new great medical school of the University of Manhattan, two years ago.
“It was a hard pull, but I plunged the line. I had to have scholarships, and I got them, although one Southern professor gave me the devil of a time.”
The lady interrupted. “Southern?” she asked. “What do you mean by ‘Southern’?”
“I mean from the former slave States﻿—although the phrase isn’t just fair. Some of our most professional Southerners are Northern-born.”
The lady still looked puzzled, but Matthew talked on.
“This man didn’t mean to be unfair, but he honestly didn’t believe ‘niggers’ had brains, even if he had the evidence before him. He flunked me on principle.

===Chinese===

他朝前欠身，同司机说了句什么，车靠到勃兰登堡门附近的道边，停了。
“多谢了，先生！”女士说。
马修一边下车一边在脑袋里搜那句正确的法语答词，怎么也想不起来：“哦——哦——不值一提。”他结结巴巴地说。
“啊——您是英国人？我当您是法国人或者西班牙人呢！”
她说一口清晰利落的英语，口音无懈可击，马修大大松了口气。万一她只说法语呢！他赶紧解释：“我是美国黑人。”
“美国黑人？”女士兴致陡生，俯身盯着他。“美国黑人！”她重复道。“多奇特——多奇特呀！我正想了一整天美国黑人！请先别走。能赏光片刻吗？司机，开车！”

四
两人坐在蒂尔加滕高大黑黢黢的树下喝茶，马修的故事倾泻而出：
“我生在弗吉尼亚，詹姆斯王子县，我们黑人拥有那里大部分土地。我母亲守寡多年，种着她那四十英亩小农场，供我这个独子读书。那儿有一所好学校，老师出自汉普顿——不远的那所了不起的寄宿学校。我读完那边的课程时年纪还小，就被送进了汉普顿。我在那儿不快活。我想学一门专业，他们非要把我造就成农夫。我恨农场。母亲末了把我送到北方。我先住在一个表亲家，后来住在纽约的朋友家，读完了中学和城市学院。我专攻医预科，靠夜里和暑期打工，再加上踢橄榄球（当然名义上是业余，其实‘补贴’给得很优厚），两年前考进了曼哈顿大学新办的大医学院。
“那是一场苦拔，可我突了线。我必须拿奖学金，我拿到了，尽管一个南方教授把我折磨了个半死。”
女士插进来。“南方？”她问，“‘南方’是什么意思？”
“我是说从前蓄奴的那几个州——虽然这话也不全公平。我们有些顶顶职业气的南方人，倒是北方生的。”
女士还是一脸困惑，可马修接着讲。
“这个人不算存心不公，可他真心不信‘黑鬼’有脑子，证据摆在眼前也不信。他按原则给我打不及格。

===Original===

I protested and finally had the matter up to the Dean; I showed all my other marks and got a reexamination at an extra cost that deprived me of a new overcoat. I gave him a perfect paper, and he had to acknowledge it was ‘good,’ although he made careful inquiries to see if I had not in some way cribbed dishonestly.
“At last I got my mark and my scholarship. During my second year there were rumors among the few colored students in the clinical hospital, especially with white women patients. I laughed. It was, I was sure, a put-up rumor to scare us off. I knew black men who had gone through New York medical schools which had become parts of this great new consolidated school. There had been no real trouble. The patients never objected﻿—only Southern students and the few Southern professors. Some of the trustees had mentioned the matter but had been shamed into silence.
“Then, too, I was firm in my Hampton training; desert and hard work were bound to tell. Prejudice was a miasma that character burned away. I believed thoroughly. I had literally pounded my triumphant way through school and life. Of course I had made insult and rebuff here and there, but I ignored them, laughed at them, and went my way. Those black people who cringed and cowered, complained of failure and ‘no chance,’ I despised﻿—weaklings, cowards, fool! Go to work! Make a way! Compel recognition!
“In the medical school there were two other colored men just managing to crawl through. I covertly sneered at them, avoided them. What business had they there with no ability or training? I see differently now. I see there may have been a dozen reasons why Phillips of Mississippi could neither spell nor read correctly and why Jones of Georgia could not count. They had no hardworking mother, no Hampton, no happy accidents of fortune to help them on.
“While I? I rose to triumph after triumph. Just as in college I had been the leading athlete and had ridden many a time aloft on white students’ shoulders, so now, working until two o’clock in the morning and rising at six, I took prize after prize﻿—the Mitchel Honor in physiology, the Welbright medal in pathology, the Shores Prize for biological chemistry. I ranked the second-year class at last commencement, and at our annual dinner at the Hotel Pennsylvania, sat at the head table with the medal men. I remember one classmate.

===Chinese===

我抗议，末了把事情闹到院长那里；我摆出全部其他成绩，换得一次复试，额外的一笔费用剥夺了我的一件新大衣。我交了一份满分的卷子，他不得不承认‘不错’，虽然他细细盘查了一番，看我有没有用什么法子作弊。
“我终于拿到了成绩和奖学金。第二年，临床医院里我们寥寥几个有色学生中间起了谣言，尤其是关于白人女病人。我一笑置之。我断定，那是一场编好了吓退我们的谣言。我认得一些黑人，读过纽约的几所医学院——如今都并进了这座新的大联合学院——从没真出过事。病人从来不反对——只有南方学生和寥寥几个南方教授。有几位董事提过这事，也被说得没了声。
“再说，我对自己受的汉普顿训练深信不疑：苦干和实干必定见效。偏见不过是一层瘴气，品格自会把它烧光。我彻头彻尾地信。我简直是横冲直撞地打穿了我的学业和人生。当然，侮辱和冷遇哪里也挨过几下，可我不理，一笑走开，走我的路。那些缩头缩脑、哭哭啼啼抱怨失败和‘没有机会’的黑人，我看不起——软骨头，胆小鬼，蠢货！去做工！杀出一条路！逼人家承认你！
“医学院里另外还有两个有色人，勉强爬着挨。我暗地里讥笑他们，躲着他们。没本事没训练，跑那儿去做什么？如今我另眼看了。我明白，密西西比的菲利普斯为什么拼也拼不对、读也读不顺，佐治亚的琼斯为什么连数也数不清，道理可以数出一打。他们没有拼死的母亲，没有汉普顿，没有好运的照拂。
“而我呢？我胜利接着胜利。正像大学里我是首席运动员、多少回被白人同学扛在肩头，如今我干到凌晨两点、六点起床，奖一个接一个地拿——生理学的米切尔荣誉奖，病理学的韦尔布莱特奖章，生物化学的肖尔斯奖。上届毕业典礼，我在二年级名列第一；在宾夕法尼亚饭店的年宴上，同得奖章的人们同坐主桌。我记得一位同学。

===Original===

He was from Atlanta, and he hesitated and whispered when he found his seat was beside me. Then he sat down like a man and held out his hand. ‘Towns,’ he said, ‘I never associated with a Negro before who wasn’t a servant or laborer; but I’ve heard of you, and you’ve made a damned fine record. I’m proud to sit by you.’
“I shook his hand and choked. He proved my life-theory. Character and brains were too much for prejudice. Then the blow fell. I had slaved all summer. I was worked to a frazzle. Reckon my hard-headedness had a hand there, too. I wouldn’t take a menial job﻿—Pullman porter, waiter, bellboy, boat steward﻿—good money, but I waved them aside. No! Bad for the soul, and I might meet a white fellow student.”
The lady smiled. “Meet a fellow student﻿—did none of them work, too?”
“O yes, but seldom as menials, while Negroes in America are always expected to be menials. It’s natural, but﻿—no, I couldn’t do it. So at last I got a job in Washington in the medical statistics department of the National Benefit. This is one of our big insurance concerns. O yes, we’ve got a number of them; prosperous, too. It was hard work, indoors, poor light and air; but I was interested﻿—worked overtime, learned the game, and gave my thought and ideas.
“They promoted me and paid me well, and by the middle of August I had my tuition and book money saved. They wanted me to stay with them permanently; at least until fall. But I had other plans. There was a summer school of two terms at the college, and I figured that if I entered the second term I could get a big lead in my obstetrical work and stand a better show for the Junior prizes. I had applied in the spring for admission to the Stern Maternity Hospital, which occupied three floors of our center building. My name had been posted as accepted. I was tired to death, but I rushed back to New York to register. Perhaps if I had been rested, with cool head and nerves﻿—well, I wasn’t. I made the office of the professor of obstetrics on a hot afternoon, August 10, I well remember. He looked at me in surprise.
“ ‘You can’t work in the Stern Hospital﻿—the places are all taken.’
“ ‘I have one of the places,’ I pointed out. He seemed puzzled and annoyed.
“ ‘You’ll have to see the Dean,’ he said finally.
“I was angry and rushed to the Dean’s office. I saw that we had a new Dean﻿—a Southerner.
“Then the blow fell.

===Chinese===

他是亚特兰大人，发现自己的座位在我旁边时，迟疑了一阵，压低了嗓门。随后他像条汉子似的坐下，伸出手来。‘唐斯，’他说，‘从前我接触的黑人，不是仆人就是苦力；可你的事我听说了，你的成绩漂亮极了。同你坐在一起，我引以为荣。’
“我握住他的手，喉咙发哽。他证明了我的人生理论。品格加头脑，偏见挡不住。随后打击落下来了。我苦干了一整个夏天。我累得脱了形。如今想来，我的死心眼也有一份。下等的活计我不肯干——普尔曼卧车侍应生（Pullman porter）、侍者、行李生、船上管事——钱不少，可我一挥手挡开。不干！糟蹋灵魂，再说兴许撞见白人同学。”
女士微微一笑。“撞见同学——难道白人同学就不打工？”
“打，可难得当下人；而美国的黑人，人家眼里天生就该当下人。这很自然，可是——不，我干不来。所以末了我在华盛顿的‘国家益友’保险行的医学统计科找了份差事。这是我们有数的大保险行之一。哦是的，我们有好几家；还兴旺。活儿辛苦，闷在屋里，光线空气都坏；可我有兴致——加班加点，摸熟了门道，把我的想法和主意都搭了进去。
“他们提拔我，给钱也厚；到八月中旬，学费书钱都攒齐了。他们要我留下长干；至少干到秋天。可我另有盘算。学院办两期暑期班，我算计着，只要插第二期，产科就能抢个大先手，三年级的奖金也更有把握。春天我就申请了斯特恩产科医院——就在我们中心楼里占了三层。我的名字已经登出去了：录取。我累得要死，还是赶回了纽约去注册。也许，换个人歇足了、头脑冷静、神经平稳——唉，我没有。八月十日，一个炎热的下午，我摸到产科教授的办公室，我记得清清楚楚。他惊讶地看着我。
“‘斯特恩医院你不能去——名额全满了。’
“‘我占着其中一个名额。’我指出来。他显得又困惑又不耐烦。
“‘你只能去见院长。’他末了说。
“我冒火冲进院长办公室。我发现我们换了新院长——一个南方人。
“随后打击落了下来。

===Original===

Seemingly, during the summer the trustees had decided gradually to exclude Negroes from the college. In the case of students already in the course, they were to be kept from graduation by a refusal to admit them a certain course, particularly in obstetrics. The Dean was to break the news by letter as students applied for the course, I had been accepted before the decision; so now he had to tell me. He hated the task, I could see. But I was too surprised, disgusted, furious. He said that I could not enter, and he told me brutally why. I threw my papers in his face and left. All my fine theories of race and prejudice lay in ruins. My life was overturned. America was impossible﻿—unthinkable. I ran away, and here I am.”
V
They had sat an hour drinking tea in the Tiergarten, that mightiest park in Europe with its lofty trees, its cool dark shade, its sense of withdrawal from the world. He had not meant to be so voluble, so self-revealing. Perhaps the lady had deftly encouraged confidences in her high, but gracious way. Perhaps the mere sight of her smooth brown skin had made Matthew assume sympathy. There was something at once inviting and aloof in the young woman who sat opposite him. She had the air and carriage of one used to homage and yet receiving it indifferently as a right. With all her gentle manner and thoughtfulness, she had a certain fainty air of haughtiness and was ever slightly remote.
She was “colored” and yet not at all colored in his intimate sense. Her beauty as he saw it near had seemed even more striking; those thin, smooth fingers moving about the silver had known no work; she was carefully groomed from her purple hair to her slim toe-tips, and yet with few accessories; he could not tell whether she used paint or powder. Her features were regular and delicate, and there was a tiny diamond in one nostril. But quite aside from all the details of face and jewels﻿—her pearls, her rings, the old gold bracelet﻿—above and beyond and much more than the sum of them all was the luminous radiance of her complete beauty, her glow of youth and strength behind that screen of a grand yet gracious matter. It was overpowering for Matthew, and yet stimulating. So his story came pouring out before he knew or cared to whom he was speaking. All the loneliness of long, lonely days clamored for speech, all the pent-up resentment choked for words.

===Chinese===

看样子，董事们夏天里作了决定：逐步把黑人从学院里排出去。对已在读的学生，就用拒绝让其修某一门课——尤其是产科——来卡住他们毕业。院长打算在学生逐个来申请那门课的时候，用信函一个个通知。我是在决定之前录取的；所以这会儿他只好当面告诉我。他恨这差事，我看得出来。可我太惊讶、太恶心、太狂怒了。他说我不能入学，又把原因讲得赤裸裸。我把文件摔在他脸上，走了。我那一整套关于种族和偏见的漂亮理论塌成了一堆瓦砾。我的人生翻了个底朝天。美国没法待了——想都不能想。我逃了出来，就到了今天这步。”

五
他们在蒂尔加滕坐了一个钟头喝茶——欧洲顶大的园子，高树，凉而黑的浓荫，一种退出人间的味道。他本没打算这样滔滔不绝、这样自剖。也许是女士以她那高华而亲切的路数，巧妙地引人倾吐。也许仅仅看见她那光洁的棕色皮肤，就叫马修认下了同情。坐在他对面的这个年轻女子，身上有种又相邀、又疏远的东西。她有一副受惯了敬奉的神采和举止，又把敬奉当作理所当然，受得淡然。纵然她举止温存、心思周到，也带一点淡淡的高傲，永远稍稍隔着一层。
她“有色”，却又完全不是他切身意义里的有色。近处看，她的美显得越发惊人；那几根纤细光洁、在银器间移动的手指，不识劳作；她从紫色的头发到纤细的足尖，处处拾掇得精致，却又几乎不带饰物；他看不出她搽没搽脂粉。她五官端正而纤巧，一只鼻孔里嵌着一粒小小的钻石。可全然撇开脸和珠宝的种种细节——她的珍珠、戒指、那只古金镯——在那之上、之外、远多于这一切总和的，是她整个美的那种通体的光辉，是那副华贵而雍容的屏障后头，青春与力量的光焰。这对马修是压倒的，又是提神的。于是他的故事倾泻而出，顾不得、也不在乎对面坐的是谁。漫长孤寂的日子的全部孤独都嚷着要说话，郁积的全部愤懑都哽着喉咙找词。

===Original===

The lady listened at first with polite but conscious sympathy; then she bent forward more and more eagerly, but always with restraint, with that mastery of body and soul that never for a moment slipped away, and yet with so evident a sympathy and comprehension that it set Matthew’s head swimming. She swept him almost imperiously with her eyes﻿—those great wide orbs of darkening light. His own eyes lifted and fell before them; lifted and fell, until at last he looked past them and talked to the tall green and black oaks.
And yet there was never anything personal in her all-sweeping glance or anything self-conscious in the form that bent toward him. She never seemed in the slightest way conscious of herself. She arranged nothing, glanced at no detail of her dress, smoothed no wisp of hair. She seemed at once unconscious of her beauty and charm, and at the same time assuming it as a fact, but of no especial importance. She had no little feminine ways; she used her eyes apparently only for seeing, yet seemed to see all.
Matthew had the feeling that her steady, full, radiant gaze that enveloped and almost burned him, saw not him but the picture he was painting and the thing that the picture meant. He warmed with such an audience and painted with clean, sure lines. Only once or twice did she interrupt, and when he had ended, she still sat full-faced, flooding him with the startling beauty of her eyes. Her hands clasped and unclasped slowly, her lips were slightly parted, the curve of her young bosom rose and fell.
“And you ran away!” she said musingly. Matthew winced and started to explain, but she continued. “Singular,” she said. “How singular that I should meet you; and today.” There was no coquetry in her tone. It was evidently not of him, the hero, of whom she was thinking, but of him, the group, the fact, the whole drama.
“And you are two﻿—three millions?” she asked.
“Ten or twelve,” he answered.
“You ran away,” she repeated, half in meditation.
“What else could I do?” he demanded impulsively. “Cringe and crawl?”
“Of course the Negroes have no hospitals?”
“Of course, they have﻿—many, but not attached to the great schools. What can Howard (rated as our best colored school) do with thousands, when whites have millions? And if we come out poorly taught and half equipped, they sneer at ‘nigger’ doctors.”

===Chinese===

女士起初听得礼貌而有分寸；随后越俯越前，却始终收着，身心那副从不滑脱一分的主宰，可那份同情与理会又那样明显，听得马修头发晕。她差不多专断地用眼睛把他从头到脚扫过——那两泓渐渐沉暗的光的大眼睛。他自己的眼睛在它们面前起了又落，起了又落，末了干脆越过它们，对着高大的绿黑色的橡树讲去。
然而她那通览一切的凝视里绝无一点个人意味，朝他俯近的形体里也绝无一点自觉。她似乎丝毫意识不到自己。她不整理什么，不瞟一眼衣饰，不抚一缕头发。她仿佛同时既不觉得自己的美与魅力，又把它当作一件事实——一件没什么特别分量的事实。她没有那些小小的女儿态；她那双眼睛显然只拿来看，却仿佛什么都看见了。
马修觉得，她那稳稳的、饱满的、放光的凝视——裹住他、几乎灼痛他的凝视——看的不是他，而是他正在画的那幅画，和那幅画意味着的东西。有这样的听众，他浑身发热，笔下线条干净而笃定。她只插过一两次话；等他讲完，她仍端端正正坐着，任那双眼睛惊心动魄的美朝他倾泻。她的手慢慢交握又松开，嘴唇微微启着，年轻的胸脯的曲线起起伏伏。
“而您逃跑了！”她若有所思地说。马修一缩，想解释，可她接着说。“奇特，”她说，“多奇特，我竟遇见了您；偏偏在今天。”她的语气里没有一点卖弄。她想着的显然不是他这个男主角，而是他这个群类、这个事实、整部戏。
“你们是两百万——三百万？”她问。
“一千万到一千二百万。”他答。
“您逃跑了。”她半自语地重复。
“不逃又能怎样？”他冲动地质问。“跪着爬吗？”
“黑人自然没有医院？”
“自然有——不少，可不挂靠那些大学府。汉普顿（要算我们最好的有色学府）拿几千人有什么办法，白人那边是几百万？我们出来时学艺不精、装备不全，人家就讥笑‘黑鬼’医生。”

===Original===

“And no Negroes are admitted to the hospitals of New York?”
“O yes hundreds. But if we colored students are confined to colored patients, we surrender a principle.”
“What principle?”
“Equality.”
“Oh﻿—equality.”
She sat for a full moment, frowning and looking at him. Then she fumbled away at her beads and brought out a tiny jeweled box. Absently she took out a cigarette, lighted it, and offered him one. Matthew took it, but he was a little troubled. White women in his experience smoked of course﻿—but colored women? Well﻿—but it was delicious to see her great, somber eyes veiled in hazy blue.
She sighed at last and said: “I do not quite understand. But at any rate I see that you American Negroes are not a mere amorphous handful. You are a nation! I never dreamed﻿—But I must explain. I want you to dine with me and some friends tomorrow night at my apartment. We represent﻿—indeed I may say frankly, we are﻿—a part of a great committee of the darker peoples; of those who suffer under the arrogance and tyranny of the white world.”
Matthew leaned forward with an eager thrill. “And you have plans? Some vast emancipation of the world?”
She did not answer directly, but continued: “We have among us spokesmen of nearly all these groups﻿—of them or for them﻿—except American Negroes. Some of us think these former slaves unready for cooperation, but I just returned from Moscow last week. At our last dinner I was telling of a report I read there from America that astounded me and gave me great pleasure﻿—for I almost alone have insisted that your group was worthy of cooperation. In Russia I heard something, and it happened so curiously that﻿—after sharp discussion about your people but last night (for I will not conceal from you that there is still doubt and opposition in our ranks)﻿—that I should meet you today.
“I had gone up to the palace to see the exhibition of the new paintings﻿—you have not seen it? You must. All the time I was thinking absently of Black America, and one picture there intensified and stirred my thoughts﻿—a weird massing of black shepherds and a star. I dropped into the Viktoria, almost unconsciously, because the tea there is good and the muffins quite unequaled.

===Chinese===

“纽约的医院也不收黑人？”
“哦收，成百地收。可我们有色学生要是只许看有色病人，那我们交出去的是一条原则。”
“什么原则？”
“平等。”
“哦——平等。”
她坐了足足一刻，皱着眉望他。随后她摸着念珠，摸出一只小小的珠宝宝盒。心不在焉地取出一支烟，点上，又敬他一支。马修接了，心里却有点打鼓。他经验里的白人女人当然抽烟——可有色女人？唉——不过，看她那双大而幽暗的眼睛蒙进蓝色的雾里，实在好看。
她终于叹了口气，说：“我不很明白。可无论如何我看出：你们美国黑人不是一把散沙。你们是一个民族！我做梦也——可我必须说明。我想请你明晚同几位朋友到我的公寓来吃饭。我们代表着——不妨坦白说，我们就是——有色人种大委员会的一部分；那些在白色世界的傲慢与暴虐底下受苦的人们的一部分。”
马修激动得一阵战栗，朝前俯过去。“你们有计划？某种解放世界的大业？”
她没有直接回答，接着说：“我们中间几乎有所有这些群体的代言人——为他们、或代表他们——唯独没有美国黑人。我们有些人认为这批从前的奴隶还谈不上合作；可我上礼拜刚从莫斯科回来。上一次晚宴上，我正讲到我在那边读到的一份来自美国的报告，它叫我吃惊，也叫我大为高兴——因为差不多只有我一个人坚持，你们这个群体配得上合作。在俄国我听到一些事，而事情巧到——昨晚我们还为你们的人激烈争论过（我不瞒你，我们内部还有怀疑和反对）——今天我就遇见了你。
“我上宫里去看新画展了——你没看过？一定要去。我一路心不在焉地想着黑人美国，那儿有一幅画把我的思绪撩得更旺——一群黑牧人的诡异的聚合，和一颗星。我信步落进维多利亚咖啡馆，几乎是无意识的，因为那儿的茶好，小圆饼更是没得比。

===Original===

I know that I should not go there unaccompanied, even in the day; white women may, but brown women seem strangely attractive to white men, especially Americans; and this is the open season for them.
“Twice before I have had to put Americans in their place. I went quite unconsciously and noted nothing in particular until that impossible young man sat down at my table. I did not know he had followed me out. Then you knocked him into the gutter quite beautifully. It had never happened before that a stranger of my own color would offer me protection in Europe. I had a curious sense of some great inner meaning to your act﻿—some world involvement. It seemed almost that the Powers of Heaven had bent to give me the knowledge which I was groping for; and so I invited you, that I might hear and know more.”
She rose, insisted on paying the bill herself. “You are my guest, you see. It is late and I must go. Then, tomorrow night at eight. My card and address﻿—Oh, I quite forgot. May I know your name?”
Matthew had no card. But he wrote in her tiny memorandum book with its golden filigree, “Matthew Towns, Exile, Hotel Roter Adler.”
She held out her hand, half turning to go. Her slenderness made her look taller than she was. The curved line of her flowed sinuously from neck to ankle. She held her right hand high, palm down, the long fingers drooping, and a ruby flamed dark crimson on her forefinger. Matthew reached up and shook her hand heartily. He had, as he did it, a vague feeling that he took her by surprise. Perhaps he shook hands too hard, for her hand was very little and frail. Perhaps she did not mean to shake hands﻿—but then, what did she mean?
She was gone. He took her card and read it. There was a little coronet and under it, engraved in flowing script, “H.R.H. the Princess Kautilya of Bwodpur, India.” Below was written, “Lützower Ufer, No. 12.”
VI
Matthew sat in the dining-room of the Princess on Lützower Ufer. Looking about, his heart swelled. For the first time since he had left New York, he felt himself a man, one of those who could help build a world and guide it. He had no regrets. Medicine seemed a far-off, dry-as-dust thing.
The oak paneling of the room went to the ceiling and there broke softly with carven light against white flowers and into long lucent curves. The table below was sheer with lace and linen, sparkling with silver and crystal.

===Chinese===

我知道我不该一个人去，哪怕在白天；白人女人可以，棕色女人对白种男人似乎有种奇怪的吸引力，尤其对美国人；如今又正是他们的猎季。
“从前有过两回，我不得不把美国人放回他们该在的位置。我去的时候全无意识，也没留意什么，直到那个没法形容的年轻人在我桌边坐下。我不知道他跟了出来。随后你把他那么漂亮地打进了水沟。在欧洲，同肤色的人向一位陌生女子伸出保护，这在我还是头一回。你的举动叫我生出一种奇怪的感觉，仿佛内里藏着什么重大的意义——牵动着整个世界。几乎像是天上的权柄俯身下来，把我正摸索的那点知识递给了我；于是我邀了你，好多听一些、多知道一些。”
她起身，坚持自己付账。“你是我的客人，明白吗。时候不早，我该走了。那么，明晚八点。我的名片和地址——哦，差一点忘了。可否请教尊姓大名？”
马修没有名片。他在她那本金丝细工的小记事簿上写下：“马修·唐斯，流亡者，红鹰旅馆。”
她伸出手来，半侧着身要走。她的纤细使她显得比实际更高。她的曲线从脖颈到脚踝蜿蜒地流下去。她把右手高高伸出，掌心向下，长长的手指垂着，食指上一枚红宝石烧着暗紫的红。马修抬手握住，握得热热乎乎。握的时候他隐隐觉得她吃了一惊。兴许他握得太重，她的手那样小、那样脆。兴许她本没有握手的意思——可那样的话，她是什么意思？
她走了。他拿起她的名片读。上面有一顶小小的冠，底下是花体的刻字：“印度布沃德普尔（Bwodpur）考蒂利亚公主殿下。”下面一行手书：“吕措河岸十二号。”

六
马修坐在公主吕措河岸宅邸的餐厅里。环顾四周，他的心涨起来。离开纽约以来，他头一回觉得自己是个人物，是那些能帮忙造一个世界、掌一个世界舵的人里的一个。他没有悔意。医学显得是一件遥远而枯燥如尘的东西。
餐厅的橡木镶板直上屋顶，在那里化作雕刻的柔光，破入白花，折成长长的明亮的曲线。下面的桌子素净地铺着蕾丝和亚麻，银器和水晶闪闪发亮。

===Original===

The servants moved deftly, and all of them were white save one who stood behind the Princess’ high and crimson chair. At her right sat Matthew himself, hardly realizing until long afterward the honor thus done an almost nameless guest.
Fortunately he had the dinner jacket of year before last with him. It was not new, but it fitted his form perfectly, and his was a form worth fitting. He was a bit shocked to note that all the other men but two were in full evening dress. But he did not let this worry him much.
Ten of them sat at the table. On the Princess’ left was a Japanese, faultless in dress and manner, evidently a man of importance, as the deference shown him and the orders on his breast indicated. He was quite yellow, short and stocky, with a face which was a delicately handled but perfect mask. There were two Indians, one a man grave, haughty, and old, dressed richly in turban and embroidered tunic, the other, in conventional dress and turban, a young man, handsome and alert, whose eyes were ever on the Princess. There were two Chinese, a young man and a young woman, he in a plain but becoming Chinese costume of heavy blue silk, she in a pretty dress, half Chinese, half European in effect. An Egyptian and his wife came next, he suave, talkative, and polite﻿—just a shade too talkative and a bit too polite, Matthew thought; his wife a big, handsome, silent woman, elegantly jeweled and gowned, with much bare flesh. Beyond them was a cold and rather stiff Arab who spoke seldom, and then abruptly.
Of the food and wine of such dinners, Matthew had read often but never partaken; and the conversation, now floating, now half submerged, gave baffling glimpses of unknown lands, spiritual and physical. It was all something quite new in his experience, the room, the table, the service, the company.
He could not keep his eyes from continually straying sidewise to his hostess. Never had he seen color in human flesh so regally set: the rich and flowing grace of the dress out of which rose so darkly splendid the jeweled flesh. The black and purple hair was heaped up on her little head, and in its depths gleamed a tiny coronet of gold. Her voice and her poise, her self-possession and air of quiet command, kept Matthew staring almost unmannerly, despite the fact that he somehow sensed a shade of resentment in the young and handsome Indian opposite.

===Chinese===

仆役们手脚轻捷，全是白人，只有一个立在公主那高背的深红座椅后面。她的右手边坐的就是马修——过了很久他才充分意识到，这对一个几乎无名的客人是怎样一份体面。
幸而他随身带着前年的晚餐上衣。不新，可裁得贴合他的身量，而他的身量值得贴合。他略略吃了一惊：除两人之外，别的男客全是全套晚礼服。可他没让这个多搅扰他。
席上共十位。公主左手边是一位日本人，衣着举止无懈可击，显然是位要人——众人对他的恭敬和他胸前的勋章都表明了。他相当黄，矮壮，一张脸是一件手法精到的、完美的面具。有两位印度人：一位庄重、傲慢、上了年纪，裹着头巾、穿着绣袍，衣饰华贵；另一位西装而戴头巾，是个年轻人，俊美而机警，眼睛不离公主。有两位中国人，一男一女：男的穿一身素净而得体的中国式蓝绸厚装，女的穿一条半中半洋的漂亮衣裙。接着是一对埃及夫妇，丈夫圆滑、健谈、客气——马修觉得，稍稍太健谈、有点太客气了；太太是位高大俊美而沉默的女人，珠翠罗裳，袒露颇多。再过去是一位冷淡而颇为僵硬的阿拉伯人，很少说话，一说就突兀。
这样的席面和酒，马修常读到，从未亲历；而席间的谈话，时而漂浮，时而半沉，把他难住，望见一片片未知的大陆，精神的和物质的都是。这一切在他都是全新：屋子，席面，仆役，这一席人。
他的眼睛管不住，一次次斜瞟向女主人。他从没见过人的肤色这样帝王般地安放：衣裳丰美流畅，从中升起那样黝黑华贵的、缀着珠宝的肌体。黑紫的头发堆在她小小的头顶，深处一点金冠的微光。她的嗓音和仪态，她的自持和静穆的号令之气，看得马修几乎失礼地瞪视——尽管他隐隐察觉，对面那位俊美的年轻印度人带了一丝不快。

===Original===

They had eaten some delicious tidbits of meat and vegetables and then were served with a delicate soup when the Princess, turning slightly to her right, said:
“You will note, Mr. Towns, that we represent here much of the Darker World. Indeed, when all our circle is present, we represent all of it, save your world of Black Folk.”
“All the darker world except the darkest,” said the Egyptian.
“A pretty large omission,” said Matthew with a smile.
“I agree,” said the Chinaman; but the Arab said something abruptly in French. Matthew had said that he knew “some” French. But his French was of the American variety which one excavates from dictionaries and cements with grammar, like bricks. He was astounded at the ease and the fluency with which most of this company used languages, so easily, without groping or hesitation and with light, sure shading. They talked art in French, literature in Italian, politics in German, and everything in clear English.
“M. Ben Ali suggests,” said the Princess, “that even you are not black, Mr. Towns.”
“My grandfather was, and my soul is. Black blood with us in America is a matter of spirit and not simply of flesh.”
“Ah! mixed blood,” said the Egyptian.
“Like all of us, especially me,” laughed the Princess.
“But, your Royal Highness﻿—not Negro,” said the elder Indian in a tone that hinted a protest.
“Essentially,” said the Princess lightly, “as our black and curly-haired Lord Buddha testifies in a hundred places. But”﻿—a bit imperiously﻿—“enough of that. Our point is that Pan-Africa belongs logically with Pan-Asia; and for that reason Mr. Towns is welcomed tonight by you, I am sure, and by me especially. He did me a service as I was returning from the New Palace.”
They all looked interested, but the Egyptian broke out:
“Ah, Your Highness, the New Palace, and what is the fad today? What has followed expressionism, cubism, futurism, vorticism? I confess myself at sea. Picasso alarms me. Matisse sets me aflame. But I do not understand them. I prefer the classics.”
“The Congo,” said the Princess, “is flooding the Acropolis. There is a beautiful Kandinsky on exhibit, and some lovely and startling things by unknown newcomers.”
“*Mais*,” replied the Egyptian, dropping into French﻿—and they were all off to the discussion, save the silent Egyptian woman and the taciturn Arab.
Here again Matthew was puzzled.

===Chinese===

几样精美的肉食和菜蔬用过，端上一道清鲜的汤，公主微微朝右一转，说：
“唐斯先生，您会注意到，我们在这里代表着黑暗世界的大部分。的确，等我们这一圈人到齐，我们是它的全部——只除了你们的黑人世界。”
“整个有色世界，只缺最黑的那一块。”埃及人说。
“漏的可不小。”马修笑着说。
“我同意。”中国人说；阿拉伯人却用法语突兀地说了句什么。马修方才说过他懂“一点”法语。可他的法语是美国那一路：从词典里挖出来、拿语法砌住的，像砖。他惊异于这一席人用语言的从容流利：那样轻易，不摸索，不迟疑，明暗拿捻得轻轻巧巧。他们用法语谈艺术，意大利语谈文学，德语谈政治，清清楚楚的英语谈一切。
“本·阿里先生的意思是，”公主说，“连您也不算黑人，唐斯先生。”
“我祖父是，我的灵魂是。在美国，黑人的血是灵魂的事，不只是皮肉的事。”
“啊！混血。”埃及人说。
“同我们大家一样，尤其同我。”公主笑道。
“可是，殿下——总不是黑奴之种。”年长的印度人说，口气里带着抗议的苗头。
“本质上正是，”公主轻轻地说，“我们那位黑而鬈发的佛陀陛下，在一百处经文里作证。不过——”略带专断地——“这个不必说了。我们的要点是：泛非洲在逻辑上属于泛亚洲；因此，今晚唐斯先生受到你们的欢迎——我确信——尤其受到我的欢迎。我从新宫回来的路上，他帮了我一个忙。”
众人面露兴意，可埃及人抢了话头：
“啊，殿下，新宫！如今又兴什么？表现主义、立体主义、未来主义、旋涡主义之后，又来了什么？我认输，我晕头转向。毕加索叫我心惊。马蒂斯叫我发烧。可我不懂他们。我还是爱古典。”
“刚果，”公主说，“正在漫过卫城。展出有一幅美的康定斯基，还有几件可爱的、惊人的东西，出自无名的新手。”
“*可是*，”埃及人回了一句，落进法语——于是全体投入了讨论，只撇下沉默的埃及太太和不善言辞的阿拉伯人。
这里马修又窘了。

===Original===

These persons easily penetrated worlds where he was a stranger. Frankly, but for the context he would not have known whether Picasso was a man, a city, or a vegetable. He had never heard of Matisse. Lightly, almost carelessly, as he thought, his companions leapt to unknown subjects. Yet they knew. They knew art, books, and literature, politics of all nations, and not newspaper politics merely, but inner currents and whisperings, unpublished facts.
“Ah, pardon,” said the Egyptian, returning to English, “I forgot Monsieur Towns speaks only English and does not interest himself in art.”
Perhaps Matthew was sensitive and imagined that the Egyptian and the Indian rather often, if not purposely, strayed to French and subjects beyond him.
“Mr. Towns is a scientist?” asked the Japanese.
“He studies medicine,” answered the Princess.
“Ah﻿—a high service,” said the Japanese. “I was reading only today of the work on cancer by your Peyton Rous in Carrel’s laboratory.”
Towns was surprised. “What, has he discovered the etiological factor? I had not heard.”
“No, not yet, but he’s a step nearer.”
For a few moments Matthew was talking eagerly, until a babble of unknown tongues interrupted him across the table.
“Proust is dead, that ‘snob of humor’﻿—yes, but his *Recherche du Temps Perdu* is finished and will be published in full. I have only glanced at parts of it. Do you know Gasquet’s *Hymns*?”
“Beraud gets the Prix Goncourt this year. Last year it was the Negro, Maran﻿—”
“I have been reading Croce’s *Aesthetic* lately﻿—”
“Yes, I saw the Meyerhold theater in Moscow﻿—gaunt realism﻿—*Howl China* was tremendous.”
Then easily, after the crisp brown fowl, the Princess tactfully steered them back to the subject which some seemed willing to avoid.
“And so,” she said, “the darker peoples who are dissatisfied﻿—”
She looked at the Japanese and paused as though inviting comment. He bowed courteously.
“If I may presume, your Royal Highness, to suggest,” he said slowly, “the two categories are not synonymous. We ourselves know no line of color. Some of us are white, some yellow, some black. Rather, is it not, your Highness, that we have from time to time taken council with the oppressed people of the world, many of whom by chance are colored?”
“True, true,” said the Princess.

===Chinese===

这些人轻捷地穿进一个个他全然生疏的世界。坦白说，若不是有上下文，他连毕加索是个人名、一座城，还是一种蔬菜都分不清。马蒂斯他压根没听说过。他的同伴们——照他看来——轻描淡写、几乎漫不经心地就跳上了陌生的题目。可他们懂。他们懂艺术、书籍、文学，懂各国的政治——不只是报纸上的政治，还有内里的潜流和私语、没发表的事实。
“啊，对不起，”埃及人换回英语，“我忘了唐斯先生只讲英语，也不关心艺术。”
兴许是马修神经过敏：他觉得埃及人和那位印度人相当频繁地——虽不见得是故意的——滑进法语和他够不着的题目里去。
“唐斯先生是科学家？”日本人问。
“他学医。”公主答。
“啊——高尚的事业，”日本人说，“就在今天，我还读到你们佩顿·劳斯在卡雷尔实验室做的癌症工作。”
唐斯吃了一惊。“怎么，他发现病原了？我没听说。”
“还没有，可又近了一步。”
有那么几分钟，马修谈得起劲，直到桌子那头一阵听不懂的语言的嘈杂打断了他。
“普鲁斯特死了，那位‘幽默的势利鬼’——是，可他的《追忆似水年华》完稿了，要出全本了。我只扫过几段。加斯克的《颂歌集》你读过吗？”
“今年的龚古尔奖给了贝罗。去年给的是那个黑人，马朗——”
“我近来在读克罗齐的《美学》——”
“是啊，我在莫斯科看了梅耶荷德剧院——瘦骨嶙峋的现实主义——《吼叫吧，中国》了不得。”
随后，脆皮棕鸡上过，公主不动声色地把话引回那个似乎有人乐于避开的话题。
“所以，”她说，“这些心怀不满的有色人民——”
她望着日本人，一停，仿佛邀请评论。他客气地欠身。
“殿下恕我斗胆进言，”他缓缓地说，“这两个范畴并不同义。我们自己并不知道有什么肤色线。我们有白，有黄，有黑。毋宁说——殿下，是不是这样——我们是不时地同世上受压迫的人们会商，而那些人恰好多半是有色的？”
“是，是。”公主说。

===Original===

“And yet,” said the Chinese lady, “it is dominating Europe which has flung this challenge of the color line, and we cannot avoid it.”
“And on either count,” said Matthew, “whether we be bound by oppression or by color, surely we Negroes belong in the foremost ranks.”
There was a slight pause, a sort of hesitation, and it seemed to Matthew as though all expected the Japanese to speak. He did, slowly and gravely:
“It would be unfair to our guest not to explain with some clarity and precision that the whole question of the Negro race both in Africa and in America is for us not simply a question of suffering and compassion. Need we say that for these peoples we have every human sympathy? But for us here and for the larger company we represent, there is a deeper question﻿—that of the ability, qualifications, and real possibilities of the black race in Africa or elsewhere.”
Matthew left the piquant salad and laid down his fork slowly. Up to this moment he had been quite happy. Despite the feeling of being out of it now and then, he had assumed that this was his world, his people, from the high and beautiful lady whom he worshiped more and more, even to the Egyptians, Indians and Arab who seemed slightly, but very slightly, aloof or misunderstanding.
