Suddenly now there loomed plain and clear the shadow of a color line within a color line, a prejudice within prejudice, and he and his again the sacrifice. His eyes became somber and did not lighten even when the Princess spoke.
“I cannot see that it makes great difference what ability Negroes have. Oppression is oppression. It is our privilege to relieve it.”
“Yes,” answered the Japanese, “but who will do it? Who can do it but those superior races whose necks now bear the yoke of the inferior rabble of Europe?”
“This,” said the Princess, “I have always believed; but as I have told your Excellency, I have received impressions in Moscow which have given me very serious thought﻿—first as to our judgment of the ability of the Negro race, and second”﻿—she paused in thought﻿—“as to the relative ability of all classes and peoples.”
Matthew stared at her, as she continued:
“You see, Moscow has reports, careful reports of the world’s masses. And the report on the Negroes of America was astonishing.

===Chinese===

“然而，”中国女士说，“抛出这道肤色线挑战的，正是称霸的欧洲；我们躲不开它。”
“而无论按哪一条算，”马修说，“是按受压迫结的盟，还是按肤色结的盟，我们黑人当然都该站在最前列。”
一阵小小的停顿，一种犹豫；马修觉得人人都在等日本人开口。他开了，缓慢而庄重：
“若不向我们的客人相当清楚、相当确切地说明，就未免不公了：黑人种族在非洲和美洲的整个问题，对我们来说，不单是一个苦难与怜悯的问题。还用说吗，对这些民族，我们怀着一切人类的同情。可对我们这一席人、对我们所代表的那个更大的人群，还有一个更深的问题——黑人种族在非洲或别处的能力、资格和真正的可能性。”
马修擜下那道辛辣的沙拉，慢慢放下了叉子。到此刻为止，他一直是幸福的。尽管时而觉得隔膜，他一直认定这是他的世界、他的族人——从他越拜越深的那位高华美丽的夫人，直到那些显得稍稍——只是稍稍——疏远或误解的埃及人、印度人和阿拉伯人。
此刻骤然清晰地立起一道影子：肤色线里头的肤色线，偏见里头的偏见，而他和他的人又成了祭品。他的眼睛沉暗下去，连公主开口也没有亮起来。
“我看不出黑人的能力怎样有什么大干系。压迫就是压迫。解除它，是我们的特权。”
“是，”日本人答，“可由谁来做？除了那些脖颈上正压着欧洲下等暴民之轭的优等种族，谁做得了？”
“这个，”公主说，“我一向也这样信；可我同阁下说过了，我在莫斯科得到的一些印象，叫我很认真地想了想——第一，想我们对黑人种族能力的判断；第二——”她沉思地顿了顿——“想一切阶级和民族彼此的能力高下。”
马修瞪着她，她接着说：
“您要知道，莫斯科有报告，对世界大众的详尽报告。而关于美国黑人的那份报告，令人震惊。

===Original===

At the time, I doubted its truth: their education, their work, their property, their organizations; and the odds, the terrible, crushing odds against which, inch by inch and heartbreak by heartbreak, they have forged their unfaltering way upward. If the report is true, they are a nation today, a modern nation worthy to stand beside any nation here.”
“But can we put any faith in Moscow?” asked the Egyptian. “Are we not keeping dangerous company and leaning on broken reeds?”
“Well,” said Matthew, “if they are as sound in everything as in this report from America, they’ll bear listening to.”
The young Indian spoke gently and evenly, but with bright eyes.
“Naturally,” he said, “one can see Mr. Towns needs must agree with the Bolshevik estimate of the lower classes.”
Matthew felt the slight slur and winced. He thought he saw the lips of the Princess tighten ever so little. He started to answer quickly, with aplomb if not actual swagger.
“I reckon,” he began﻿—then something changed within him. It was as if he had faced and made a decision, as though some great voice, crying and reverberating within his soul, spoke for him and yet was him. He had started to say, “I reckon there’s as much highborn blood among American Negroes as among any people. We’ve had our kings, presidents, and judges” He started to say this, but he did not finish. He found himself saying quite calmly and with slightly lifted chin:
“I reckon you’re right. We American blacks are very common people. My grandfather was a whipped and driven slave; my father was never really free and died in jail. My mother plows and washes for a living. We come out of the depths﻿—the blood and mud of battle. And from just such depths, I take it, came most of the worthwhile things in this old world. If they didn’t, God help us.”
The table was very still, save for the very faint clink of china as the servants brought in the creamed and iced fruit.
The Princess turned, and he could feel her dark eyes full upon him.
“I wonder I wonder,” she murmured, almost catching her breath.
The Indian frowned. The Japanese smiled, and the Egyptian whispered to the Arab.
“I believe that is true,” said the Chinese lady thoughtfully, “and if it is, this world is glorious.”
“And if it is not?” asked the Egyptian icily.
“It is perhaps both true and untrue,” the Japanese suggested. “Certainly Mr.

===Chinese===

当时我怀疑它的真实性：他们的教育、他们的工作、他们的财产、他们的组织；还有他们顶着的那种几率——可怕地、压倒一切地不利的几率，他们就是顶着它，一寸一寸、一场心碎一场心碎，锻造出自己坚定的向上之路。倘若那份报告属实，他们今天已然是一个民族，一个现代民族，配同这里的任何民族并肩而立。”
“可我们能信莫斯科吗？”埃及人问，“我们这不是在与危险的家伙为伍，倚着断了的芦苇吗？”
“唔，”马修说，“要是他们在一切事情上都像这份来自美国的报告这样可靠，那他们值得一听。”
年轻的印度人语调温和平缓，眼睛却亮着。
“自然，”他说，“诸位看得出，唐斯先生势必赞同布尔什维克对下层阶级的估量。”
马修感到这轻轻的一记讥刺，缩了一下。他仿佛看见公主的嘴唇极轻微地抿紧。他急急开口作答，与其说是自负，不如说是硬撑着派头。
“我估摸——”他开了口——随即他心里有什么变了。仿佛他已面对并作出了决断，仿佛有个巨大的声音，在他灵魂里呼喊、回荡，替他说了话，却也就是他自己。他本要说：“我估摸美国黑人里的高贵血统，不比哪一个民族的少。我们有过我们的君王、总统和法官。”这话他起了头，却没有说完。他发现自己说得相当平静，下巴微微扬着：
“我估摸您说得对。我们美国黑人是顶普通的百姓。我祖父是个挨鞭子、被驱赶的奴隶；我父亲从来没有真正自由过，死在牢里。我母亲靠种地洗衣过活。我们是从深处走出来的——从战斗的血和泥。而我想，这个旧世界里大多数有价值的东西，正是从这样的深处走出来的。倘若不是，那就求上帝可怜我们。”
餐桌上一片安静，只有侍者们端上奶油冰镇水果时，瓷器碰出极轻的叮声。
公主转过身来，他觉出她那双黑眼睛满满地落在他身上。
“我想不到——真想不到，”她喃喃道，几乎屏住了呼吸。
印度人皱起眉。日本人微笑，埃及人同阿拉伯人耳语。
“我相信这是真话，”中国女士若有所思地说，“倘若如此，这个世界便是辉煌的。”
“倘若不是呢？”埃及人冷冷地问。
“也许真假参半吧。”日本人提了个话头，“当然，唐

===Original===

Towns has expressed a fine and human hope, although I fear that always blood must tell.”
“No, it mustn’t,” cried Matthew, “unless it is allowed to talk. Its speech is accidental today. There is some weak, thin stuff called blood, which not even a crown can make speak intelligently; and at the same time some of the noblest blood God ever made is dumb with chains and poverty.”
The elder Indian straightened, with blazing eyes.
“Surely,” he said, slowly and calmly, “surely the gentleman does not mean to reflect on royal blood?”
Matthew started, flushed darkly, and glanced quickly at the Princess. She smiled and said lightly, “Certainly not,” and then with a pause and a look straight across the table to the turban and tunic, “nor will royal blood offer insult to him.” The Indian bowed to the tablecloth and was silent.
As they rose and sauntered out to coffee in the silk and golden drawing-room, there was a discussion, started of course by the Egyptian, first of the style of the elaborate piano case and then of Schönberg’s new and unobtrusive transcription of Bach’s triumphant choral Prelude, “Komm, Gott, Schöpfer.”
The Princess sat down. Matthew could not take his eyes from her. Her fingers idly caressed the keys as her tiny feet sought the pedals. From white, pearl-embroidered slippers, her young limbs, smooth in pale, dull silk, swept up in long, low lines. Even the delicate curving of her knees he saw as she drew aside her drapery and struck the first warm tones. She played the phrase in dispute﻿—great chords of aspiration and vision that melted to soft melody. The Egyptian acknowledged his fault. “Yes﻿—yes, that was the theme I had forgotten.”
Again, Matthew felt his lack of culture audible, and not simply of his own culture, but of all the culture in white America he had unconsciously and foolishly, as he now realized, made his norm. Yet withal Matthew was not unhappy. If he was a bit out of it, if he sensed divided counsels and opposition, yet he still felt almost fiercely that that was his world. Here are culture, wealth, and beauty. Here was power, and here he had some recognized part. God! If he could just do his part, any part! And he waited impatiently for the real talk to begin again.
It began and lasted until after midnight.

===Chinese===

斯先生表达了一个美好而富有人情的期望，只是我担心，血统终究是要说话的。”
“不，未必，”马修喊道，“除非容它开口。如今它说的话纯属偶然。有一种稀薄孱弱的玩意儿叫血统，连王冠也逼不出它一句聪明话；同时，上帝造的一些最高贵的血统，却被锁链和贫穷憋成了哑巴。”
年长的印度人挺直了身子，目光灼灼。
“恕我冒昧，”他缓缓地、平静地说，“这位先生该不是有意指摘王室的血统吧？”
马修一惊，脸黑黑地涨红了，飞快看了公主一眼。她微笑着轻描淡写地说：“当然不是。”随后一顿，目光径直越过餐桌，望向那顶头巾和那件束腰长袍：“王室的血统也不会侮辱于他。”印度人朝桌布一躬，沉默了。
众人起身，踱进那间绸缎金黄的客厅去用咖啡。席间起了一场议论，当然是埃及人开的头，先谈那架考究琴匣的式样，又谈勋伯格新编的、不事张扬的巴赫凯旋圣咏前奏曲《来吧，上帝，创造者》。
公主坐下。马修的眼睛离不开她。她的手指闲闲地抚着琴键，小小的脚去寻踏板。从绣珠的白缎鞋往上，她年轻的肢体裹在素净的暗色绸里，顺着低低的长线一路起伏。她撩开衣裙、敲下最初几个和暖的音时，连她膝头那一线精致的弧曲，他也看见了。她弹了那句起争议的乐句——向往与远望的大和弦，融成柔曼的旋律。埃及人认了错：“对——对，这就是我忘掉的那个主题。”
马修又一次觉得自己缺教养缺得刺耳——缺的不止是他自己的教养，还有整个白人美国的教养；他如今才明白，他曾不自觉地、愚蠢地把它当成了标准。然而马修毕竟不是不快活的。就算他有点格格不入，就算他嗅得出意见分歧和对立，他仍旧近乎凶悍地觉得，这是他的世界。这里有文化、财富和美，这里有权力，而他在这里有一份公认的位置。上帝啊！但求让他尽他的一份力，随便哪一份！他不耐烦地等着真正的谈话重新开始。
谈话开始了，一直持续到后半夜。

===Original===

It started on lines so familiar to Matthew that he had to shut his eyes and stare again at their swarthy faces: Superior races﻿—the right to rule﻿—born to command﻿—inferior breeds﻿—the lower classes﻿—the rabble. How the Egyptian rolled off his tongue his contempt for the “r‑r‑rabble”! How contemptuous was the young Indian of inferior races! But how humorous it was to Matthew to see all tables turned; the rabble now was the white workers of Europe; the inferior races were the ruling whites of Europe and America. The superior races were yellow and brown.
“You see,” said the Japanese, “Mr. Town, we here are all agreed and not agreed. We are agreed that the present white hegemony of the world is nonsense; that the darker peoples are the best the natural aristocracy, the makers of art, religion, philosophy, life, everything except brazen machines.”
“But why?”
“Because of the longer rule of natural aristocracy among us. We count our millenniums of history where Europe counts her centuries. We have our own carefully thought-out philosophy and civilization, while Europe has sought to adopt an ill-fitting mélange of the cultures of the world.”
“But does this not all come out the same gate, with the majority of mankind serving the minority? And if this is the only ideal of civilization, does the tint of a skin matter in the question of who leads?” Thus Matthew summed it up.
“Not a whit﻿—it is the natural inborn superiority that matters,” said the Japanese, “and it is that which the present color bar of Europe is holding back.”
“And what guarantees, in the future more than in the past and with colored more than with white, the wise rule of the gifted and powerful?”
“Self-interest and the inclusion in their ranks of all really superior men of all colors﻿—the best of Asia together with the best of the British aristocracy, of the German Adel, of the French writers and financiers﻿—of the rulers, artists, and poets of all peoples.”
“And suppose we found that ability and talent and art is not entirely or even mainly among the reigning aristocrats of Asia and Europe, but buried among millions of men down in the great sodden masses of all men and even in Black Africa?”
“It would come forth.”
“Would it?”
“Yes,” said the Princess, “it would come forth, but when and how? In slow and tenderly nourished efflorescence, or in wild and bloody upheaval with all that bitter loss?”

===Chinese===

开头的话路，马修熟悉得只能闭上眼睛，再盯着这些黝黑的面孔重看一遍：优等种族——统治的权利——生而指挥——劣等人种——下等阶级——暴民。埃及人舌头一滚，把“暴——暴——暴民”的轻蔑滚得多顺！年轻的印度人对劣等种族又是何等不屑！可在马修看来，这一切何其滑稽——所有的牌桌全翻了过来：如今暴民是欧洲的白人工人；劣等种族是欧美掌权的白人。优等种族，是黄种人和棕色人种。
“您看，”日本人说，“唐斯先生，我们这些人既一致又不一致。我们一致认为，当今白人对世界的霸权是无稽的；一致认为，更深的民族才是天然的至上贵族——是艺术、宗教、哲学、生活，除赤裸裸的机器之外一切东西的创造者。”
“可为什么？”
“因为天然贵族在我们中间统治得更久。我们数我们的历史以千年计，欧洲数她的是百年。我们有自己的深思熟虑的哲学和文明，而欧洲只求得了一套东拼西凑、并不合身的世界文化大杂烩。”
“可这一切岂不都出自同一道门——多数人类伺候少数人类？倘若这就是文明的唯一理想，那么谁来领跑这件事上，皮肤的色调还重要吗？”马修就这样归结。
“毫不重要——要紧的是天然的、生来的优越。”日本人说，“而恰恰这一点，被今日欧洲的肤色栅栏拦住了。”
“那么有什么保证——将来比过去更能、有色人比白人更能——保证有才有势者的明智统治？”
“利害之心，加上把一切颜色的真正优越者收进他们的行列——亚洲的精华，连同英吉利贵族、德意志容克、法兰西文人与金融家的精华——连同一切民族的主宰者、艺术家和诗人。”
“假如我们发现，能力、才华和艺术并不全在、甚至主要不在亚欧在位的贵族那里，而是埋在千百万人的大水洼般的众生之中，甚至埋在黑色非洲呢？”
“那它自会破土而出。”
“当真？”
“当真，”公主说，“它自会破土而出。可是何时？怎样？是在缓慢的、精心培育的花期里，还是在狂野血腥的大动荡里，连同那一切惨痛的损耗？”

===Original===

“Pah!” blurted the Egyptian﻿—“pardon, Royal Highness﻿—but what art ever came from the canaille!”
The blood rushed to Matthew’s face. He threw back his head and closed his eyes, and with the movement he heard again the Great Song. He saw his father in the old log church by the river, leading the moaning singers in the Great Song of Emancipation. Clearly, plainly he heard that mighty voice and saw the rhythmic swing and beat of the thick brown arm. Matthew swung his arm and beat the table; the silver tinkled. Silence dropped on all, and suddenly Matthew found himself singing. His voice full, untrained but mellow, quivered down the first plaintive bar:

> “When Israel was in Egypt land﻿—”

Then it gathered depth:

> “Let my people go!”

He forgot his audience and saw only the shining river and the bowed and shouting throng:

> “Oppressed so hard, they could not stand,

Let my people go.”

Then Matthew let go restraint and sang as his people sang in Virginia, twenty years ago. His great voice, gathered in one long deep breath, rolled the Call of God:

> “Go down, Moses!

Way down into the Egypt land,

Tell Old Pharaoh

To let my people go!”

===Chinese===

“呸！”埃及人冲口而出，“殿下恕罪——可是，从暴民里能出什么艺术！”
血涌上马修的脸。他仰起头，闭上眼，随着这个动作，他又听见了那首大歌。他看见他父亲在河边古老的木教堂里，领着呜咽的歌手们唱《解放大歌》。清晰、真切，他听见那洪大的嗓音，看见那条厚实的棕色手臂有节奏的挥击。马修挥起手臂，一掌拍在桌上，银器叮当作响。满座倏然无声，忽然，马修发觉自己在唱歌。他的嗓音饱满，未经训练却圆润，颤出第一句哀婉的调子：

> “当以色列人在埃及地——”

随后它沉厚起来：

> “让我的百姓走吧！”

他忘了听众，只看见那条亮闪闪的河，和那些俯身欢呼的人群：

> “受压太深，站也站不住，

让我的百姓走吧。”

随后马修放开了拘束，像二十年前在弗吉尼亚他的族人那样唱。他伟大的嗓音，蓄在一口悠长的深呼吸里，滚出上帝的呼召：

> “下去吧，摩西！

一路下到埃及地，

告诉老法老：

让我的百姓走吧！”

===Original===

He stopped as quickly as he had begun, ashamed, and beads of sweat gathered on his forehead. Still there was silence﻿—silence almost breathless. The voice of the Chinese woman broke it.
“It was an American slave song! I know it. How﻿—how wonderful.”
A chorus of approval poured out, led by the Egyptian.
“That,” said Matthew, “came out of the black rabble of America.” And he trilled his *r*. They all smiled as the tension broke.
“You assume then,” said the Princess at last, “that the mass of the workers of the world can rule as well as be ruled?”
“Yes﻿—or rather can work as well as be worked, can live as well as be kept alive. America is teaching the world one thing and only one thing of real value, and that is, that ability and capacity for culture is not the hereditary monopoly of a few, but the widespread possibility for the majority of mankind if they only have a decent chance in life.”
The Chinaman spoke: “If Mr. Towns’ assumption is true, and I believe it is, and recognized, as some time it must be, it will revolutionize the world.”
“It will revolutionize the world,” smiled the Japanese, “but not﻿—today.”
“Nor this *siécle*,” growled the Arab.
“Nor the next﻿—and so *in saecula saeculorum*,” laughed the Egyptian.
“Well,” said the little Chinese lady, “the unexpected happens.”
And Matthew added ruefully, “It’s about all that does happen!”
He lapsed into blank silence, wondering how he had come to express the astonishing philosophy which had leapt unpremeditated from his lips. Did he himself believe it? As they arose from the table the Princess called him aside.
VII
“I trust you will pardon the interruption at this late hour,” said the Japanese. Matthew glanced up in surprise as the Japanese, the two Indians, and the Arab entered his room. “Sure,” said he cheerily, “have any seats you can find. Sorry there’s so little space.”
It was three o’clock in the morning. He was in his shirt sleeves without collar, and he was packing hastily, wondering how on earth all these things had ever come out of his two valises. The little room on the fifth floor of the Roter Adler Hotel did look rather a mess. But his guests smiled and so politely deprecated any excuses or discomfort that he laughed too, and leaned against the window, while they stood about door and bed.

===Chinese===

他收得同起头一样突兀，羞愧起来，额上沁出汗珠。依旧是寂静——寂静得近乎屏息。中国女士的声音打破了它。
“这是一首美国奴隶歌！我知道它。多——多了不起。”
一片赞许涌了出来，领头的正是埃及人。
“那，”马修说，“正是从美国的黑色暴民里长出来的。”他卷着舌头打了个颤音。紧张的空气散了，众人都笑了。
“那么您认定，”公主终于说，“世界的劳工大众既能被统治，也能去统治？”
“是——更确切地说，既能被驱使干活，也能自己干活；既能被人养活，也能自己活。美国正在教给世界一样东西，只有一样真正有价值的东西，那就是：文化上的能力与才具，并非少数人的世袭专利，而是多数人类的普遍可能——只要他们在生活里得到一个过得去的机会。”
中国人开口了：“倘若唐斯先生的假定成立——我相信它成立——并且有朝一日被承认——它必被承认——它将革了世界的命。”
“它会革了世界的命，”日本人微笑道，“但不是——今天。”
“也不是这个世纪。”阿拉伯人咕哝。
“也不是下个世纪——如此世世代代，永无穷尽。”埃及人笑道。
“唔，”娇小的中国女士说，“意外总会发生。”
马修苦笑着补了一句：“发生的大概也只有意外了！”
他坠入一片茫然的沉默，纳闷自己怎么讲出了这套惊人的哲学——不假思索地跳到唇边的哲学。他自己信吗？众人离席时，公主把他唤到一旁。
七
“深更半夜打扰，还望海涵。”日本人说。马修惊讶地抬头，日本人、两位印度人和阿拉伯人正走进他的房间。“当然，”他快活地说，“找得着什么就坐什么。地方太窄，抱歉。”
凌晨三点钟。他没戴领子，只穿着衬衫，正匆匆收拾行李，纳闷这么多东西当初究竟是怎么从两只手提箱里变出来的。红鹰旅馆五楼这间小屋看上去确实够乱。可客人们客客气气地笑着，把一切歉意和不适都挡了回去，他也笑了，倚在窗边，他们便分站在门口和床边。

===Original===

“You had, I believe,” continued the Japanese, “an interview with her Royal Highness, the Princess, before you left her home tonight.”
“Yes.”
“I﻿—er﻿—presume you realize, Mr. Towns, that the Princess of Bwodpur is a lady of very high station﻿—of great wealth and influence.”
“I cannot imagine anybody higher.”
The elder Indian interrupted. “There are,” he said, “of course, some persons of higher hereditary rank than her Royal Highness, but not many. She is of royal blood by many scores of generations of direct descent. She is a ruling potentate in her own right over six millions of loyal subjects and yields to no human being in the ancient splendor of her heritage. Her income, her wealth in treasure and jewels, is uncounted. Sir, there are few people in the world who have the right to touch the hem of her garment.” The Indian drew himself to full height and looked at Matthew.
“I’m strongly inclined to agree with you there,” said Matthew, smiling genially.
“I had feared,” continued the younger Indian, also looking Matthew squarely in the eye, “that perhaps, being American, and unused to the ceremony of countries of rank, you might misunderstand the democratic graciousness of her Royal Highness toward you. We appreciate, sir, more than we can say,” and both Indians bowed low, “your inestimable service to the Princess yesterday, in protecting her royal person from insufferable insult. But the very incident illustrates and explains our errand.
“The Princess is young and headstrong. She delights, in her new European independence, to elude her escort, and has given us moments of greatest solicitude and even fright. Meeting her as you did yesterday, it was natural for you to take her graciousness toward you as the camaraderie of an equal, and﻿—quite unconsciously, I am sure﻿—your attitude toward her has caused us grave misgiving.”
“You mean that I have not treated the Princess with courtesy?” asked Matthew in consternation. “In what way? Tell me.”
“It is nothing﻿—nothing, now that it is past, and since the Princess was gracious enough to allow it.

===Chinese===

“我想，”日本人接着说，“今晚离开她府上之前，您同公主殿下有一番谈话。”
“是。”
“我——呃——想唐斯先生明白，布沃德普尔的公主是位身份极崇高的贵妇——有巨大的财富和势力。”
“我想不出还有谁更高。”
年长的印度人插进来。“当然，”他说，“世上有几位血统门第比殿下更高的，但为数不多。她身负王族血统，直系传承数十代。她凭自身便是统治六百万忠顺臣民的君主，论家世的古老煊赫，不让人世间的任何人。她的岁入，她珍宝的财富，无从计数。先生，这世上够资格碰她衣角的人，屈指可数。”印度人挺足身量，看着马修。
“这一点上我十二分乐意赞同您。”马修和蔼地笑着说。
“我一直担心，”年轻的印度人也直视着马修的眼睛，接着说，“您是美国人，又不习惯有身份国家的礼数，也许会误解殿下对您那份民主的亲切。我们感激您，先生，言语无法表达，”两位印度人齐齐深深一躬，“感激您昨天护卫公主御体、使其免遭难堪侮辱的那份无可估量的功劳。而恰恰是那件事，说明也解释了我们此来的缘由。
“公主年轻，任性。她新得了欧洲式的独立，喜欢甩开随从自去游玩，不止一次叫我们揪心彻骨，甚至魂飞魄散。昨天您那样同她相遇，自然会把她的亲切当作平等者的情谊，而且——我敢说全然是无意的——您对她的态度，引起了我们严重的疑惧。”
“您是说，我对公主失礼了？”马修惶然问，“在哪里？请讲。”
“没有什么——事情既已过去，公主又宽宏地不予计较，就没有什么了。

===Original===

But you may recall that you never addressed her by her rightful title of ‘Royal Highness’; you several times interrupted her conversation and addressed her before being addressed; you occupied the seat of honor without even an attempted refusal and actually shook her Highness’ hand, which we are taught to regard as unpardonable familiarity.”
Matthew grinned cheerfully. “I reckon if the Princess hadn’t liked all that she’d have said so﻿—”
The Japanese quickly intervened. “This is, pardon me, beside our main errand,” he said. “We realize that you admire and revere the Princess not only as a supremely beautiful woman of high rank, but as one of rare intelligence and high ideals.”
“I certainly do.”
“And we assume that anything you could do﻿—anyway you could cooperate with us for her safety and future, we could count upon your doing?”
“To my very life.”
“Good﻿—excellent﻿—you see, my friends,” turning to the still disturbed Indians and the silent, sullen Arab, “it is as I rightly divined.”
They did not appear wholly convinced, but the Japanese continued:
“In her interview with you she told you a story she had heard in Moscow, of a widespread and carefully planned uprising of the American blacks. She has entrusted you with a letter to the alleged leader of this organization and asked you to report to her your impressions and recommendations; and even to deliver the letter, if you deem it wise.
“Now, my dear Mr. Towns, consider the situation: First of all, our beloved Princess introduces you, a total stranger, into our counsels and tells you some of our general plans. Fortunately, you prove to be a gentleman who can be trusted; and yet you yourself must admit this procedure was not exactly wise. Further than that, through this letter, our reputations, our very lives, are put in danger by this well-meaning but young and undisciplined lady. Her unfortunate visit to Russia has inoculated her with Bolshevism of a mild but dangerous type. The letter contains money to encourage treason. You know perfectly well that the American Negroes will neither rebel nor fight unless put up to it or led like dumb cattle by whites. You have never even heard of the alleged leader, as you acknowledged to the Princess.”
“She is evidently well spied upon.”
“She is, and will always be, well guarded,” answered the elder Indian tensely.
“Except yesterday,” said Matthew.

===Chinese===

可您或许记得：您从没有用她应得的尊称‘殿下’称呼过她；您几次打断她的话，不等她开口您先开口；您占了尊座，连虚让一下都没有；您还真的握了殿下的手——在我们受的教育里，那是不可饶恕的放肆。”
马修痛快地咧嘴一笑。“我估摸，公主要是不喜欢这些，她早说了——”
日本人连忙拦住话头。“这个，恕我直言，离我们的正题远了。”他说，“我们知道，您敬慕公主，不只因为她是一位位分极崇高的绝色佳人，更因为她智力罕有、志向高洁。”
“当然。”
“那么我们假定：凡您做得到的、凡您能同我们合作以保她安全与将来的，我们都指望您去做，对吗？”
“舍命也在所不辞。”
“好——好极了——你们看，诸位，”他转向仍愤愤不平的印度人和沉默阴沉的阿拉伯人，“我猜得不错吧。”
他们似乎并未全然信服，日本人却继续说下去：
“在同您的谈话里，她把在莫斯科听到的一个故事讲给了您：美国黑人一场范围广泛、计划周密的起义。她把一封给那个所谓领袖的信托付给您，请您把您的印象和建议报告给她；倘若您认为妥当，甚至请您把信送到。
“现在，亲爱的唐斯先生，请想一想这局面：第一，我们敬爱的公主把您——一个全然陌生的人——引进了我们的谋划，还把我们的若干总计划讲给您听。所幸您证明是位可以信托的君子；可您自己也得承认，这一步并不怎么高明。再者，通过这封信，我们的名誉、我们的性命，都悬在这位好心的、然而年轻而无纪律的贵妇手里。她那趟不幸的俄国之行，叫她染上了一种温和却危险型的布尔什维主义。那封信里装着煽动叛乱的钱。您完全清楚，美国的黑人既不会造反，也不会战斗，除非有人撺掇，或者像牲口一样被白人牵着走。就连那位所谓的领袖，正如您向公主承认的，您也从没听说过。”
“她显然被人严密监视着。”
“她被严密保护着，今后也永远会这样。”年长的印度人紧张地答道。
“昨天除外。”马修说。

===Original===

But the Japanese quickly proceeded. “Why then go on this wild goose chase? Why deliver dynamite to children?”
“Thank you.”
“I beg your pardon. I may speak harshly, but I speak frankly. You are an exception among your people.”
“I’ve heard that before. Once I believed it. Now I do not.”
“You are generous, but you are an exception, and you know you are.”
“Most people are exceptions.”
“You know that your people are cowards.”
“That’s a lie; they are the bravest people fighting for justice today.”
“I wish it were so, but I do not believe it, and neither do you. Every report from America﻿—and believe me, we have many﻿—contradicts this statement for you. I am not blaming them, poor things, they were slaves and children of slaves. They cannot even begin to rise in a century. We Samurai have been lords a thousand years and more; the ancestors of her Royal Highness have ruled for twenty centuries﻿—how can you think to place yourselves beside us as equals? No﻿—no﻿—restrain your natural anger and distaste for such truth. Our situation is too delicate for niceties. We have been almost betrayed by an impulsive woman, high and royal personage though she be. We have come to get that letter and to ask you to write a report now, to be delivered later, thoroughly disenchanting our dear Princess of this black American chimera.”
“And if I refuse?”
The Japanese looked pained but patient. The others moved impatiently, and perceptibly narrowed the circle about Matthew. He was thinking rapidly; the letter was in his coat pocket on the bed beyond the Japanese and within easy reach of the Indians if they had known it. If he jumped out the window, he would be dead, and they would eventually secure the letter. If he fought, they were undoubtedly armed, and four to one. The Japanese was elderly and negligible as an opponent, but the young Indian and the Arab were formidable, and the older Indian dangerous. He might perhaps kill one and disable another and raise enough hullabaloo to arouse the hotel, but how would such a course affect the Princess?
The Japanese watched him sadly.
“Why speak of unpleasant things,” he said gently, “or contemplate futilities? We are not barbarians. We are men of thought and culture. Be assured our plans have been laid with care. We know the host of this hotel well. Resistance on your part would be absolutely futile.

===Chinese===

日本人却赶忙接了下去。“那又何必去追这空头野鹅？何必把炸药送到孩子手里？”
“多谢夸奖。”
“恕我失言。我或许说得重，但我说的是实话。您是您的民族中的例外。”
“这话我听过。从前我信。如今不信了。”
“您宽宏大量，可您是例外，您自己也知道。”
“大多数人都是例外。”
“您知道您的人民是懦夫。”
“那是谎话；他们是当今为正义而战的最勇敢的民族。”
“但愿如此，可我不信，您也不信。来自美国的一切报告——请相信，我们看到过许多——都驳斥您这句话。我不怪他们，可怜的人，他们是奴隶和奴隶的儿女，一百年里连个头也开不了。我们武士做上等人已有一千多年；殿下的祖先统治了二十个世纪——您怎么想同我们并肩而立、平起平坐？不——不——请按住您对这种事实的自然愤怒与反感。我们的处境太微妙，容不得客套。我们险些被一个任性的女人出卖——尽管她是位尊贵的王室人物。我们是来取那封信的，并请您现在就写一份报告，日后呈上，好彻底祛除我们亲爱的公主对这个美国黑蜃楼的迷梦。”
“要是我拒绝呢？”
日本人面露痛色，却仍耐心。其余的人不耐烦地动了动，围着马修的圈子眼见地收紧了。他脑筋飞转：信就在床上、日本人身后他那件外套的口袋里，印度人一伸手就够得着——倘若他们知道的话。跳窗，他会摔死，信最终也保不住。动手，他们必定带着家伙，四对一。日本人上了年纪，作为对手可以不计；年轻的印度人和阿拉伯人是硬手，年长的那个更危险。他或许能结果一个、废掉另一个，闹出足够大的动静惊起全旅馆——可这样一场面，又会把公主置于何地？
日本人忧伤地望着他。
“何必说这些不愉快的事，”他温和地说，“何必想那些无用功？我们不是野蛮人。我们是思想和文化的人。请放心，我们的计划布置周密。这家旅馆的老板我们熟得很。您若抵抗，绝无用处。

===Original===

The back stairs opposite your entrance are quite clear and will be kept clear until we go. And when we go, the letter will go with us.”
Matthew set his back firmly against the window. His thoughts raced. They were armed, but they would use their arms only as a last resort; pistols were noisy and knives were messy. Oh, they would use them﻿—one look at their hard, set eyes showed that; but not first. Good! Then first, instead of lurching forward to attack as they might expect, he would do a first-base slide and spike the Japanese in the ankles. It was a mean trick, but anything was fair now. He remembered once when they were playing the DeWitt Clinton High﻿—But he jerked his thoughts back. The Japanese was nearest him; the fiery younger Indian just behind him and a bit to the right, bringing him nearer the bed and blocking the aisle. By the door was the elder Indian, and at the foot of the bed, the Arab.
Good! He would, at the very first movement of the young Indian, who he instinctively knew, would begin the mélée, slide feet forward into the ankles of the Japanese, catching him a little to the right so that he would fall or lurch between him and the Indian. Then he would with the same movement slide under the low iron bed and rise with the bed as weapon and shield. But he would not keep it. No; he would hurl it sideways and to the left, pinning the young Indian to the wall and the Japanese to the floor. With the same movement he would attempt a football tackle on the Arab. The Arab was a tough customer﻿—tall, sinewy, and hard. If he turned left, got his knife and struck down, quick and sure, Matthew would be done for. But most probably he would, at Matthew’s first movement, turn right toward his fellows. If he did, he was done for. He would go down in the heap, knocking the old Indian against the door.
Beside that door was the electric switch. Matthew would turn it and make a last fight for the door. He might get out, and if he did, the stairs were clear. The coat and letter? Leave them, so long as he got his story to the Princess. It was all last desperate throw. He calculated that he had good chances against the Japanese’s shins, about even chances to get under the bed unscathed, and one in two to tackle the Arab. He had not more than one chance in three of making the door unscathed, but this was the only way.

===Chinese===

您房门对面的后楼梯空着，在我们走之前会一直空着。而我们走的时候，信会跟我们一道走。”
马修把背死死抵住窗户。他的念头飞奔。他们带着家伙，可家伙只到万不得已才用：手枪响声大，刀子见血脏。哦，他们会用的——看看他们那几双僵硬冷酷的眼睛就知道——但不会先用。好！那么先下手：不照他们料想的那样扑上前攻击，他要来一记垒上滑铲，直铲日本人的脚踝。这招是损了点，可眼下什么都算公平。他记得有一回对德威特·克林顿中学——他把念头拽了回来。日本人离他最近；火性的年轻印度人就在日本人身后偏右一点，倒把他往床边逼，堵住了过道。门边是年长的印度人，床脚是阿拉伯人。
好！年轻印度人一有动作——他本能地断定，混战将由这人开头——他就两脚前滑，铲进日本人的脚踝，偏右一点铲，叫他跌向或者扑向自己同印度人之间。然后借着同一个动作从那张低矮的铁床底下钻过去，再连床带起，拿床当武器当盾牌。可他不会久拿。不；他要把床朝左侧猛掷，把年轻印度人钉在墙上，把日本人压在地下。再借同一个动作，朝阿拉伯人来一记橄榄球抱摔。那阿拉伯人是块硬料——高大，精瘦，结实。他若左转身，摸到刀，又快又稳地劈下来，马修就完了。可多半，马修一动，他会右转向自己的同伴。那样，他就完了。他会整个人塌下去，把老印度人撞在门上。
门边就是电门。马修会摁下它，为夺门做最后一搏。他或许出得去，出得去，楼梯是空的。外套和信？丢下就是，只要他能把原委传到公主耳朵里。这是孤注一掷。他盘算：对付日本人的胫骨，胜算颇大；全身钻到床底，胜算各半；抱摔阿拉伯人，一比一。要不带伤冲到门边，三成把握不到——可只有这一条路。

===Original===

If he surrendered without a fight﻿—That was unthinkable. And after all, what had he to lose? Life? Well, his prospects were not brilliant anyhow. And to die for the Princess﻿—silly, of course, but it made his blood race. For the first time he glimpsed the glory of death. Meantime﻿—he said﻿—be sensible! It would not hurt to spar for time.
He pretended to be weighing the matter.
“Suppose you do steal the letter by force, do you think you can make me write a report?”
“No, a voluntary report would be desirable but not necessary. You left with the Princess, you will remember, a page of directions and information about America to guide her in the trip she is preparing to make and from which we hope to dissuade her. You appended your signature and address. From this it will be easy to draft a report in handwriting so similar to yours as to be indistinguishable by ordinary eyes.”
“You add forgery to your many accomplishments.”
“In the pursuit of our duty, we do not hesitate at theft or forgery.”
Still Matthew parried: “Suppose,” he said, “I pretended to acquiesce, gave you the letter and reported to the Princess. Suppose even I told the German newspapers of what I have seen and heard tonight.”
There was a faraway look in the eyes of the Japanese as he answered slowly: “We must follow Fate, my dear Mr. Towns, even if Fate leads﻿—to murder. We will not let you communicate with the Princess, and you are leaving Berlin tonight.”
The Indians gave a low sigh almost like relief.
Matthew straightened and spoke slowly and firmly.
“Very well. I won’t surrender that letter to anybody but the Princess﻿—not while I’m alive. And if I go out of here dead I won’t be the only corpse.”
Every eye was on the Japanese, and Matthew knew his life was in the balance. The pause was tense; then came the patient voice of the Japanese again.
“You﻿—admire the Princess, do you not?”
“With all my heart.”
The Indians winced.
“You would do her a service?”
“To the limit of my strength.”
“Very well. Let us assume that I am wrong. Assume that the Negroes are worth freedom and ready to fight for it. Can you not see that the name of this young, beautiful, and highborn lady must under no circumstances be mixed up with them, whether they gain or lose? What would not Great Britain give thus to compromise an Indian ruler?”
“That is for the Princess to decide.”
“No!

===Chinese===

不战而降——想都别想。再说，他有什么可输的？命？唉，他的前程本也不怎么光明。而为公主去死——当然傻，可这念头叫他血液奔涌。他头一回窥见了死亡的光荣。眼下呢——他对自己说——放明白些！拖一拖时间，没什么坏处。
他装出掂量的样子。
“就算你们用武力抢走那封信，你们以为还能逼我写报告？”
“不，报告出于自愿当然最好，但并非必需。您还记得，您留给公主一页关于美国的说明和资料，为她筹划中的那次旅行作引导——我们也正想劝她放弃那次旅行。那页纸上附着您的签名和地址。凭这个，仿您的笔迹拟一份报告轻而易举，寻常的眼睛分辨不出。”
“在你们的诸般才艺上头，还添了伪造。”
“为履行职责，偷窃伪造，在所不惜。”
马修还在周旋：“假如，”他说，“我假装应承，把信交给你们，再报告公主。假如我干脆把今晚的所见所闻捅给德国的报纸呢？”
日本人眼里现出一种渺远的神色，缓缓答道：“我们必须追随命运，亲爱的唐斯先生，哪怕命运引向——杀戮。我们不会让您同公主通消息，而且您今夜就离开柏林。”
两位印度人低低舒了一口气，近乎如释重负。
马修挺直身子，说得缓慢而坚定。
“很好。只要我活着，这封信除了公主本人，我不交给任何人。要是我横着出这扇门，尸首也绝不止我一具。”
所有的眼睛都望着日本人，马修知道自己的命悬在天平上。停顿绷得紧紧的；然后又传来日本人那耐心的声音。
“您——爱慕公主，是吗？”
“全心全意。”
印度人一阵瑟缩。
“您愿意为她效劳？”
“竭尽我的力气。”
“很好。就当是我错了。就当黑人配得上自由，也准备为自由而战。您难道不明白：这位年轻、美丽、出身高贵的女士的名字，无论他们成败，都万万不能同他们搅在一起？大英帝国还有什么不肯给的，只要这样扳倒一位印度王侯？”
“那要由公主自己决定。”
“不！

===Original===

She is a mere woman﻿—an inexperienced girl. You are a man of the world. For the last time, will you rescue her Royal Highness from herself?”
“No. The Princess herself must decide.”
“Then﻿—”
“Then,” said the Princess’ full voice, “the Princess will decide.” She stood in the open doorway, the obsequious and scared landlord beside her with his passkeys. She had thrown an opera cloak over her evening gown, and stood unhatted, white-slippered, and ungloved. She threw one glance at the Indians, and they bowed low with outstretched hands, She stamped her foot angrily, and they went to their knees. She wheeled to the Arab. Without a word, he stalked out. The Japanese alone remained, calm and imperturbable.
“We have failed,” he said, with a low bow.
The Princess looked at him.
“You have failed,” she said. “I am glad there is no blood on your hands.”
“A drop of blood more or less matters little in the great cause for which you and I fight, and if I have incurred your Royal Highness’ displeasure tonight, remember that, for the same great cause, I stand ready tomorrow night to repeat the deed and seal it with my life.”
The Princess looked at him with troubled eyes. Then she seated herself in the only and rather rickety chair and motioned for her two subjects to arise. Matthew never forgot that scene: he, collarless and in shirt sleeves, with sweat pouring off his face; the room in disorder, mean, narrow, small, and dingy; the Japanese standing in the same place as when he entered, in unruffled evening dress; the Indians on their knees with hidden faces; the Princess, disturbed, yet radiant. She spoke in low tones.
“I may be wrong,” she said, “and I know how right, but infinitely and calmly right, you usually are. But some voice within calls me. I have started to fight for the dark and oppressed peoples of the world; now suddenly I have seen a light. A light which illumines the mass of men and not simply its rulers, white and yellow and black. I want to see if this thing is true, if it can possibly be true that wallowing masses often conceal submerged kings. I have decided not simply to send a messenger to America but shortly to sail myself perhaps this week on the *Gigantic*. I want to see for myself if slaves can become men in a generation. If they can﻿—well, it makes the world new for you and me.”

===Chinese===

她不过是个女人——一个不更事的女孩。您是个见过世面的男人。最后一次问您：您肯不肯把殿下从她自己手里救出来？”
“不。要由公主自己决定。”
“那么——”
“那么，”公主那圆润的嗓音说，“公主就自己决定。”她立在敞开的门口，身边是那位卑躬屈膝、惊惶失措的房东，手里拿着万能钥匙。她往晚礼服上披了件观剧斗篷，不戴帽，穿白缎鞋，光着手。她朝两位印度人扫了一眼，两人长揖到地、双手前伸；她怒得顿脚，两人便跪了下去。她转向阿拉伯人。他一言不发，大步走了出去。只剩日本人留在原地，平静而纹丝不动。
“我们失败了。”他深深一躬，说。
公主看着他。
“你们失败了，”她说，“我很高兴你们手上没有血。”
“多一滴血少一滴血，在你我共赴的大业里算不得什么。倘若今晚我招了殿下的不快，请记住：为了同一桩大业，明晚我随时准备故技重施，并以性命作保。”
公主用不安的眼睛看了他片刻，随后在屋里唯一那把摇摇晃晃的椅子上坐下，示意她的两个臣民起身。马修永远忘不了那一幕：他，没领子，只穿衬衫，满脸淌汗；屋子凌乱、寒碜、窄小、昏暗；日本人站在进门时的原地，晚礼服一丝不乱；两位印度人跪着，掩着脸；公主，心绪激荡，却神采照人。她低声说话。
“我也许错了，”她说，“我也知道您们通常何等正确——无限地、平静地正确。可我心里有个声音唤我。我已经开始为世界上深色皮肤和受压迫的民族而战；如今我突然看见了一道光。一道照亮大众而不只照亮其主宰的光——白的、黄的、黑的。我想看一看这件事是不是真的：沉浮打滚的大众里，会不会常常藏着没顶的王。我已决定，不只是派一位信使去美国，我自己随后就乘船出发，也许就在这个礼拜，乘‘巨人号’。我要亲眼看看，奴隶能不能在一代人的时间里变成人。倘若能——那它就把你我脚下的世界翻新了。”

===Original===

The Japanese started to speak, but she would not pause:
“There is no need for protest or advice. I am going. Mr. Towns will perform his mission as we agreed, if he is still so minded, and as long as he is in Europe, these two gentlemen,” she glanced at the Indians, “will bear his safety on their heads, at my command. Go!”
The Indians bowed and walked out slowly, backward. She turned to the Japanese.
“Your Highness, I bid you good night and goodbye. I shall write you.”
Gravely the Japanese kissed her hand, bowed, and withdrew. The Princess looked at Matthew. He became acutely conscious of his appearance as she looked at him almost a full minute with her great, haunting eyes.
“Thank you, again,” she said slowly. “You are a brave man﻿—and loyal.” She held out her hand, low, to shake his.
But the tension of the night broke him; he quivered, and taking her hand in both his, kissed it.
She rose quickly, drew herself up, and looked at first almost affronted; then when she saw his swimming eyes, a kind of startled wonder flashed in hers. Slowly she held out her hand again, regally, palm down and the long fingers drooping.
“You are very young,” she said.
He was. He was only twenty-five. The Princess was all of twenty-three.

===Chinese===

日本人正要开口，她不容停顿：
“不必抗议，也不必劝谏。我这就去。唐斯先生将按我们的约定完成他的使命，只要他还愿意；而且只要他在欧洲一日，这两位先生，”她瞥了印度人一眼，“奉我之命，要拿他们的头颅担保他的安全。去吧！”
两位印度人躬身，倒退着缓缓出去了。她转向日本人。
“殿下，我祝您晚安，也道别。我会写信给您。”
日本人庄重地吻了她的手，一躬，退了出去。公主看着马修。她那双摄人心魄的大眼睛把他看了几乎整整一分钟，他猛然意识到自己这副模样。
“再次谢谢您。”她缓缓地说，“您是个勇敢的人——又忠诚。”她把手低低伸出，同他相握。
可这一夜的紧张把他压垮了；他颤抖着，双手捧住她的手，吻了它。
她迅速站起来，挺直身子，起初看去几乎像是受了冒犯；待看见他盈泪的眼睛，她的眼里也闪过一种吃惊的诧异。她缓缓地又一次伸出手来，一派庄严，掌心向下，长长的手指垂着。
“您很年轻。”她说。
他确实年轻。他不过二十五岁。公主呢，整二十三岁。

## Part / 部
===Original===

IV The Maharajah of Bwodpur
1926, April﻿–﻿April, 1927
The miracle is Spring. Spring in the heart and throat of the world. Spring in Virginia, Spring in India, Spring in Chicago. Shining rain and crimson song, roll and thunder of symphony in color, shade and tint of flower and vine and budding leaf. Spring﻿—two Springs, with a little Winter between. But what if Spring dip down to Winter and die, shall not a lovelier Spring live again? Love is eternal Spring. Life lifts itself out of the Winter of death. Children sing in mud and rain and wind. Earth climbs aloft and sits astride the weeping skies.
I
The rain was falling steadily. One could hear its roar and drip and splash upon the roof. All the world was still. Kautilya listened dreamily. There was a sense of warmth and luxury about her. Silk touched and smoothed her skin. Her tired body rested on soft rugs that yielded beneath her and lay gently in every curve and crevice of her body. She heard the low music of the rain above, and the crimson, yellow, and gold of a blazing fire threw its shadows all along the walls and ceiling. The shadows turned happily and secretly, revealing and hiding the wild hues of a great picture, the reflections of a mirror, the flowers and figures of the wall. In silence she lay in strange peace and happiness﻿—not trying to think, but trying to sense the flood of the meaning of that happiness that spread above her. Her head lifted; slowly, noiselessly, with infinite tenderness, she stretched her arms toward Matthew, till his head slipped down upon her shoulder. Then, on great, slow, crimson islands of dream, the world floated away, the rain sang; and she slept again.
Long hours afterward in the silence that comes before the dim blue breaking of the dawn, Matthew awoke with a start. The rain had ceased; the fire was dim and low; a vague sense of terror gripped him. His breath struggled dizzily in his throat, and then a little shaft of sunshine, pale, clear, with a certain sweetness from the white dampness of sky and earth, wandered down from the high window and leapt and lay on the face of happiness. She lay very still, so still that at first he scarce could see the slow rise and fall of the soft silk that clung to her breast. And then the surge of joy shook him until he had to bite back the sob and wild laughter.
Hard had been their path to freedom.

===Chinese===

四　布沃德普尔土王
1926年4月至1927年4月
奇迹是春天。春天在世界的心头与喉间。弗吉尼亚的春天，印度的春天，芝加哥的春天。亮闪闪的雨，绯红的歌，色彩交响的翻滚与雷霆，花与藤与初生嫩叶的浓淡明暗。春天——两个春天，中间夹着一个小小的冬天。可倘若春天低头没入冬天而死，不会有更可爱的春天重新活过来吗？爱是永恒的春天。生命从死亡的冬里把自己举起来。孩子们在泥泞、雨水和风里歌唱。大地攀上高处，跨骑着饮泣的天空。
一
雨下个不停。听得见它打在屋顶上的轰响、滴答和飞溅。全世界都静了。考蒂利亚梦一般听着。她周身有一种温暖和奢华。丝绸抚着她，熨着她的肌肤。她疲倦的身子歇在柔软的地毯上，地毯在她身下起伏，温柔地贴进她身体的每一道曲线和皱褶。她听见头顶雨的低低的乐声，熊熊炉火的绯红、金黄与橙金把影子投满四壁和天花板。影子快活而隐秘地转动，一显一藏一幅大画上狂野的色彩，一面镜子的反光，墙上的花与人物。她默默躺着，浸在奇异的安宁和幸福里——不去想，只去感觉那漫过头顶的幸福之意义的洪流。她抬起头；缓缓地，无声地，带着无限的温柔，她朝马修伸出手臂，直到他的头滑落在她肩上。随后，在一座座巨大、缓慢、绯红的梦的岛上，世界漂走了，雨唱着歌；她又睡了。
许久之后，在黎明前那种青灰微明的寂静里，马修猛然惊醒。雨停了；炉火昏昏将熄；一阵莫名的恐怖攫住了他。他的呼吸晕眩地哽在喉咙里，随后一小束阳光，苍白，明净，带着天空和大地白色湿气里的一点甜，从高窗上游下来，跳上那张幸福的脸。她静静躺着，那样静，起初他几乎看不见贴着她胸口的软绸那缓慢的起伏。随即欢乐的巨浪摇撼他，摇得他只好咬住呜咽和狂笑。
他们通往自由的路，走得好苦。

===Original===

In his first high courage, Matthew had pictured themselves walking through that door and into the light; a powerful step, a word of defiance against the indignant, astonished, angry wave of the world. Yet in truth they had walked out with hands clasped and faces down, and he never knew what words he said or tried to say. Phrases struck upon their ears.
“﻿—knifed his race﻿—a common bawd﻿—a five-minute infatuation﻿—primitive passion﻿—”
Across the endless length of the parlor they had toiled, and down by the blazing dinner table; out far, far out into the narrowing hall; they had brushed by people who shrank away from them. Coatless, hatless, they had walked into the cold and shivering storm. And then somehow they were warm again. Then happiness had fallen softly upon them. Hand in hand they walked singing through the rain.
“Where are we going, Matthew?” she had whispered long hours later, as her tired feet faltered.
And he lifted her in his arms and raised his face to the water and answered:
“We are going down the King’s highway to Beauty and Freedom and Love. I can hear life growing down there in the earth and pulling beneath the hard sidewalks and white bones of the dead. Listen, God’s darling, to the singing of the rain; hear the dawn coming afar and see the white wings of the mist, how they beat about us.”
And so they had come home and slept in his attic nest.
Slowly Matthew lifted himself, arranged the golden glory of the Chinese rug again around her, tucking in her little feet and drawing it close at the side. Noiselessly he slipped to the fireplace and made the golden flames hiss and sputter and swirl up to the sun-drenched sky, and then he came back and stretched himself beside her, slipping, as she slept on, her head upon the curve of his elbow and looking down upon her face.
It was a magnificent face. Something had come and something had gone since the day when he saw it first. Something had gone of that incomprehensible beauty of color, infinite fineness of texture, richness of curve, loveliness of feature, which made her then to his eyes the loveliest thing in the world. But in its place there lay upon her peaceful, sleeping countenance a certain strength and nobility; a certain decision and calm, that was like beauty swept with life, like sunshine softened with mist.

===Chinese===

在他起初的昂扬勇气里，马修曾想象两人迈过那道门、走进光里：一记有力的步伐，一句向那愤怒、错愕、狂涛般的世界挑战的话。可实际上，他们手挽着手、垂着脸走了出去，他说了什么话、想说什么话，他从来不知道。只言片语打在耳边。
“——刺了自己的种族——一个人尽可夫的贱货——五分钟的痴恋——原始的情欲——”
他们穿过长得没有尽头的客厅，走过燃烧的餐桌旁；走出去，远远地，远远地走进越收越窄的门厅；他们擦过纷纷躲开的人。没穿外套，没戴帽子，他们走进寒冷战栗的风暴。随后不知怎么，他们又暖了过来。幸福轻轻落在他们身上。他们手牵手，唱着歌走过雨里。
“我们要去哪儿，马修？”几小时后，她疲倦的脚步踉跄时，低声问。
他把她抱进怀里，仰脸迎着雨水，答道：
“我们正走在国王的大道上，走向美，走向自由，走向爱。我听得见生命在地底生长，在坚硬的人行道和死人白骨下面拔动。听啊，上帝的宠儿，听雨的歌唱；听黎明从远处来，看雾的白翅膀，怎样在我们四周扑打。”
就这样他们回了家，睡在他阁楼上的窝里。
马修缓缓起身，把中国地毯金色的华彩重新围拢在她身上，把她的小脚掖好，把侧边收拢。他无声地滑到壁炉边，叫金色的火焰嘶嘶地喷溅、旋起，卷向洒满阳光的天空，然后回到她身边，在她旁边躺下，趁她睡着的当口，把她的头轻轻移进自己臂弯的弧里，俯看她的脸。
那是一张壮丽的脸。自他初见那天起，有些东西来了，有些东西去了。去了的是那种不可思议的色之美、质之无限细腻、曲线的丰饶、眉目的可爱——那些曾使她在他眼里成为世上最可爱之物的东西。但取而代之，落在她安宁睡颜上的，是一种力量与高贵；一种决断与平静，像被生命拂过的美，像被薄雾柔化的阳光。

===Original===

The heavy coils of purple hair had been cut away, and yet the hair still lay thick and strong upon her forehead.
Suddenly he wanted to see her eyes, the eyes that he had never forgotten since first he looked into them, eyes that were pools at once of mystery and revelation, misty with half-sensed desire, and calm with power. He wanted to see her eyes again and see them at once with the high consciousness of birth that belonged to the Princess of the Lützower Ufer and with that look of surrender and selfless love that he had caught in the little room behind the parlor. He wanted again desperately to see those eyes which said all these things; yet he lay very still lest for a single moment he should disturb her. And then he looked down, and her eyes were looking up to him. Slowly and happily she smiled.
“Krishna,” she murmured. His mind went racing back through the shadows and he whispered back, “Radha.” And again they slept.
When Kautilya awoke again, there was a slow music stealing in from the inner room. It was the andante from Beethoven’s Fifth Symphony, infinite in tenderness, triumph, and beauty; and it came from afar so that no scratch of the phonograph or creak of mechanism spoiled its sweet melody. She sat up suddenly with a little cry of joy, throwing aside the great Chinese rug and swathing herself in the silk of the white mandarin’s robe that lay ready for her. With this music in her ears she found the bathroom with its tub of steaming water and with its completeness, half plaintive with neglect.
A half hour later she found new silken things in the dressing-room. The rug lay upon the floor, and the old worn easy-chair was drawn before the fire. Beside the flaming dance of the fire was a low, white Turkish taboret; toward it Matthew came, clad in an old green bathrobe which hung carelessly along his tall body. There was a tarboosh with faded tassel upon his head. The hot coffee steamed on the salver, with toast and butter and cream. There was an orange in halves and a little yellow rosebud. She laughed in the sheer delight of it all, and held him long and close before they turned to their eating. The morning sun poured in.
The music was changed to that largo of Dvorák built on the echoing pain of the Negro folksong, which is printed on the other side of the Victor record.

===Chinese===

厚重的紫色发卷剪掉了，然而额上的头发依旧又密又韧。
忽然他想看她的眼睛。自第一次望进去，他从未忘记过的眼睛；那是一泓神秘兼启示的深潭，半醒的欲望使它朦胧，权力使它平静。他想再看她的眼睛，一眼看尽两样东西：吕措河岸公主那与生俱来的高贵自觉，同他在客厅后面小屋里捕捉到的那副交付与忘我之爱的神情。他不顾一切地想再看那双说着这一切的眼睛；可他静静躺着，生怕有一刻惊扰她。随后他低头看去，她的眼睛正仰望着他。她缓缓地、幸福地笑了。
“克里希纳，”她喃喃道。他的心跑马一样穿过重重暗影，回她一句：“罗陀。”两人又睡了。
考蒂利亚再醒来时，一阵缓缓的乐声从里屋渗进来。是贝多芬第五交响曲的行板，温柔、凯旋与美，无穷无尽；乐声来自远处，唱针的沙沙同机械的吱呀一点败坏不了它的甜。她欢叫一声霍然坐起，甩开大块的中国地毯，把自己裹进那件早已备下的白色满大人袍的丝绸里。和着耳边的乐声，她找到浴室，一缸蒸汽腾腾的热水，一应俱全，带着久被冷落的几分幽怨。
半小时后，她在更衣室里找到新的丝质衣物。地毯铺在地上，旧的破安乐椅拉到炉前。炉火烈舞的旁边，是一张低矮的白色土耳其小圆凳；马修朝它走来，身上一件旧绿浴袍，松松垮垮挂在他高大的身上。他头上戴着一顶流苏褪色的塔布什帽。银盘上热咖啡冒着气，配着吐司、黄油同奶油。一只对半切开的橙子，一朵小小的黄玫瑰苞。她为这一切欢笑出纯粹的欢喜，在他们转向食物之前，久久地紧紧拥抱。早晨的阳光倾泻进来。
音乐换成了德沃夏克那支广板，建立在黑人民谣回声般的痛苦之上，就印在胜利唱片的那一面。

===Original===

Matthew would not let her stir, and after a while from the kitchen came a brave splashing of dishes and a song. He came back soon, bringing an old volume of poems. Without a word, only a long look, they nestled in the chair near the fire and read. And so the day passed half wordless with beauty and sound, full of color and content, until the sunlight went crimson and blue upon the walls and the fire shadows danced again.
“There are so many things I would ask you,” said Matthew. And then Kautilya took his head between her hands and laid the breadth of his shoulders upon her knees and said:
“And there are so many things I want to tell you of myself. I want to tell you all the story of my life; of my falling and rising; of my love for you; and of that mother of yours who lives far down in Virginia in the cabin by the wood. Oh, Matthew, you have a wonderful mother. Have you seen her hands? Have you seen the gnarled and knotted glory of her hands?” And then slowly with wide eyes: “Your mother is Kali, the Black One; wife of Siva, Mother of the World!”
II
Matthew was talking in the darkness as they lay together closely entwined in each other’s arms.
“You will tell me, dear one, all about yourself? How came you here, masquerading as a trade union leader?”
“It must be a long, long story, Matthew﻿—a Thousand Nights and a Night?”
“So short a tale? Talk on, Scheherazade!”
So Kautilya whispered, nestling in his arms:
“But first, Matthew, sing me that Song of Emancipation﻿—sing that Call of God, ‘Go down, Moses!’ I believe, though I did not then know it﻿—I believe I began to love you that night.”
Matthew sang. Kautilya whispered:
“When you left me and went to jail, I seemed first to awake to real life. From clouds I came suddenly down to earth. I knew the fault was mine and the sacrifice yours. I left the country according to my promise to the government. But it was easy to engineer my quick return from London in a Cunard second cabin, without my title or real name.
“And then came what I shall always know to have been the greatest thing in my life. I saw your mother. No faith nor religion, Matthew, ever dies. I am of the clan and land that gave Gotama, the Buddha, to the world. I know that out of the soul of Brahma come little separations of his perfect and ineffable self and they appear again and again in higher and higher manifestations, as eternal life flows on.

===Chinese===

马修不让她动弹，过了一会儿，厨房里传来一阵勇敢的杯盘声和一支歌。他很快回来，捧着一卷旧诗集。不说一句话，只对望一眼长长，他们偎进炉边的椅子里读。这一天就这样半无言地过去，满是美与声，满是色彩与餍足，直到阳光在墙上转成绯红与青蓝，炉影又跳起舞来。
“有那么多事要问你。”马修说。考蒂利亚双手捧起他的头，把他宽阔的肩膀放上自己的膝头，说：
“也有那么多事，我要讲我自己。我要把我一生的故事全讲给你：我的沉沦与奋起；我对你的爱；还有你那位母亲，她远远住在弗吉尼亚林边的小屋里。哦，马修，你有一位了不起的母亲。你看过她的手吗？你看过她那双手疙瘩盘结的荣耀吗？”随后她睁大眼睛，慢慢地说：“你的母亲就是迦梨，那位黑肤的；湿婆之妻，世界之母！”
二
黑暗里，两人紧紧相拥着躺在一起，马修说着话。
“你会把你自己的一切都讲给我听吧，亲爱的？你怎么会到这儿来，扮成一个工会领袖？”
“那得是个很长很长的故事，马修——一千零一夜那么长？”
“这么短的故事？讲下去吧，山鲁佐德！”
于是考蒂利亚偎在他怀里，低声讲：
“可先要，马修，给我唱那首解放之歌——唱那支上帝的呼召，‘下去吧，摩西！’我相信——虽然那时我还不明白——我相信，那晚我开始爱上你了。”
马修唱了。考蒂利亚低语：
“你离我去坐牢，我好像头一回真正醒了过来。我从云端猛然坠到地上。我知道错在我，牺牲在你。我遵守对政府的诺言出了国。可是从伦敦凭一张冠达二等舱的票，不带头衔不露真名，很快折返回来，容易安排得很。
“接着来了那件事，我将永远知道它是我一生里最伟大的事。我见到了你的母亲。没有哪种信仰哪种宗教，马修，会真正死去。我出自把乔达摩佛陀给予世界的部族和土地。我知道，从梵的灵魂里，会分出他完美而无以名之的自我的小小碎片，随着永恒生命的流淌，一次又一次以越来越高的一重重现。

===Original===

And when I saw that old mother of yours standing in the blue shadows of twilight with flowers, cotton, and corn about her, I knew that I was looking upon one of the ancient prophets of India and that she was to lead me out of the depths in which I found myself and up to the atonement for which I yearned. So I started with her upon that path of seven years which I calculated would be, in all likelihood, the measure of your possible imprisonment. We talked it all out together. We prayed to God, hers and mine, and out of her ancient lore she did the sacrifice of flame and blood which was the ceremony of my own great fathers and which came down to her from Shango of Western Africa.
“You had stepped down into menial service at my request﻿—you who knew how hard and dreadful it was. It was now my turn to step down to the bottom of the world and see it for myself. So I put aside my silken garments and cut my hair, and, selling my jewels, I started out on the long path which should lead to you. I did not write you. Why did I need to? You were myself, I knew. But I sent others, who kept watch over you and sent me news.
“First, I went as a servant girl into the family of a Richmond engineer.”
Matthew started abruptly, but Kautilya nestled closer to him and watched him with soft eyes.
“It was difficult,” she said, “but necessary. I had known, all my life, service, but not servants. I had not been able to imagine what it meant to be a servant. Most of my life I had not dreamed that it meant anything; that servants meant anything to themselves. But now I served. I made beds, I swept, I brought food to the table, sometimes I helped cook it. I hated to clean kitchens amid dirt and heat, and I worked long hours; but at night I slept happily, dear, by the very ache of the new muscles and nerves which my body revealed.
“Then came the thing of which your mother had warned me, but which somehow I did not sense or see coming, until in the blackness of the night suddenly I knew that someone was moving in my room; that someone had entered my unlocked door.”
Matthew arose suddenly and paced the floor. Then he came and sat down by the couch and held both her hands.
“Go on,” he said.
“I sat up tense and alert and held in my hand the long, light dagger with its curved handle and curious chasing; a dagger which the grandfathers of my grandfather had handed down to me.

===Chinese===

当我看见你那位年老的母亲立在暮色蓝影里，四下是花、棉花和玉米，我知道我正望着印度的一位古先知，她要领我走出我沉陷的深渊，登上我渴望的救赎之路。于是我随她踏上那条七年之路；我算计过，那多半正是你可能的刑期。我们把一切商量了个透。我们向上帝祷告，她的上帝，也是我的；她凭她的古老秘术，行了火与血的祭——那是我自己伟大的先人们的仪轨，传到她那里，又是从西非的尚戈一脉传下的。
“你应我的请求，屈身去做了下人的差事——你明知道那有多苦多可怕。如今轮到我屈身，下到世界的底层，亲眼去看它。于是我脱下丝绸衣裳，剪了头发，变卖了珠宝，踏上那条通向你的漫漫长路。我没有写信给你。何必要写？你就是我自己，这我知道。可我派了别人，守着你，给我递消息。
“起初，我去做使女，进了里士满一个工程师的家。”
马修猛地一动，考蒂利亚却偎得更紧，用温柔的眼睛望着他。
“很苦，”她说，“可必须。我一生知道仆役，却不知道做仆役。我从来想象不出做仆役是什么滋味。大半辈子我连想都没想过那意味着什么；想过仆役对他们自己意味着什么。可如今我伺候人。我铺床，我扫地，我把饭菜端上桌，有时还帮着做。我恨在油腻同燠热里打扫厨房，我一天做许多钟头的工；可夜里我睡得香甜，亲爱的，凭着我身体里新醒的肌肉与神经的酸楚。
“接着来了那件事——你母亲警告过我，可我不知怎的没有觉出它、看见它来——直到黑夜的黑暗里，我忽然知道有人在摸进我的房间；有人推开了我没闩的门。”
马修霍然起身，在地板上踱步。随后他走回来，在睡榻边坐下，握住她双手。
“讲下去。”他说。
“我绷紧了坐起，手里攥着那把轻巧的长匕首，弯柄，錾着古怪的花纹；是我祖父的祖父传给我的。

===Original===

That night, Matthew, I was near murder, but the white man, my employer, slipped as I lunged, and the dagger caught only the end of his left eye and came down clean across cheek, mouth, and chin, one inch from the great jugular vein, just as the mistress with her electric torch came in.
“Instead of arrest which I thought I surely faced, the man was hurried out and off and the woman came to me in the still morning, worn and pale, and said, ‘I thank you. This is perhaps the lesson he needed.’
“She paid me my little wage and I walked away.”
“But, Kautilya, why, why did you go through all this? What possible good could it do?”
“Matthew, it was written. I went to Petersburg and worked in a tobacco factory, sitting cramped at a long bench, stripping the soft fragrant tobacco leaf from those rough stems. It was not in itself hard work, but the close air, the cramped position, the endless monotony, made me at times want to scream. And there were the people about me: some good and broken; some harsh, hard, and wild. Leering men, loud-mouthed women. I stayed there three endless months until it seemed to me that every delicate thought and tender feeling and sense of beauty had been bent and crushed beyond recognition. So I took the train and came to Philadelphia.
“I worked in two restaurants; one on Walnut Street, splendid and beautiful. The patrons usually were kind and thoughtful, with only now and then an overdressed woman who had to express her superiority by the loudness of her tones, or a man who was slyly insulting or openly silly. Only the kitchen and the corridors bruised me by their contrast and ugliness. Singularly enough in this place of food and plenty, the only proper food we waitresses could get to eat was stolen food. I hated the stealing, but I was hungry and tired. From there I went down to South Street to a colored restaurant and worked a long time. It was an easygoing place with poor food and poor people, but kind. They crowded in at all hours, They were well-meaning, inquisitive; and if a busy workingman or a well-dressed idler sought to take my hand or touch my body he did it half jokingly and usually not twice.”
“Servant, tobacco-hand, waitress; mud, dirt, and servility for the education of a queen,” groaned Matthew.
“And is there any field where a queen’s education is more neglected? Think what I learned of the mass of men!

===Chinese===

那一夜，马修，我险些杀了人；可那白人，我的雇主，在我扑刺时滑了一下，匕首只刮到他左眼眼梢，顺着面颊、嘴、下巴干净地划下来，离颈上的大血管只一寸——恰在那时，太太端着手电筒闯了进来。
“没有等来我自认必定的拘捕，那男人被匆匆弄出门去；清晨，那位太太来到我面前，憔悴而苍白，说：‘谢谢你。这也许正是他需要的一课。’
“她付了我那点工钱，我走掉了。”
“可是，考蒂利亚，为什么，为什么你要受这些？能有什么用？”
“马修，这是写定的。我去彼得斯堡，进了烟厂，缩着身子在长凳前一坐一天，把柔软芳香的烟叶从粗梗上剥下来。活计本身不重，可窒闷的空气、蜷缩的姿势、无尽的单调，有时叫我真想尖叫。还有我四周的人：有的善良而破碎；有的粗暴、狠硬、狂野。斜眼瞟人的男人，大嗓门的女人。我在那里熬了没完没了的三个月，直到我觉得每一种纤细的念头、温柔的情感、对美的感觉，都被压弯、碾碎，认不出来了。于是我上火车，去了费城。
“我在两家馆子做工；一家在胡桃街，堂皇漂亮。主顾多半和气周到，只偶尔有个穿着过头的女人，非得用嗓门宣示她的优越，或者一个男人，阴损地侮辱人，或者公开犯傻。只有厨房同走廊，以它们的反差同丑陋硌疼我。奇怪的是，在这个满是食物同丰足的地方，我们这些女侍唯一能正经吃到的饭食，竟是偷来的。我恨偷，可我又饿又累。从那里我南下南街，进了一家有色人馆子，做了很久。那是个散漫地方，粗茶淡饭，穷人，但和善。他们什么钟点都挤进来。他们心眼好，爱打听；倘若哪个忙着的工人或者穿得体面的闲汉想拉我的手、碰我的身子，他也多半半开玩笑，而且通常不会有第二次。”
“使女、烟草工、女侍；泥泞、肮脏同卑屈，给一位女王上的课。”马修呻吟道。
“哪里还有哪一块田地，更亏待女王的课业？想想我学到的关于大众的一切吧！

===Original===

I got to know the patrons: their habits, hardships, histories. I was the friend of the proprietors, woman and husband; but the enterprise didn’t pay. It failed. I cried. But just as it was closing I learned of your release, and after but a year, suddenly I was in heaven. I thought I had already atoned.
“But I knew that yet I must wait. That you must find your way and begin to adjust your life before I dared come into it again. And so I went to New York, that my dream of life and of the meaning of life to the mass of men might be more complete.
“I discovered a paper box-making factory on the lower East Side. It was a nonunion shop and I worked in a basement that stank of glue and waste, ten and twelve hours a day for six dollars a week. It was sweated labor of the lowest type, and I was aghast. Then the workers tried to organize﻿—there was a strike. I was beaten and jailed for picketing, but I did not care. That which was begun as a game and source of experience to me became suddenly real life. I became an agent, organizer, and officer of the union. I knew my fellow laborers, in home and on street, in factory and restaurant. I studied the industry and the law, I traveled, made speeches, and organized. Oh, Matthew it was life, life, real life, even with the squalor and hard toil.”
“Yes, it was life. And the Veil of Color lifted from your eyes as it is lifting even from my blindness. Those people there, these here they are all alike, all one. They are all foolish, ignorant, and exploited. Their highest ambition is to escape from themselves﻿—from being black, from being poor, from being ugly into some high heaven from which they can gaze down and despise themselves.”
“True, my Matthew, and while I was learning all this which you long knew, you seemed to me striving to unlearn. Oh, how I watched over you! You came down to Virginia. Hidden in the forest, I watched with wet eyes. Hidden in the cabin, I heard your voice. I caught the sob in your throat when your mammy told of my coming. I knew you loved me still, and I wanted to rush into your arms. But, ‘Not yet﻿—not yet!’ said your wise old mother.
“I was working busily and happily when the second blow fell, the blow that came to deny everything, that seemed to say that you were not self of my own self and life of the life which I was sharing in every pulse with you. You married. I gave up.”

===Chinese===

我认得了那些主顾：他们的习惯、艰难、来历。我做了老板娘同她男人的朋友；可这买卖不赚钱，倒了。我哭了。可就在关张的时候，我听说你获释；才过了一年，我忽然上了天。我以为我已经赎完了罪。
“可我知道还得等。你必须先找到自己的路，先安顿你的生活，我才敢再走进它。于是我去了纽约，让我对生命、对生命之于大众的意义的梦，更完整些。
“我在下东区寻到一家纸盒厂。那是不挂工会的厂子，我在一间胶水同废料臭气熏天的地下室做工，一天十到十二个钟头，一礼拜六块钱。那是最低等的血汗劳动，我骇然。随后工人们要组织起来——罢工了。我因为纠察挨了打、坐了牢，可我不在乎。那桩以游戏同阅历开始的事，忽然变成了真实的生活。我做了工会的代理、组织者同职员。我认得了我的劳工同伴，在他们在家里，在街上，在厂里同馆子里。我研究这门工业同法律，我旅行、演讲、组织。哦，马修，那是生活，生活，真实的生活，连同它的腌臜同苦役。”
“是，那是生活。肤色的帷幕从你眼前掀开了，正如它也在从我的盲目里掀开。那边那些人，这边这些人——他们全都一样，全是一个。他们都愚蠢、无知、被剥削。他们最高的志向，是从他们自己那里逃出去——逃出黑，逃出穷，逃出丑，逃进某个高高在上的天国，好从那里俯视并轻蔑他们自己。”
“不错，我的马修。而当我学着这一切你早就知道的东西时，你在我眼里却在拼命地忘掉它。哦，我多么守望着你！你回了弗吉尼亚。我藏在林子里，含泪看着。我藏在小屋里，听见你的声音。你的老妈妈讲起我来的时候，我听见你喉咙里哽着的那声哭。我知道你仍旧爱我，我想扑进你的怀抱。可是，‘还不到时候——还不到时候！’你那位智慧的老母亲说。
“我正忙忙碌碌、快快活活地做工，第二记打击落了下来——那记否认一切的打击，仿佛在说，你不是我自己血中之血、我同你一脉一息相共的那生命之生命。你结婚了。我放弃了。

===Original===

“You did not understand, Kautilya. You seemed lost to me forever. I was blindly groping for some counterfeit of peace. If I had only known you were here and caring!”
“I went down again to Virginia and knelt beside your mother, and she only smiled. ‘He ain’t married,’ she said. ‘He only thinks he is. He was wild like, and didn’t know where to turn or what to do. Wait, wait.’
“I waited. You would not listen to my messengers whom continually I sent to you﻿—the statesmen of Japan, the Chinese, the groping president of the Box-Makers. Like Galahad you would not ask the meaning of the sign. You would not name my name. How could I know, dearest, what I meant to you? And yet my thought and care hovered and watched over you. I knew Sammy and Sara and I saw your slow and sure descent to hell. I tried to save you by sending human beings to you. You helped them, but you did not know them. I tried again when you were sitting in the legislature down at Springfield. You knew, but you would not understand. You sneered at the truth. You would not come at my call.”
“I did not know it was your voice, Kautilya.”
“You knew the voice of our cause, Matthew﻿—was that not my voice?”
Matthew was silent. Kautilya stroked his hand.
“We met in London, the leaders of a thousand million of the darker peoples, with, for the first time, black Africa and black America sitting beside the rest. I was proud of the Negroes we had chosen after long search. There were to be forty of us, and, Matthew, only you were absent. I looked for you to the last. It seemed that you must come. We organized, we planned, and one great new thing emerged﻿—your word, Matthew, your prophecy: we recognized democracy as a method of discovering real aristocracy. We looked frankly forward to raising not all the dead, sluggish, brutalized masses of men, but to discovering among them genius, gift, and ability in far larger number than among the privileged and ruling classes. Search, weed out, encourage; educate, train, and open all doors! Democracy is not an end; it is a method of aristocracy. Some day I will show you all we said and planned.
“All the time, until I left for this great meeting I had expected that somehow, some way, all would be well. Some time suddenly you would come away. You would understand and burst your bonds and come to us﻿—to me. But as I left America fear entered my heart﻿—fear for your soul.

===Chinese===

“你不明白，考蒂利亚。你于我像是永远失去了。我在盲目地摸索某种和平的赝品。我要是早知道你就在这里、还在挂念，该多好！”
“我再下弗吉尼亚，跪在你母亲身边，她只是笑。‘他没有结婚，’她说，‘他只当自己结了。他那阵子野了性子，不知往哪儿转、该做什么。等吧，等吧。’
“我等了。你不肯听我不断派去的信使——日本的政要们、中国人、制箱工会那位摸索着的会长。你像加拉哈德，不肯问那神示的意义。你不肯说出我的名字。最亲爱的，我怎能知道我之于你是什么？然而我的思念同挂怀始终盘旋着，守望着你。我认得萨米同萨拉，我看着你缓缓而确实地坠向地狱。我试着派人去救你。你帮助他们，却不认得他们。你坐在斯普林菲尔德的州议会里时，我又试了一次。你知道了，却不肯明白。你讥笑真相。你不肯应我的召唤。”
“我不知道那是你的声音，考蒂利亚。”
“你知道我们事业的声音，马修——那不就是我的声音？”
马修沉默了。考蒂利亚抚着他的手。
“我们在伦敦集会，十亿深色民族的领袖们，破天荒第一次，黑色非洲同黑色美洲同众人并肩而坐。我为我们千挑万选的黑人代表骄傲。我们该有四十人，马修，独独缺你。我盼望你到最后一刻。你仿佛必然会来。我们组织，我们规划，一件伟大的新东西浮现出来——你的那个词，马修，你的预言：我们承认，民主是发现真正贵族的方法。我们坦率地展望：不是要举起全数死的、沉滞的、被兽化的众生，而是要在他们中间，发掘出比特权者同统治阶级多得多的天才、禀赋与才干。搜寻，甄拔，激励；教育，训练，打开所有的门！民主不是目的；它是贵族的方法。改日我把我们说过、计划过的一切都给你看。
“直到动身来赴这次大会，我一直指望事情总会好起来，以某种方式、在某条路上。你会忽然醒悟走开。你会明白，挣断枷锁，到我们这里来——到我这里来。可我离开美国时，恐惧进了我的心——为你的灵魂恐惧。

===Original===

I began to feel that I must act﻿—I must take the step, I must rescue you from the net in which you were floundering.
“I remember the day. Gloom of fog held back the March spring in London. The crowded, winding streets echoed with traffic. I heard Big Ben knelling the hour of noon, and a ray of sunlight struggled dizzily on the mauve Thames. A wireless came. You were selling your soul for Congress.
“Before, you had stolen for others. You had upheld their lies﻿—but your own hands were clean, your heart disclaimed the dirty game. Now you were going to lie and steal for yourself. I saw the end of our world. I must rescue you at any cost﻿—at any sacrifice. I rushed back across the sea. Five days we shivered, rolled, and darted through the storm. Almost we cut a ship in two on the Newfoundland banks, but wrenched away with a mighty groan. I landed Friday morning, and left at two-fifty-five﻿—at nine next morning I was in Chicago. That night I led your soul up from Purgatory﻿—free!
“And here we are, Matthew, my love; and it is long past the hour of sleep; and you are trembling with apprehension at things which did not happen, at pits into which I did not fall, at failures over which we both have triumphed.”
The Princess paused, and Matthew started up. There was a loud insistent knocking at the door.
“Go,” said the Princess. “Have we not both expected this?”
Matthew hesitated a moment and then walked to the door and opened it. A colored police officer and two white men in citizens’ clothes stepped in quickly and started as if to search, until they saw the Princess sitting on the disheveled bed.
“Well?”
“We were hunting for you two,” said one of the plainclothes men.
“And you have found us?” asked Matthew.
“Yes, evidently. We wondered where you were spending the night.”
“We were spending the night here, together,” said Matthew.
“Together,” repeated the Princess.
The other man began to write furiously.
“You admit that,” said the first man.
“We admit it,” said Matthew, and the Princess bowed her head.
“Perhaps we had better look around a little,” said the other man tentatively. But the policeman protested.
“You got what you wanted, ain’t ya? Mr. Towns is a friend of mine, and I don’t propose to have no monkey business. If you’re through, get out.” And slowly they all passed through the door.
III
May, and five o’clock in the morning.

===Chinese===

我开始觉得我必须行动——必须迈出这一步，必须把你从那张挣扎的罗网里救出来。
“我记得那一天。雾的阴翳在伦敦拖住三月的春。拥挤蜿蜒的街道回响着车马。我听见大本钟敲响正午，一线阳光晕眩地挣扎在淡紫的泰晤士河上。一封无线电报来了。你正为进国会出卖灵魂。
“从前，你为别人偷。你撑着他们的谎——可你自己的手是干净的，你的心否认那肮脏的把戏。如今你要为你自己撒谎、偷窃。我看见我们世界的末日。我必须救你，不惜任何代价——任何牺牲。我奔回来，横渡大海。五天里我们在风暴中颤抖、翻滚、疾窜。在纽芬兰浅滩我们险些把一条船拦腰撞成两截，一声巨吼硬生生把船头拧开了。星期五早晨我登陆，两点五十五分上车——次日早晨九点，我已在芝加哥。那天夜里，我把你的灵魂从炼狱里领了上来——自由了！
“而我们在这里了，马修，我的爱；早过了睡的钟点；你还在为那些没有发生的事、我没有跌进去的坑、我们俩都已凯旋的失败，吓得发抖。”
公主顿住，马修霍然起身。门上响起急促的大声敲门。
“去吧，”公主说，“我们不是早就都料到了吗？”
马修迟疑片刻，走向房门，开了门。一个有色警官同两个便衣白人疾步进来，作势就要搜查，直到看见公主坐在凌乱的床上。
“怎么样？”
“我们正找你们两个。”一个便衣说。
“你们找到了？”马修问。
“是啊，明摆着。我们纳闷你们在哪儿过夜。”
“我们就在这儿过的夜，在一起。”马修说。
“在一起。”公主重复道。
另一个人开始飞快地写。
“你们承认这一点。”第一个人说。
“我们承认。”马修说，公主垂下了头。
“也许我们最好稍微看看四周。”另一个人试探着说。可那警官不干了。
“你们要到手的不是到手了吗？唐斯先生是我的朋友，我不打算让谁胡来。完了就出去。”于是他们慢慢地一个个出了门。
三
五月，清晨五点。

===Original===

The sun was whispering to the night, and the mist of its words rose above the park. Matthew and Kautilya swung rapidly along through the dim freshness of the day. They both had knapsacks and knickerbockers, and shoulder to shoulder, hand in hand, and singing low snatches of song, they hurried through Jackson Park. It was such a morning as when the world began: soft with breezes, warm yet cold, brilliant with the sun, and still dripping with the memory of sweet, clean rain. There was no dust﻿—no noise, no movement. Almost were the great brown earth and heavy, terrible city, still. Singing, quivering, tense with awful happiness, they went through the world. Far out by the lake and in the drowsy afternoon, when they had eaten sausage and bread and herbs and drunk cool water, after Kautilya had read the sacred words of the Rig-veda, she laid aside the books and talked again, straining his back against her knees as they sat beneath a black oak tree, her cheek beside his ear, while together they stared out upon the waving waters of Lake Michigan.
Matthew said:
“Now tell me beautiful things, Scheherazade. Who you are and what? And from what fairyland you came?”
“I cannot tell you, Matthew, for you do not know India. Oh, my dear one, you must know India.
“India! India! Out of black India the world was born. Into the black womb of India the world shall creep to die. All that the world has done, India did, and that more marvelously, more magnificently. The loftiest of mountains, the mightiest of rivers, the widest of plains, the broadest of oceans﻿—these are India.
“Man is there of every shape and kind and hue, and the animal friends of man, of every sort conceivable. The drama of life knows India as it knows no other land, from the tragedy of Almighty God to the laugh of the Bandar-log; from divine Gotama to the sons of Mahmoud and the stepsons of the Christ.
“For leaf and sun, for whiff and whirlwind; for laughter, and for tears; for sacrifice and vision; for stark poverty and jeweled wealth; for toil and song and silence﻿—for all this, know India. Loveliest and weirdest of lands; terrible with flame and ice, beautiful with palm and pine, home of pain and happiness and misery﻿—oh, Matthew, can you not understand? This is India﻿—can you not understand?”
“No, I cannot understand; but I feel your meaning.”
“True, true! India must be felt.

===Chinese===

太阳正同黑夜耳语，它的话语化成的雾升在公园上空。马修同考蒂利亚在清晨微明的鲜润里快步前行。两人都背着背包，穿着灯笼裤，肩并肩，手牵手，低低哼着歌的片段，匆匆穿过杰克逊公园。那是一个世界初创之晨：软风，微暖还凉，阳光灿亮，还滴滴答答带着甜净雨水的记忆。没有尘土——没有噪音，没有骚动。棕色的大地同沉重可怕的城市，几乎都静止。唱着，颤着，因骇人的幸福而绷紧着，他们穿行世界。远远地到了湖边，到了昏昏欲睡的午后，吃罢了香肠、面包同香草，喝罢了凉水，考蒂利亚诵过《梨俱吠陀》的圣文，她把书搁到一边，又说起话来；他们坐在一棵黑橡树下，她让他把背抵着她的膝，她的颊贴着他的耳，两人一起望着密歇根湖翻涌的水光。
马修说：
“现在给我讲些美的东西吧，山鲁佐德。你是谁，是什么人？从哪一片仙境来？”
“我讲不清，马修，因为你不认识印度。哦，我亲爱的，你必须认识印度。
“印度！印度！世界从黑色的印度里诞生。世界还要爬进印度黑色的子宫里死去。世界做过的一切，印度都做过，而且做得更奇、更壮。最高的山，最大的河，最阔的平原，最广的洋——这些就是印度。
“那里的人，形形色色，每一种模样、族类同肤色；人的兽中之友，凡想得到的每一种。生命的戏剧，认识印度胜过认识任何别的土地：从全能上帝的悲剧，到班达罗格猴群的哄笑；从神圣的乔达摩，到马哈茂德的子嗣同基督的继子。
“为叶与阳光，为微风与旋风；为笑，为泪；为牺牲与远见；为赤贫与珠玉之富；为劳作、歌与静默——为了这一切，去认识印度。最美也最怪的土地；以冰与火为可怖，以棕榈同松柏为美丽，痛与福与苦楚之乡——哦，马修，你难道不懂么？这就是印度——你难道不懂？”
“不，我不懂；可我感得到你的意思。”
“对，对！印度必须去感觉。

===Original===

No man can know India, and yet the shame of it, that men may today be counted learned and yet be ignorant, carelessly ignorant, of India. The shame, that this vast center of human life should be but the daubed footstool of a stodgy island of shopkeepers born with seas and hearts of ice.”
“But you know India, darling. Tell India to the world.”
“I am India. Forgive me, dearest, if I play with words beyond meaning﻿—beyond the possibility of meaning. Now let me talk of myself﻿—of my little self﻿—”
“That is more to me than all India and all the world besides.”
“I was born with the new century. My childhood was a dream, a dream of power, beauty, and delight. Before my face rose every morning the white glory of the high Himalayas, with the crowning mass of Gaurisankar, kissing heaven. Behind me lay the great and golden flood of Holy Ganga. On my left hand stood the Bo of Buddha and on my right the Sacred City of the Maghmela.
“All about me was royal splendor, wealth and jewels and beautiful halls, old and priceless carpets, the music of tinkling fountains, the song and flash of birds; and when I clapped my childish hands, servants crawled to me on their faces. Of course, much that I know now was missing﻿—little comforts of the West; and there were poverty, pain, sickness, and death; but with all this, around me everywhere was marble, gold and jewels, silk and fur, and myriads who danced and sang and served me. For I was the little Princess of Bwodpur, the last of a line that had lived and ruled a thousand years.
“We came out of the black South in ancient days and ruled in Rajputana; and then, scorning the yoke of the Aryan invaders, moved to Bwodpur, and there we gave birth to Buddha, black Buddha of the curly hair. Six million people worshiped us as divine, and my father’s revenue was three hundred lakhs of rupees. I had strange and mighty playthings: elephants and lions and tigers, great white oxen and flashing automobiles. Parks there were and palaces, baths and sweet waters, and amid it all I walked a tiny and willful thing, curbed only by my old father, the Maharajah, and my white English governess, whom I passionately loved.

===Chinese===

没有人能认识印度；而耻辱的是，如今人可以算作博学了，却对印度无知——漫不经心地无知。耻辱啊，这人类生命的浩大中心，竟只配做一座小岛的马虎涂抹的脚凳——那岛上住着一伙天生揣着冰海冰心的店主。
“可你认识印度，亲爱的。把印度讲给世界。”
“我就是印度。原谅我，最亲爱的，如果我在玩弄超出意义的字眼——超出可能有的意义。现在让我讲讲我自己——讲讲我小小的自己——”
“那对我来说，比整个印度、整个世界加起来还重。”
“我随新世纪的头一年出生。我的童年是一场梦，一场权势、美与欢乐的梦。每天清晨在我面前升起的，是高峻喜马拉雅的白色荣光，顶着高里桑卡尔的冠冕之峰，亲吻着天。我身后横卧圣恒河金色的浩荡洪流。我左手立着佛陀的菩提树，右手立着马格梅拉的圣城。
“我的四围是王家的煊赫，财富、珠宝同华美的殿堂，古旧无价的地毯，喷泉淙淙的音乐，鸟的鸣啭与闪光；我一拍童稚的手掌，仆役们便脸贴着地爬向我。当然，如今我知道的许多东西那时都还没有——西方的小小舒适；那里也有贫困、病痛与死亡；可纵然如此，我四围到处是大理石、黄金与珠宝，丝绸与皮裘，还有成千上万为我歌舞、伺候我的人。因为我就是布沃德普尔的小公主，一个传承千年、统治千年的谱系之末。
“我们在上古从黑色的南方出来，在拉杰普塔纳立国；后来，不屑于雅利安征服者的轭，迁到布沃德普尔；在那里，我们诞下了佛陀——黑色卷发的佛陀。六百万人民奉我们为神，我父亲的岁入有三千万卢比。我有奇特而威风的玩物：象、狮、虎，高大的白牛同闪亮的汽车。有园林同宫殿，浴场同甘泉，而在这一切中间，我走动，一个小小的、任性的人儿，只受两样管束：我的老父亲，土王，同我狂热爱着的英国白人女教师。

===Original===

“I had, of course, my furious revolts: wild rebellion at little crossings of my will; wild delight at some of the efforts to amuse me; and then came the culmination when first the flood of life stopped long enough for me to look it full in the face.
“I was twelve and according to the ancient custom of our house I was to be married at a great Durbar. He was a phantom prince, a pale and sickly boy who reached scarcely to my shoulder. But his dominion joined with mine, making a mighty land of twelve million souls; of wealth in gold and jewels, high mineral walls, and valleys fat with cream. All that I liked, and I wanted to be a crowned and reigning Maharanee. But I did not like this thin, scared stick of a boy whose pearls and diamonds seemed to drag him down and make his dark eyes shine terrorstricken beneath his splendid turban.
“I enjoyed the magnificent betrothal ceremony and poked impish fun at the boy who seemed such a child. A tall and crimson Englishman attended him and ran his errands and I felt very grand, riding high on my silken elephant amid applauding thousands. The ‘Fringies,’ as we called the English, were here in large numbers and always whispering in the background, nodding politely, playing with me gravely, and yielding to my whims. I confess I thought them very wonderful. I set them, unconsciously, above my own people.
“I remember hearing and but half understanding the talk of my guardian and counselors. They were apparently vastly surprised that the English had allowed this marriage. It would seem the English had long resisted the wishes of the people of Sindrabad. They had, you see, more power in his land than in ours. Our land was independent﻿—or at least we thought so. To be sure we sent no ministers to foreign lands﻿—but what did we know or care of foreign lands? To be sure our trade was monopolized by the English, but it was good and profitable trade. Internally we were free and unmolested, save that an unobtrusive Englishman was always at court. He was the Resident. He ‘advised’ us and spied upon us, as I now know.
“Now it was different in Sindrabad, where my little prince ruled under English advisers. Sindrabad was in the iron grip of the English. They long frowned upon the power of Bwodpur, a native, half-independent Indian state.

===Chinese===

“我自然也闹过我那些狂暴的反抗：意志稍受拂逆便狂野地反叛；人们哄我的某些法子又叫我喜欢得发狂；随后来了顶点：生命的洪流头一回停得够久，容我把它的脸看个正着。
“我十二岁，按我们家族的古俗，我要在一个盛大的杜尔巴上成婚。他是个幻影王子，一个苍白孱弱的男孩，个头几乎齐不到我的肩。可他的领地同我的连在一起，拼成一千二百万生灵的大国；黄金珠宝之富，高高的矿壁，乳脂丰美的河谷。这一切我都中意，我也想戴王冠、做在位的女王。可我不中意这根细弱胆小的木头男孩，他的珍珠钻石仿佛把他往下坠，叫他华美的头巾底下那双黑眼睛亮着惊惶。
“那场盛大的订婚礼我享受得很，还淘气地捉弄那个孩子气十足的男孩。一个高大的红脸英国人陪着他跑前跑后，我觉得神气极了，骑在我丝绸装点的象背上，受万民喝彩。‘佛郎机’——我们这样叫英国人——来了许许多多，总在背后嘀咕，客气地点头，一本正经地陪我玩，顺着我的性子。我承认我觉得他们了不起。我不自觉地把他们摆在我的族人之上。
“我记得听见我的监护人同谋臣们谈话，只懂得一半。英国人竟容许这桩婚事，他们显然大感意外。看情况，英国人早已抵制辛德拉巴德人民的意愿。他们在他那块土地上，权势比在我们的大。我们的土地是独立的——或者至少我们自以为如此。的确，我们不派使臣去外国——可外国我们懂什么、又关心什么？的确，我们的贸易被英国人垄断，可那是好买卖、赚钱的买卖。内政上我们自由、不受滋扰，只是宫里总坐着一位不声不响的英国人。他是驻扎官。他‘建言’，也侦查我们——这是如今我才知道的。
“辛德拉巴德就不一样了，我的小王子在英国参议下治国。辛德拉巴德在英国人的铁腕里。他们久已皱眉于布沃德普尔——一个土邦、半独立的印度国——的权势。

===Original===

They refused to countenance a marriage alliance with Sindrabad and continued to refuse; then suddenly something happened. A new English Resident appeared, a commissioner magnificent with medals, well trained, allied to a powerful English family of the nobility, and backed by new regiments of well-armed men. He had lived long in the country of my phantom prince; now he came to us smiling and bringing the little Maharajah by the hand and giving consent and benediction to our marriage.
“I heard my father ask, aside, hesitating and frowning:
“ ‘What is back of all this?’
“But I only half listened to this talk and intrigue. I wanted the Durbar and the glory of the pageant of this marriage. So in pomp and magnificence beyond anything of which even I, a princess, had dreamed, we were married in the high hills facing the wide glory of the Himalayas; the drums boomed and the soldiers marched; the elephants paraded and the rajahs bowed before me and I was crowned and married, her Royal Highness, the Maharanee of Bwodpur and Sindrabad. There was, I believe, some dispute about this ‘royal,’ but father was obdurate.”
“You mean that you were really a wife, while yet a child?” asked Matthew.
“Oh, no, I was in reality only a betrothed bride and must return to my home for Gauna, that is, to wait for years until I was grown and my bridegroom should come and take me to his home. But he never came. For somehow, I do not remember why, there came a time of darkness and sorrow, when I could not go abroad, when I was hurried with my nurse from palace to palace and got but fleeting glances of my phantom prince even on his rare calls of ceremony. Once I came upon him in a long, cold and marble corridor as, running, I escaped from Nurse. He was standing, thin, pale, and in tears. His brown skin was gray and drawn; he looked upon me with great and frightened eyes and whispered: ‘Flee, flee! The English will kill you too.’ That was all.
“I do not know how it happened. I know that the English commissioner was transfixed with horror. This bronze boy, just as he had started home, was found in the forest, his face all blood, dead. My father was wan with anger, and, it seemed, all against the English. He did not accuse them directly of this awful deed, but he knew that the death of both these married children, the last of their line, would throw both countries into the control of England.

===Chinese===

他们不肯认可同辛德拉巴德的联姻，一拖再拖；随后忽然，风向变了。来了新的英国驻扎官，一位勋章煌煌的专员，训练有素，攀着一门显赫的英国贵族，背后有新编的、武装精良的团。他在我幻影王子的国度住过多年；如今他笑吟吟地来，牵着小土王的手，亲口允准我们的婚事并赐福。
“我听见父亲在旁边问，迟疑着，皱着眉：
“‘这背后是什么名堂？’
“可这些话这些计谋我只半听半不听。我要的是杜尔巴，要这场婚姻大典的煊赫。于是，排场同壮丽超过了我这个公主梦想过的一切，我们在高山上、面对喜马拉雅辽阔的荣光成了婚；鼓声隆隆，士兵开步；大象游行，诸王侯向我躬身，我受冠冕、成婚礼——殿下，布沃德普尔与辛德拉巴德的女土王。我记得，为了这个‘王室’字样还有过一番争执，可父亲寸步不让。”
“你是说，你还是个孩子时，就真的做了妻子？”马修问。
“哦不，实际上我只是订了亲的新娘，得回娘家守‘加乌纳’——就是说要等上好些年，等我长大，新郎来迎我过门。可他永远没有来。不知怎的——我不记得缘由了——来了一段黑暗同悲伤的时日，我不能出门，奶妈带着我从一座宫殿仓皇躲到另一座，连他礼节性的稀罕来访，我也只偷得几眼。有一回，我从奶妈身边逃开跑着，在一条长而冷的大理石走廊撞见他。他站着，瘦削、苍白、流着泪。他的棕色皮肤灰败而紧绷；他用大大的、惊恐的眼睛望着我，低声说：‘逃吧，逃吧！英国人连你也要杀。’就这一句。
“我不知道事情怎么发生的。我知道那位英国专员吓得魂飞魄散。这青铜色的男孩，正要动身回家，被人发现死在林中，满脸血污。我父亲气得面无血色，而且，看样子，全是冲着英国人的。他没有直接控告他们这桩可怕的罪行，可他明白：这两个成婚的孩子——两族最后的血脉——若都死了，两国便都落进英格兰的掌心。

===Original===

There were wild rumors in the air of the court. In strict compliance with ancient custom, I as a widow should have died with my little bridegroom, but even the priests saw too much power for England in this, and suddenly my father summoned my English companion and sent me with her to England, while he reigned in my name in Sindrabad and in his own right in Bwodpur.
“My governess was a quiet, clear-eyed woman, with a heart full of courage and loyalty. Sometimes I thought that she and my father had loved each other and that because of the hopelessness of this affection she was suddenly sent home and I with her.
“Then came beautiful days. I loved England. I loved the work of my tutors and the intercourse with the new world that spread before me. I stayed two full years, until I was fourteen, and then again came clouds. There was a tall English boy of whom I saw much. We had ridden, run, and played together. He told me he loved me. I was glad. I did not love him, but I wanted him to love me because the other girls had sweethearts. But he was curiously fierce and gruff about it all. He wanted to seize and embrace me and I hated the touch of his hands, for after all he was not of royal blood, which then meant so much to me.
“One day he suddenly asked me to run away and marry him. I laughed.
“ ‘Yes, I mean to marry you,’ he said. ‘I am going to have you. I don’t care if you are colored.’ I gasped in amazement. He didn’t *care*. He, a lowborn shopman’s brat, and I, a princess born. I, ‘*colored*’! I wanted to strike him with my croquet mallet. I rushed away home.
“It seemed that the scales had fallen from my eyes. I understood a hundred incidents, a dozen veiled allusions and little singular happenings. I suddenly realized that these dull, loud, ugly people actually thought me inferior because my skin was browner than their bleached and roughened hides. They were condescending to me﻿—me, whose fathers were kings a thousand years before theirs were ragpickers.
“I rushed in upon my governess. I opened my lips to rage. She stopped me gently: my father was dead in Bwodpur. I was summoned to India to marry and reign. But I did not go. The news of my father’s death came on August first, 1914. When I reached London and the India Office, August fourth the world was at war.

===Chinese===

宫闱里流言四起。按古礼严规，我身为寡妇，本该随我的小新郎死去；可连祭司们也看出这样一来英格兰权柄更重，于是父亲忽然召来我的英国女伴，把我托付给她送去英国；他则以我的名义统治辛德拉巴德，凭自己的名分统治布沃德普尔。
“我的女教师是位沉静、目光清澈的女人，一腔勇气与忠心。有时我想，她同我父亲原是相爱的，正因为这份情无望，她才忽然被遣送回国，我也随她同去。
“随后是好日子。我爱英格兰。我爱老师们授我的功课，爱那个在我面前展开的新世界的往来。我住了整两年，到十四岁，乌云又来了。有个高个子的英国男孩，我们常见面。我们一起骑马、奔跑、玩耍。他对我说他爱我。我高兴。我并不爱他，可我要他爱我，因为别的姑娘都有情郎。可他在这事上出奇地粗蛮。他要抓住我、抱我，我恨他手的触碰，毕竟他没有王族的血，那时这于我重于一切。
“一天他忽然要我同他私奔成婚。我笑了。
“‘是的，我打定主意娶你，’他说，‘我要得到你。你是黑人我也不在乎。’我惊得倒抽一口气。他*不在乎*。他，一个下等店主的崽子；我，一位天生的公主。我，‘黑人’！我真想拿槌球槌砸他。我一路跑回家。
“仿佛鳞屑从我眼里落尽了。我一下子懂了一百件事、十几句含沙射影的话同种种蹊跷。我猛然明白：这些乏味、吵闹、丑陋的人，当真觉得我低他们一等，只因我的皮肤比他们漂白糙化的皮色深。他们在对我屈尊——对我，我的先人做王的时候，他们的先人还在拾破烂。
“我冲到女教师跟前。我张开嘴要发作。她轻轻拦住了我：我父亲在布沃德普尔去世了。召我回印度成婚、即位。可我没有去。父亲死讯传来那天是1914年8月1日。等我赶到伦敦的印度事务部，8月4日，世界大战打响了。

===Original===

“There ensued a series of quick moves followed by protracted negotiations; the English explained that it would never do to start their royal charge for India in time of war. Bwodpur retorted that it would never do to have their Maharanee far away in England in time of war. The India Office delicately suggested that the presence in England of an Indian princess of high birth and influence would do much to cement the empire and win the war for civilization, and secretly they whispered that it would be unwise to send to India, when English power was weak, a person who might become a rallying center for independence.
